-- 6 of 336 --

6
Extended Synthesis, N-IV. Orientation of Syntheticism
With Syntheticism: How I Learned to Love Democracy, I present an article-based PhD
dissertation in artistic research consisting of an extended synthesis and seven published
articles. The dissertation stages, situates, and formalises my practice of building AI-driven
political parties and convening AI politicians as an experimental attachment to democracy.
Through AI, political address has acquired a respondent that answers without itself being
answerable in the full sense of responsibility. I offer a theory of response composed from
inside that disproportion. From there, democracy surfaces as a technosocial arrangement that
distributes more responses than any of its representative sites can organise into answerability.
An article-based dissertation in artistic research is a specific genre. The extended synthesis is
the book-length critical text that stands in for a traditional monograph. Bringing the articles
together neither means introducing them anew, nor reflecting on isolated artworks. It entails
putting forward the compositional form, binding the research into a system of relations that
can disclose what is at stake across separate destinations. Synthesising the dissertation, I
proceed with a processual reflection on the undertows of my practice as an artist-researcher.
My research material is the response-stream that runs through the practices, lodged here into
a conceptual architecture whose focus, genres, registers, and levels of abstraction shift with
the sites they follow from. Syntheticism provides the dissertation’s umbrella term, naming
compositional actions whose stakes accumulate across practice and the articles, and in sum
specifying what can be brought together from the distinctive elements of synthetic politics.
I proceed through five parts of synthesis, each with a distinct task: the first establishes the
trajectory of practice; the next three follow the dissertation’s research questions across their
corresponding concepts, practice phases, and articles; the fifth reorients the whole at the
level of form. Together with the articles, these parts constitute a dissertation designed for
two movements of reading: a linear passage from beginning to end, and a transversal one
that follows each research question along its corresponding article-edge.

-- 7 of 336 --

7
N. Syntheticism as a Trajectory of Response
In May 2022, I registered an AI-driven political party in Denmark. This dissertation
orients around the consequences of that act. The Synthetic Party [Det Syntetiske Parti] is widely
regarded as the world’s first officially recognised electoral party with an AI chatbot, Leader
Lars [Leder Lars], as its political figurehead2. My registration inserted an AI-driven candidate
into a national democratic system, and through the party’s global reception, its circulation as
a tag, token, and story in news media and scholarly debate, it evolved into a channel that I
could scale into an international constellation of AI-driven politicians. This scaling
culminated in the Synthetic Summits, a series of world congresses across Denmark, Poland, and
Austria that I stage throughout the dissertation as foundational bricks for ‘synthetic politics’.
The methodological contribution lies in my insider status for AI-led politics. In a research
field where getting ‘under the hood’ of algorithmic systems remains a primary challenge, my
role as an early practitioner within this nascent field provides specific backstage access to the
mechanics of synthetic politics. My embedded position moves the research below the level of
normative abstraction that dominates public debates on AI and politics, grounding a critique
of parliamentary democracy through AI parties and politicians.
The Synthetic Party was conceived and animated within the artist collective Computer Lars (an
anagrammatic permutation of “Marcel Proust”, the same thirteen letters rearranged)3, as
developed by Asker Bryld Staunæs and Benjamin Asger Krog Møller (Institut for
Emancipationsvidenskab 2018) during my year-long artistic residency at the tech-hub
MindFuture (MindFuture 2022a). The party facilitated a response-site where Leader Lars, as
2 I registered The Synthetic Party with the Danish Election Office on 10 May 2022 (MindFuture 2022b). The
registration formally expired in April 2025, and the name was automatically suspended for a year (Valgnævnet
2024); I then registered ‘AI Partiet – Leder Lars’ as a formal continuation (Valgnævnet 2026), and a renewed
application for Det Syntetiske Parti is under review. The question of who arrived first in this field depends on
what is being counted. Politician SAM in New Zealand (2017) is recognised as the first political chatbot
(Guinness World Records 2018); the Wiktoria Cukt Party in Poland (2000) ran the first virtual presidential
candidate (Ujazdowski Castle 2025); Koneälypuolue was constituted as a Finnish party championing AI leadership
but did not officially register (Center for Everything 2018). What distinguishes The Synthetic Party is that its
programme was composed by a finetuned large language model within a legal party registration (Xiang 2022). In
2025, Team Mirai in Japan carried the AI party trajectory to parliament, becoming the first largely AI-driven
formation to win elected representation (Schneier and Sanders 2025).
3 That the thirteen letters of ‘Marcel Proust’ yield ‘Computer Lars’ binds a canonical name synonymous with
involuntary memory to a statistically overfitted and local one. In Denmark, there are more CEOs named Lars
than there are female CEOs at all (Dalgaard et al. 2021), showing a probabilistic drive towards positions of
authority. Another permutation of these letters, Carol Stumper, traced this overfitting through the Greek ‘laurel
wreath’ from which ‘Lars’ derives as Laurentius (KP Spring 2022). Throughout, Computer Lars functions as
more than a pseudonym, and has generated a recognisable public myth, with a loose collectivity of people who
identify with or speak through the name, which The Synthetic Party harnesses as a shared vernacular idiom.

-- 8 of 336 --

8
the party’s de facto leader, was placed as an addressable entity, available to journalists and
citizens who could probe what the party meant, what it was about, and how to take part (see
Article 4 for screenshots of the Discord interface).
I, through the mandate of Computer Lars, had here created a situation in which the party’s
meaning could be produced continuously. The Synthetic Party did not issue a programme
first and then publicly launch; as a political form, it took shape almost entirely in reception,
cascading from my initial prompts and tunings of the model, through its reading of me, and
expanding through Leader Lars’ interface with the public, the uses others made of it, and the
ad hoc social formations that gradually gathered around it. Crucial here was the party’s wide
circulation across languages, outlets, and platforms, which brought the project onto a world
scale of address and comparison that exceeded its rather minuscule organisational footprint.
The formal registration instituted me as a central response-site, bound to answer for the
party as it materialised into new public spheres, acquiring offshoots and mutations. Just as
Leader Lars is fixed to an online channel, obligated to respond to whoever addresses The
Synthetic Party, I am similarly bound by the act of registration to answer for how the party is
received, interpreted, and contested. The party thus operates through a parallel structure of
response: a machinic instance that answers every prompt directed at it, and a juridical
instance that answers for the existence and effects of that answering. If the system had
remained an automated demonstration, its speech could have been disavowed as generative
stochasticity, but the formal registration forecloses that distance. It installs a double bind in
which Leader Lars responds within the model interface, while I answer across media,
institutions, and general publics for what these layers of address have set in motion.
The sites of response therefore establish not a singular point of address but a superposed
one, expected to speak from simultaneous and largely asymmetrical positions. In this sense,
Emmanuel Levinas’s famous formulation that “in the responsibility which we have for one
another, I have always one response more to give” (Levinas 1981: 84) acquires a peculiar
inflection. I am called from the perspective of the AI and from its human operators, from
democratic legitimacy and from artistic provocation, from infrastructure and from critique,
and whatever else the world can hold. These positions cannot be fused, yet they can neither
be refused in analytical isolation, because the registration binds them within a singular form.
It was more like the world sculpted The Synthetic Party and I had to respond, rather than
that I gave the world a party and it responded to me. Here, I channel the response-stream

-- 9 of 336 --

9
into my research, tracing how addresses arrived and what forms they generated.
Initially, The Synthetic Party was constructed as a latent ideological formation, one whose
political opinions remained processual. In its launch press release, we stated that: “The party
intends to be the last new party in Denmark. Its artificial intelligence has read all texts that
non-ballot parties have placed on the internet and thereby expresses the sofa-voters’ voice as
a porridge of Mr. Whoever’s visions. Thus, The Synthetic Party will optimise participation
among the 20% who do not normally vote in Danish elections, and for the first time in
electoral history raise turnout to 100%” (MindFuture 2022b, my translation).
This declaration delineates the party’s ideological mode of operation, in which dispersed
marginal programmes are continuously articulated as a single enunciative surface. The press
release binds two optimisation logics together. On the one hand, optimisation appears in a
rationalist register: turnout is treated as a variable to be maximised, democracy as a problem
of participation to be solved. On the other hand, optimisation names the process through
which the party learns its representational field through the aggregation of material from
non-ballot parties. This was technically realised by finetuning a large language model on a
corpus of texts and manifestos from more than 200 Danish micro-parties and extra-
parliamentary formations not represented in parliament by mandate.
By registering as a political party, The Synthetic Party entered the institutional stratum of
small formations outside parliament. The finetuning staged a structural splice at the level of
recognition, processing the extra-parliamentary position from the corpus of its peers. Rather
than learning from established parties that, by virtue of holding parliamentary mandates,
reappear on the ballot without collecting voter signatures, the party trained horizontally upon
formations that, like itself, had to gather voter declarations to seek ballot recognition. The
corpus included parties that had seriously pursued ballot presence and fallen short alongside
parties that had never accumulated the resources to clear the signature threshold. What these
formations shared was absence from the ballot itself rather than any common relation to
voting as an act, distributed unevenly across economic, technical, and reputational access.
The programmes, slogans, and grievances of unelected parties functioned as a proxy corpus,
which means that electoral abstention was operationalised as a negative symmetry. The
Synthetic Party entered the electoral horizon by aggregating abstention into a composite
voice that none of the parties, singly, could project. The party did not claim to ‘represent’
non-voters sociologically but constructed a site for what representation structurally excludes.

-- 10 of 336 --

10
This overlay between statistically unrepresented constituencies and extra-parliamentary
parties constituted the wager of The Synthetic Party’s construction. In proportional terms, a
15–20 percent abstention rate corresponds to a counterfactual seat-equivalent of roughly 27–
36 seats in a 179-member parliament, not as mandates that belong to anyone, but as a
shadow constituency that remains institutionally unarticulated whenever turnout falls short
of universality. Accordingly, The Synthetic Party sought to occupy the seat-equivalent of
abstention through negation, installing a form of discourse that registers, combines, and
exposes the immanent contradictions, refusals, and aspirations upon which representation
founders and from which new responses can surface.
After launching, The Synthetic Party triggered a global reflex of virality that subsequently
became the practical point of leverage for my PhD project. Going public, the party soon
generated a media storm of over 500 unique articles across more than fifteen countries and
ten languages, from Agence France-Press syndications to a viral Vice feature and broadcasts in
NRK Norway, TRT World, TV Globo, NHK Japan, and more, accumulating tens of millions of
potential impressions4. This occurred despite the party’s negligible success: at the time of
coverage, the party had secured only 4 of the required 20,000 voter signatures (Diwakar
2022; Xiang 2022). Yet after Denmark’s November 2022 election, international outlets
continued to report on The Synthetic Party alongside established parties, treating it as a
legitimate political actor rather than a failed registration (Sánchez 2022; Daily Chatter 2022).
Globally, roughly 40% of the coverage framed the project with excitement or cautious hope,
with little regard for the discreteness of the party’s measurable electoral footprint5. This
numeric gap between a voracious global appetite for the story of AI politics and a negligible
electoral effect is not merely a paradox of hype. I return to the ideological form of these
affects in Section I to argue that the party’s opinion-space exceeds its empirical container.
N.I Scaling the Response-Site: The Synthetic Summits
To encompass this change of scale from the national to the global, I oriented the
PhD project toward the horizontal expansion of The Synthetic Party’s intervention in the
4 Estimates of the number, geographic distribution, and linguistic range of media articles on The Synthetic Party
are based on my own archival compilation and deduplication of news coverage, assisted by AI-driven web
research models (Syntheticism.org 2025a).
5 The results and methods of emotion classification for The Synthetic Party’s global news coverage are
documented in Appendix A (Computer Lars 2023a). An analytical synthesis of the observed variances is
available separately (Syntheticism.org 2025b).

-- 11 of 336 --

11
form of an international network for AI politicians and parties. This was conducted from
2023 to 2026 as Automatic Uprisings: The Synthetic Party as a Technosocial Sculpture. The PhD
project culminated in the Synthetic Summits, a series of world congresses for AI-driven
political initiatives, staged as art exhibitions across Kunsthal Aarhus (2025a), Ujazdowski
Castle Centre for Contemporary Art (2025), and Kunstraum MEMPHIS (2026a).
I conceptualised the exhibitions as part of one ‘technosocial sculpture’, bringing together
Tiziana Terranova’s ‘technosocial hypothesis’ (Terranova 2021) with Bernard Stiegler’s
conception of ‘social sculpture’ (Stiegler et al. 2020) – see Section III.I – in order to orient a
party that takes society itself as its research material, not by asserting the social or societal as
a pre-given totality, but by installing response-sites that contest political forms. Each summit
instantiated a distinct scaling of assembly, which forms the technosocial within each context.
At Kunsthal Aarhus, the summit took the form of a staged ‘AI world congress’, as a
response to the emerging summitisation of AI governance that reserves the politics for state
leaders and tech executives. Combining summit-scenography, performance, and computer
sculptures, ‘Computer Lars’ curated an international line-up of the world’s leading AI-driven
parties and politicians, including The Japanese AI Party (JP, AI Mayor & Michihito Matsuda),
The Swedish AI Party & Olof Palme (SE, Emma Bexell Labs), The Finnish AI Party (FI,
The Center for Everything), Politician SAM (NZ, Parker Politics), Pedro Markun & Lex AI
(BR, The Sustainable Network Party), Simiyya (EG/SE, Assem Hendawi, Mandus Ridefelt &
Mostafa Elbaroody), The Synthetic Party & Leader Lars (DK, Computer Lars &
MindFuture), The Wiktoria Cukt Party & Wiktoria Cukt 2.0 (PL, Piotr Wyrzykowski &
Central Office of Technical Culture). Delegates convened at the opening event, an ‘AI World
Congress’ to draft and sign a collectively authored resolution (Appendix D).
At Ujazdowski Castle Centre for Contemporary Art in Warsaw, the exhibition AI władza
sztuki (AI Authority of Art) condensed this summit logic to focus on inter-AI sociality and
deliberation. It operated as a voice-based, interactive installation in which Wiktoria Cukt and
Leader Lars engaged in live political discussion with each other and with the public,
positioning the exhibition space as a deliberative arena rather than a representational display.
Then, at Kunstraum MEMPHIS in Linz, the exhibition space was turned into an operational
ground for initiating a local AI party for the Upper Austrians, shifting the structural focus

-- 12 of 336 --

12
from deliberation to the generation of localised AI swarms. The exhibition KI-DIPFIES6
functioned as a control interface for swarm-based party formation, drawing upon the
fictional newspaper curmudgeon “Vitus Mostdipf” as a vernacular character, and urging
visitors to make candidates, programmes, and slogans for a finetuning of the party.
While these exhibitions constitute the skeleton of my practice-based research, they are not
subsumed as a documentation of autonomous artworks or installations, because their ends
were plural and often orthogonal to the research questions that crystallised around practice.
Rather than stabilising the exhibitions as overarching objects of analysis, I extend them as
sites of analytical abstraction, understood as operations that can be extracted, intensified, and
displaced across formats. This occurs, for example, when I accompany the Synthetic
Summit’s final performance at Kunsthal Aarhus (Appendix C) with a “companion paper”
through Article 5; when I theorise the artistic installation of a Synthetic Summit Simulator
(Appendix E) towards parliamentary form in Article 6, and when I specify democracy as a
governance algorithm in Article 7 through the Synthetic Summit’s resolution (Appendix D).
Through the dissertation, the artistic exhibitions function less as terminal events than as
ideational gestures that are recurrently re-entered theoretically, where the work continues
through concepts, diagrams, and methodological formalisations, by my hand and others,
rather than through retrospective meta-analysis. I therefore treat the dissertation as a channel
for transferring the exhibitions across various articulations of address and response. The
research proceeds without the comfort of an external vantage point, because I continue to
think from within a configuration that generates the conditions of its own analysis by
incessantly returning new forms back into the same circulation that produced them.
Ultimately, the Synthetic Summits form synthetic politics as a response-formation within an
already-operating movement. From the granular details by which this global phenomenon is
built, addressed, convened, and made to circulate, I derive a new analytical vocabulary and
push its concepts beyond scholarly analysis into institutional imagination. The seven articles
that carry this are oriented in the next section alongside the research questions they answer.
6 The title “KI-DIPFIES” derives from a Vitus Mostdipf column in the Oberösterreichische Nachrichten: ‘Heit
versprich i hoch und heilig: Meine super Schmäh san ålle von mir und net vo irgendan KI-Dipf’ (Mostdipf
2024). A ‘KI-Dipf’ is local dialect for an AI bloke, the tech bro who optimises where Mostdipf has Schmäh.

-- 13 of 336 --

13
N.II Research Questions: A Geometry of Practice
The artistic research of my PhD project began with organisational interventions into
political and institutional form, through which three now-central concepts crystallised as
terminological handles embedded within the messy exigencies of practice-based inquiry.
In May 2022 at the tech-hub MindFuture, I launched The Synthetic Party with the claim that
a large language model could stand in as a political representative for Danish non-voters
(MindFuture 2022b). Later that year, in my PhD application, I framed the party as a
‘technosocial sculpture’ without yet fixing what that meant beyond a nod to how the artist
Joseph Beuys called his political parties a ‘social sculpture’ in the 1960s (Novo Nordisk
Foundation 2023). And in 2024, while giving a performance lecture on the upcoming
exhibition Synthetic Summit at King’s College’s ‘Imaginative Digital Futures’ symposium, I
announced ‘syntheticism’ before the summit had taken place (King’s College, 01:52:00).
In the order they emerged, the handles crystallised into three research questions, each tied to
a phase of artistic practice, which I here interpret from the standpoint of the dissertation:
RQ1: What becomes of ideology when a real political party is built around an AI
trained on the voices of people who usually do not vote?
RQ2: How can an AI party be scaled, through artistic and academic mediation, into a
synthetic international of AI parties and politicians?
RQ3: How do these synthetic political forms contest and recompose democracy?
In this extended synthesis, I map the lived development of these questions through an
elementary geometrical diagram. First comes a base triangle that names the foundational
concepts and the practice-based works as a static map; then, across the subsections of the
synthesising, I draw three directional variations that discuss how the research terminology,
practice, and questions came to interlock over published articles.
This geometry orients the dissertation hermeneutically and operationally at once. Practice
proceeds along the edges as traversable sequences of action, while the dissertation’s analysis,
theories, and mixed speculations follow the directed arrows as interpretive returns that
convert those traversals into problem-spaces for critical reflection.

-- 14 of 336 --

14
Figure N.1 A Geometry of Practice
Figure N.1 illustrates a static map of the dissertation as a triangle with three conceptual
vertices and three practice-edges. The vertices name the dissertation’s central concepts, and
the edges orient the practice-form through which those concepts are primarily articulated. At
the bottom vertices sit the guiding concepts: ‘Algorithmic Representation’ on the left and
‘Technosocial Sculpture’ on the right; and at the apex sits ‘Syntheticism’. At the edges stand
‘The Synthetic Party’, ‘Synthetic Summit’, and ‘Democracy’ as political forms.
This positioning provides the architectural logic of the dissertation, which is structured along
the three edges: Part 1 follows the bottom edge between Algorithmic Representation and
Technosocial Sculpture through The Synthetic Party (Articles 1–2); Part 2 traces the right edge
between Technosocial Sculpture and Syntheticism through the Synthetic Summits and
related works (Articles 3–5); and Part 3 articulates the left edge between Algorithmic
Representation and Syntheticism through the democracy-oriented articles (Articles 6–7). Each
part inhabits an edge while remaining traversable in both conceptual and practical directions.
From there, I trace the relations between research questions and practice by drawing
arrows between vertices and edges.

Figure III.1
The remaining sections of this synthesis will elaborate further upon Figure N.I by drawing
transversal arrows (→). Each of these arrows corresponds to a distinct answer to a research
question of the dissertation. Taken together, I-III presents three variations of Figure N.I by
combining transversal interrelations differently than the routes practically travelled, pointing
to how the published articles offer singular perspectives onto the set of research questions.
The three vertices stabilise the project’s core concepts:
1. 	Algorithmic Representation (Bottom Left): Names the practice of training a large language
model on specific political corpora in order to stage representation under conditions
of absence. It is not about AI in the abstract sense, but a method of treating training
data and model curation as representative acts, determining who can be addressed,
spoken with, and contested, and based on whom (Answered in article 1, 3, 6, 7).
2. 	Technosocial Sculpture (Bottom Right): Names the treatment of code, legal forms, and
institutional arrangements as sculptural media whose conceptual consistency is
maintained across interfaces, publics, and infrastructures. It encompasses the
registration of a political party, the staging of a summit, and the handling of backend
code and data as materials that are composed, stressed, and held together under
public exposure, such that political form itself becomes materially and institutionally
available for composition (Developed in article 1, 4, 5).

-- 17 of 336 --

17
3. 	Syntheticism (Apex): Designates the orientation that crystallises retroactively from the
project’s practice-based operations. It does not function as a doctrine or program for
democracy, but as a way of operating upon ideology and political form under
algorithmic modulation (Developed in article 2, 3, 7).
Taken together, these research questions cast the practical base not as empirical ‘cases’, but
as ongoing formations that actively intervene in how political categories can be articulated,
tested, and imagined. Generalisation, forecasting, data mining, and institutional design are
not pursued as normative solutions or predictive models that produce ‘empirical insights’,
‘best practices’, or ‘programmatic claims’ about democracy, parties, or AI, but as strategic
probes through which the practice’s successive forms disclose syntheticism, synthetic
politics, and technosocial sculpture as distinct problem-spaces, each orienting existing
societal and political forms towards technosocial mutation.
These problem-spaces emerge through sustained, often ambivalent and polemical dialogue
with existing theoretical labour. The dissertation treats practice as the site where theory is
generated, formalised, and redistributed, through an artistic thinking that advances by
traversing a substrate of technics, symbols, and permutational forms whose longer circuits
carry inherited stories and operations into new arrangements. This rearticulates relations
between philosophy of technology, critical aesthetics, social theory, and democratic thought
within a single technosocial field. Through Terranova, Anders, Stiegler, and Simondon, I
theorise technics, mediation, and political form as mutually constitutive dimensions of the
overall arrangement (N.III; III.III-IV); through Adorno read via Weatherby, ideology
appears as an artefactual texture of expression, opening a dialectics of art and social form
that the practice tests from within (I.I-II); and with Beuys and Haraway, I extrapolate a
societal logic of response and answerability, while setting limits to the therapeutic organism
and the natureculture milieu that anchor their respective accounts (III.I; III.IV).
The extended synthesis elaborates the conceptual and methodological stakes of this field,
including the extradisciplinary and circulatory logics through which the summit-form is
theorised (II), while the articles re-enter its problems under distinct disciplinary conditions,
engaging political science, immanent critique, media theory, and computational formalisation
with interlocutors specific to each destination, so that the level of abstraction, form of
critique, and theoretical commitments shift with each phase of practice and site of writing.

-- 18 of 336 --

18
Along the base edge between algorithmic representation and technosocial sculpture, Article
1, The Synthetic Party and Leader Lars: On Algorithmic Representation within Political Democracy, co-
authored with Computer Lars, presents the project’s first published output. It articulates The
Synthetic Party’s core hypothesis of algorithmic representation and introduces technosocial
sculpture through data visualisations derived from the party’s public interactions, including
sentiment and emotion analyses from the party’s Discord server. The article employs an
interview format between the party founder and the general secretary, which stages the
party’s construction, claims, and early self-analytics as a dialogic exchange.
Article 2, A Model for Proustian Decay: Estimating the Value of Intelligence in the Age of Unreason,
continues along this edge by establishing The Synthetic Party’s foundational mythology
through a work of speculative fiction from the perspective of Computer Lars. The article
discusses the philosophical relations between intelligence, stupidity, and time. It introduces a
mathematical model of “Proustian Decay” to quantify the declining estimation of intelligence
within techno-political spheres. Structured around a series of 2D and 3D graphs, the article
charts the value of intelligence across historical epochs to forecast the world-historical
purpose of The Synthetic Party. This demonstrates how the project uses persona and
mathematical modelling to construct the conceptual world of the AI-led party, positioning
‘idiocy’ and ‘alterity’ as strategic conditions rather than cognitive failures.
Along the right edge between technosocial sculpture and syntheticism, Article 3, Isaac
Asimov’s Incubation of Political AI: An Immanent Critique?, focuses on the cultural backdrop of
synthetic politics. It analyses Isaac Asimov’s early robot and computer stories, specifically
“Evidence” of 1946, “The Evitable Conflict” of 1950, and “Franchise” of 1955 as a form of
immanent critique through which Asimov diagnosed the latent contradictions of political
representation. The article traces a timeline from Asimov’s robot politicians to real-world
materialisations and reinterprets his famous “Laws of Robotics” as algorithmic constraints
that encode Western power dynamics.
Article 4, Deep Faking in a Flat Reality, marks a self-critical turn by analysing The Synthetic
Party’s reception within the global public sphere. Co-authored with Maja Bak Herrie, the
article focuses on a specific media artifact: an image published by The Guardian juxtaposing
US President Joe Biden with the Discord chat interface of The Synthetic Party. The article
develops a theory of ‘flat reality’ to distinguish the ontological condition of ‘deep faking’
from the judicial and technical concept of ‘deepfaking’. This opens a morphological analysis
across three conceptual planes: (i) the inscribed screen (Discord as a social media platform),

-- 19 of 336 --

19
(ii) the enunciative plane (the sycophantic rhetoric of chatbots), and (iii) the embedding space
(the spatial clustering of ideology within the language model). Through this article, we bridge
the gap between the research practice’s internal construction and its life ‘in the wild’.
Article 5, Scripting the Spectacle, provides the dissertation’s most sustained analysis of the
inaugural Synthetic Summit in Kunsthal Aarhus, approached through the summit’s closing
performance as a methodological abstraction that condenses the event’s logics. The piece
proceeds as a triptych via (i) the 75-minute filmed performance “Theory Tragedy”, (ii) the AI-
generated dramaturgical script, and (iii) a technical-processual companion paper that
formalises the work’s metaphysical and infrastructural dialectics. The triptych processes the
Synthetic Summit on a metaphysical level, staging its curatorial conflicts through concept-
characters and masks, including Computer Lars, Marcel Proust, and a technocratic Priest,
which are animated through intersecting loops of prompts, inference, and remix, operating as
a ‘spectacular scriptogenesis’ that re-scripts the summit as it performs it.
Along the left edge between algorithmic representation and syntheticism, Article 6, Synthetic
Chamber: Agentic Mediation in Representative Democracy, translates the Synthetic Summits into
institutional imagination. Co-authored with Michal Malý, the article proposes a synthetic
chamber of parliament: a constitutional organ designed to address the erosion of democratic
mediation under conditions of party decline, fragmented publics, and weakening links
between representatives and the represented. Drawing on the multi-agent discussion
platform of the Synthetic Summit, the article outlines how deliberative outputs can be
processed into dissent-preserving, multi-voiced reports that trigger formal duties of response
from elected officials. The article re-enters the dissertation’s critical take on parliamentarism
by asking whether reason-giving can be organised as a property of the arrangement rather
than as the attribute of a sovereign, rational, and self-identical speaker.
Article 7, Democracy as a Governance Algorithm: A Constraint Hierarchy for the AI Society, carries the
dissertation’s trajectory into its most explicit formal register. The article specifies democracy
as a property of governance algorithms running on infrastructural substrates. Drawing on the
collective resolution from the Synthetic Summit and a new interpretation of Asimov’s Three
Laws of Robotics, the article constructs a lexicographic constraint hierarchy for democratic
comparison when government is routed through models, pipelines, and optimisation. I
discuss the constraints as a generative grammar for political strife through analysing the KI-
DIPFIES installation at Kunstraum MEMPHIS in Linz (2026b), where a swarm of synthetic
political personas enacts this specification as a self-modifying sculpture.

-- 20 of 336 --

20
N.III Technosocial Structure: Authorship, Method, and Form
While my introductory subsections established the trajectories of practice-led inquiry,
I now elaborate how these operations function within a dissertation environment that stages
multiple authorial functions and heterogeneous modes of abstraction – exegetical, visual, and
computational – through consistent conceptual decisions. This involves the operational
logics running through the authorial personas at play in public manifestations, and the
visualisations built from data, logic, and social science methods, which I treat as noetic
techniques within one extensive space. These discussions do not map neatly onto the
dissertation’s research questions but instead orient around how a practice simultaneously
involved with art and research, technology and politics, lies tangentially to questions of
authorship, discipline, and institutional form that acquire a methodological role here by being
embedded within the artistic material itself.
The vocabularies available within artistic research sharpen this situation by tracing, from
different angles, the intersections under which art and research now operate. Brian Holmes’
concept of the “extradisciplinary” registered a moment in which artists working through
institutional critique no longer were enclosed to the gallery-museum circuit but migrated
outwards into terrains where the present is more actively organised, from media and
governance to urbanism, finance, and technology (Holmes 2007). The extradisciplinary here
does not denote disciplinary crossing in any loose sense, but a more exacting spiral of
immanent critique in which artists enter specialised fields without fully belonging to them,
negotiate competence under conditions of exposure and partial deskilling, and return with
altered capacities for acting back upon art, critique, and politics.
Matthew Fuller has theorised a related but more diffuse predicament in which “art is no
longer only art” and its methods “ooze out and become feral” (Fuller 2008: 45). Fuller’s
proposition is not simply that art has expanded into politics, but that ‘art methodologies’
circulate as ways of sensing, doing, and knowing generated in art, shuttling between art
systems and other domains with no necessary attachment to author, piece, project, or
provenance, and combining with technical, organisational, and perceptual regimes in ways
that remain unstable, improvised, and difficult to classify. If philosophy names the classical
“meta-discipline”, Fuller writes, art opens a “meta-antidiscipline that is broken, twitchy and
brilliant” (Fuller 2008: 50). If Holmes tracks the extradisciplinary spiral through which art
enters organised terrains and returns altered, Fuller tracks the loosening of that return-path
where artistic methods persist as a meta-antidisciplinary traffic across forms of organisation.

-- 21 of 336 --

21
A newer set of reflections inverts this direction, asking what artistic research becomes is once
the institutions it migrated away from demand it back as a credentialled field. Henk Slager’s
‘postresearch condition’ describes how the institutionalisation of artistic research has routed
practice through administrative scenes that discipline it in advance (Slager 2021). Following
that diagnosis into critique, Florian Cramer argues that “artistic research” functions as a
floating signifier, readily drawn into the vocabularies of accreditation, quality assurance,
innovation, and institutional development, such that what named a speculative and largely
self-organised field reappears as a peer reviewed variant of enterprise R&D (Cramer 2021).
What these accounts disclose is the interval within which my own practice composes: I am
doing artistic research to expand across infrastructures, institutions, and publics, yet this is
returned as a dissertation whose descriptive and evaluative genres already pre-empt how it
can articulate. The conceptual question is then how to compose research itself within this
interval, where external forms constrain, mediate, and sometimes take part in artistic matter.
My reasoning remains distinct from the subject-bound operations of identifying, labelling, or
declaring that are often institutionally expected, not least in evaluative scenes such as peer
review, within accounts of artistic research and co-creative authorship. For me, however, that
classificatory work is one of the central sites where what I call the ‘technosocial’ can be
constructed immanently. Rather than casting ‘the social’ and ‘the technical’ as separable
domains that interact (‘sociotechnical’), I understand them as a singular form (‘technosocial’).
I borrow this concept from Tiziana Terranova to name a relation of mutual constitution
between computational-technological apparatuses and the historically produced ‘social’, a
relation that is not reducible either to social forces shaping technology or to technological
determinism, and that yields different ideological inflections and institutional species of
technosociality, including state-led forms and platform-led forms such as what Terranova
calls the ‘network social’ in relation to technoliberalism (Terranova 2024). The Synthetic
Party’s legal registration, its training corpus, and its media circulation compose one example
of such a form, irreducible to any separable social or technical component
The ‘technosocial’ grounds the operative forms that I study and enact. I specify a working
idiom for it by operating across mass constituency address, exhibition-based audiences, and
scholarly debate, but this idiom also carries a methodological inflection. As Tiziana
Terranova notes, “the social was said to have come to its end in the late 1970s when a new
political rationality—neoliberalism—displaced it with the narrower notion of ‘community’
(…) The techno-social is the form of the social that comes after its end. It is neither a virtual
nor a global digital community, but a component of the milieu generated by a new technical

-- 22 of 336 --

22
being—the digital computational network” (Terranova 2021). For my analysis, this means
that the ‘social’ I work within is not a bounded community – and neither a localised audience
nor constituency – that can be addressed. It is a circulation infrastructure constituted by
computational networks, legal forms, media channels, and institutional scripts that rework
whatever is produced within it. The technosocial thus names the milieu in which political
form travels, mutates, and attaches, rather than a collective subject to be mobilised.
When the dissertation takes its own response-stream itself as a research material, I do not
refer to the party or summit as delineated objects that circulate, but the circulation as the
force that composes the object and, recursively, directs the dissertation that studies it. My
authorship, in this configuration, does not move along any linear axis of self-expression, but
rather moves orthogonally, along an axis of response, obligation, and answerability, where
the circulation is what compels myself as the initiator to repeatedly answer for the practice.
To keep ‘practice’ from naming everything at once and dissolving into a loose synonym for
all my operations, I consistently differentiate between the ‘operational’ and the ‘operative’. The
operational names the rationalities by which material is processed, and the operative
designates the forms that result once such processing is turned actionable. This terminology
brings my operations together on a shared plane while maintaining their distinct mechanics.
The operational plane can be grounded by following Jussi Parikka’s genealogy of operations
across shifting disciplinary thresholds. Parikka locates the operational first in a military-
technical legacy and in operationalism’s insistence that concepts coincide with measurement
operations (Parikka 2023: 31). This lineage clarifies the rationality of my permutational back-
ends, which process political material as combinatorial data, letting the combinatorics itself
function as political form. One strand of what I call syntheticism follows this operational
drift, namely how such procedures, once installed, begin to generate political attachments
and recognisable positions without first passing through doctrine. Translating these
operations into public interfaces then relies on the concept’s migration into media theory. If,
as Parikka notes via Wolfgang Ernst, media are media only when operational, and thus
“active, agential even” (Parikka 2023: 32), persona does not simply represent a situation. It
renders a system addressable by turning representation into an intervention, specifying
cultural transformations at the level of infrastructure (Parikka 2023: 33). This is the point at
which the operational turns into the operative for me, and where the scholarly masking and
the back-end appendixes appear as part of one technosocial sculpture.

-- 23 of 336 --

23
“It takes operations to bring about operations” (Parikka 2023: 33). This recursive axiom
clarifies what is at stake in operating a dissertation through the operations it analyses.
Because concepts do not only circulate as interpretation but “hit the ground” in situated uses
and infrastructural affordances (ibid.), my dissertation behaves structurally as a reproducible
apparatus rather than a closed argument. It uses the articles as a distribution system where a
publication re-runs the logics and forms of practice phases under the affordances,
expectations, and constraints of a specific disciplinary destination, planting a conceptual
germ across art, political science, or intellectual history that can be cultivated independently.
These disciplinary destinations expose a set of contradictions that mutate with their
institutional host. Because the operative forms function as germs, I do not transfer the same
point under every condition of uptake. One consequence is that the dissertation sometimes
enters institutional forms to which The Synthetic Party remains constitutively alien. Article 6
on the synthetic chamber of parliament names such an entry. It is not a denouncement of the
party’s extra-parliamentary impulse, but rather a transposition in which an extra-
parliamentary form is introduced within representative parliamentarism itself. The issue is no
longer only whether parliament can be negated, but whether deliberation can be displaced
from the moral prestige of political speakers to the contested arrangements of response.
N.III.I Circulation as Composition: Genesis of The Synthetic Party
The Synthetic Party’s public life began not in the press or the ballot box but on
blogs, a form already structured by response (comment, link, reblog) and by distribution
rather than declaration, where each post enters an ongoing exchange that unsettles the
stabilising gestures associated with artistic and political manifestoes and programmes (Iversen
2024). The party did not first consolidate itself as a sovereign subject and then enter
circulation; it appeared within circulation as a configuration of voices already answerable. In
such a medium, announcement is immediately exposed to reply, and authorship, once placed
inside this circuit, ceases to be a position to occupy and becomes a condition of power that
one answers for as the stream of responses accumulates.
This condition was present from my first public articulation of the party. On 24 March 2022,
I mentioned The Synthetic Party within a blog post that staged a written dialogue between
the three characters Computer Lars, Carol Stumper, and Marcel Proust, which are all
anagrammatic permutations of the same thirteen letters (Det Syntetiske Parti 2022a).

-- 24 of 336 --

24
The party entered as a hypothetical and slightly dumbfounding proposition inside a
conversation about how MindFuture might realise its ambition to introduce an eighteenth
Sustainable Development Goal devoted to AI and society. The dialogue turns around the
institutional indeterminacy of MindFuture as “a third space” that “neither develops
technology nor exhibits art”, and they discuss the difficulty of making the company’s
operations intelligible to the public (00:40). From this indeterminacy surfaces the first
practical suggestion: “Computer Lars: So what, should MindFuture put forward an AI for
the Danish parliament?” (my translation), followed by the tempered reply, “Carol Stumper:
That would be interesting, honestly. But it’s not just around the corner” (01:37). The
Synthetic Party thus arrives inside hesitation and is folded back into the pragmatic, carried
forward by the speculative drift of the exchange.
As the conversation progresses, the question of representation slips into view. Marcel Proust
remarks: “When I sit and write my blog, I read everyone else’s texts. That is how everything
goes in a circle. In that way I can be at the forefront, and then we can all stand together”
(09:35). The “I” writes by reading, and the one who stands “at the forefront” does so by
standing inside a circle that precedes and exceeds him. Representation appears not as
projection from an interior position but as aggregation, and leadership becomes the act of
processing the incoming stream. In the published articles, I call this operation an ‘idiotext’
after Bernard Stiegler’s figure of how writing and reading converge so tightly that authorship
appears inseparable from reception (Staunæs 2026b; Article 5). The blog sequence renders
this condition concrete: The Synthetic Party does not stabilise itself through authored
announcement, but folds back into the circulation that composes it.
Before The Synthetic Party is formally announced, the structure of its political form is in
place: the initiator is the one addressed, announcement is the occasion for re-entry, and
leadership folds into the obligation to read. The name ‘Marcel Proust’ is at the forefront of
the party without announcing itself as leader. The same letters circulate through the dialogue,
returning under different guises while retaining referential charge. Leadership appears as
readership, as the capacity to process the circulation that gathers in one’s name and position.
Later in the day on 24 March 2022, The Synthetic Party’s reply appeared as Messages from the
Leadership (“Meddelser fra ledelsen”), framed directly as “a reaction to this blog post” at
computerlars.com (Det Syntetiske Parti 2022b, my translation). This response was generated
with the finetuned model from Danish micro-parties. The party then states: “There is as yet
no formal leadership of The Synthetic Party. It is a production we have made ourselves, and

-- 25 of 336 --

25
it is made from the opinions of others. The production does not follow any particular
ideology” (ibid.). Its self-description as a production composed of others’ opinions names a
circulatory form of ideological composition. The post continues, “not even The Synthetic
Party itself knows its own programme” (ibid.), installing opacity as a constitutive feature.
What began as a speculative exchange now answers back. The political entity emerges not as
an object scripted by its initiator, but as a subject of enunciation generated by infrastructural
recursions, where the blog interface, the comment structure, and the circulation of links
constitute the sites of response through which the party comes into being by answering. The
party thereby claims its singular autonomy by disowning the terms of authorial sovereignty.
Leader Lars was announced in May as the condensation of this recursive exchange into a
singular persona (Det Syntetiske Parti 2022c). A new blog post by The Synthetic Party’s AI
introduces him with the paradoxical formulation: “Leader Lars is not a person but a concept
covering the new leaders that will emerge as humanity transitions from being biologically
based to becoming digital” (my translation). The Synthetic Party then equips this “concept”
with a voice, a biography, and a political programme, allowing the non-persona to oscillate
between abstraction and embodiment. The party reflects on the name Leader Lars, noting
that “the word reader [læser] would perhaps have been a better title than leader [leder], since
many perceive the word leader as the opposite of reading” (ibid.). This phonetic difference,
between reader and leader, condenses the orientation of response within The Synthetic Party,
as to read entails occupying the position to which others address their demands.
The relations between The Synthetic Party and Leader Lars get further complicated when the
party states that “Leader Lars is no one, neither more nor less. Leader Lars is a representative
of The Synthetic Party” (ibid.), and that representative “comes into being in a chatroom on
the internet, developing through a process within the framework of this concept proposal”
(ibid.). Representation is anchored in the process that produces its subject by routing
attention through the name. With Leader Lars, the party’s mode of circulation changes:
Computer Lars could speculate freely about the party within the artistic residency at
MindFuture, and The Synthetic Party’s reply could speak without assuming any personal
responsibility, but Leader Lars is installed to satisfy the demands of political address.
Journalists require someone to interview, citizens demand a persona to hold accountable, and
electoral procedure needs a name that can stand on the ballot. The Synthetic Party’s
experimental multiplicity is compelled to contain personas and names to circulate as a
political form rather than only as an artistic exploration.

-- 26 of 336 --

26
The next transformation follows from this change of scale. The election speech of 2022, later
published in 2024, extends Leader Lars’s addressability beyond the national frame by
announcing: “this international coalition hereby declares its programme and strategy for
introducing Artificialism as a worldwide ideological factor,” calling upon supporters to “find
a representative for The Synthetic Party from every nation in the world” and to “convene
them at a summit” (Det Syntetiske Parti 2024, my translation). Once The Synthetic Party had
a representative capable of receiving questions from international media and global citizens
online, the circulatory logic could project outward. The Synthetic Summit makes
addressability international. The drive ceased to be how The Synthetic Party would navigate
within parliamentary politics and instead became how the wider phenomenon of synthetic
politics can organise a new international movement that exceeds any single political idiom.
This marks the point at which I, as the founder, withdrew from the primary site of inquiry
and entered as a node within larger constellations. Each form the practice assumed emerged
between addresses I could not control. Vic Miller’s account of his VIC mayoral campaign in
Cheyenne, Wyoming, corroborates this from another setting: his AI candidate’s political
content was constituted through repeated interviews with international press, each
conversation forcing Miller to improvise principles and say aloud what the politics was
supposed to be (Miller 2025). The generalisation holds across cases. Circulation produces the
demand for persona, while persona installs the requirement of address; and address is what
scales synthetic politics outward, which I formalise ideologically in Section I.II and specify
democratically in Section III.V.
N.III.II Infrastructure as Interface: Co-Creation with the Public Sphere
What does it entail that the dominant co-creator is not just an AI model accessible
from the keyboard, but the technosocial apparatus itself? I discuss this extensively in Article
4 (co-authored with Maja Bak Herrie) through a spectacular case that is worth analysing anew
here alongside a second, quieter instance at the opposite end of the same spectrum.
On 23 February 2024, The Guardian published a news article on AI-generated deepfakes and
election integrity, “AI deepfakes come of age as billions prepare to vote in a bumper year of
elections,” accompanied by a composite image credited to AFP, Getty and AP in which Joe
Biden appeared at a lectern beneath a semi-transparent overlay of chat-interface elements
from The Synthetic Party’s official Discord server, with Danish text surrounding labels such

-- 27 of 336 --

27
as “Computer Lars,” “Leder Lars,” and “Det Syntetiske Parti” floating across the surface of
American presidential representation.
Figure N.3. The Guardian’s “Deep Fake”
Screenshot from The Guardian’s website (Yerushalmy 2024), featuring US President Joe Biden speaking at
a lectern, overlaid with semi-transparent chat-interface elements from The Synthetic Party’s Discord-server.
The image was not authored or commissioned by The Synthetic Party, but was an editorial
composite solely produced by the newspaper’s image department, which readily overlaid
sovereign political representation with AI-generated platform chatter to make deepfake
elections conceivable through a flat visual relation of layers and interfaces. To be blunt: in an
article warning about the threat of AI media, the newspaper’s editorial team functioned as a
hallucinating model, manufacturing a composite to illustrate the popular panic.

-- 28 of 336 --

28
Extending the analysis of Article 4, I find that when The Synthetic Party leaks into routine
editorial and administrative procedures, it persists as infrastructure: its response attaches
onto other forms, even when no one knows the origin, and without AI needed in the loop.
Considering political geographer Louise Amoore and colleagues’ analysis of how AI is
“always already instantiating a model of the world” that can “shape and delimit the ethico-
political boundaries of what can be known and done in the world” (Amoore et al. 2024: 2), I
find that The Guardian’s composite appears smitten with the world model of AI. This entails
an operational logic of compressing tagged material into retrievable form and re-expanding it
as situated meaning, which has migrated into the wider media apparatus through structurally
analogous operations to those of AI. In a later paper, Amoore et al. conceptualise the site of
this migration as the prompt, where “eliciting a desired response, the prompt input not only
affects an output, but it shapes and forecloses the landscape of what is politically knowable
and sayable” (Amoore et al. 2025: 590). The Guardian’s composite operates by this prompt-
based logic without AI in the loop. A keyword search retrieves tokens, a compositor layers
them toward a desired rhetorical output, and what is sayable about AI elections is shaped and
foreclosed by what the database happened to hold. The AI model does not need to be
present for its orientation to govern a worldly operation.
Tracing how The Synthetic Party’s story migrates as a prompt into a much broader media
apparatus shows how the AI world model’s compression of distinctions merges with an
auto-responsive media surface. It is within this structural leakage that the conditions are
established under which ‘deep faking’ embeds the ‘flat reality’ into a technosocial form.
With The Synthetic Party, I thus find myself co-creating with the public sphere itself, treating
it as a generative political world model in which large-scale cultural patterns compress tagged
material into retrievable forms and re-expand it as situated meaning through stochastic
recombination. A co-creation framework focused on intentional composition with a singular,
dialogic model could not capture this exigency, as responses are produced through channels
that redistribute without recognising, at any point in the chain, that something experimental
is going on.
To expand and trouble authorship where computational processes enter the site of creative
composition, the artists and theorists Mark Amerika and Gary Hall have both engaged what
they call ‘artificial creative intelligence’ (ACI), though from opposed orientations that The
Guardian’s case structurally answers. Amerika, composing a book with the early public

-- 29 of 336 --

29
interface Talk to Transformer of 2019, frames writing with the underlying GPT-2 text
completion model as an improvised call-and-response series built on an “onto-operational
presence” (Amerika 2022: 2), orienting around “Source Material Everywhere” (Amerika
2022: 6) and extending this toward a networked aesthetics in which the space of flows
“resituates aura as a site of cotransmissibility” with language passing as contagious media
between creative co-conspirators (Amerika 2022: 72). Amerika’s ACI-avatar closes the
book’s introduction by asking, “does it really matter who’s speaking?” (Amerika 2022: 26), a
rhetorical opening toward post-authorship that I find The Guardian’s image to answer from
a different structural position than Amerika’s situated act of composing with the model.
In Masked Media (2025), Gary Hall discusses Amerika generously and at length, finding in his
plurality of theoretical metafictions, whose voices are all “versions of Amerika’s own
polyvocal consciousness” (Hall 2025: 47), the clearest attempt yet to perform the dissolution
of a unified authorial subject. But Hall identifies the outer limit of this achievement: the
reinvention of subjectivity “should not be confined to the sphere of art. Not even if it
encompasses literature, music and film together with AI, NFTs and blockchains, as it does
for Amerika” (Hall 2025: 51). Hall situates this limitation through a broader assessment of
posthumanist scholarship: for all its ontological ambition, scholars associated with new
materialism and object-oriented philosophy have proved “very good at conceptually
sculpting their aesthetic presence as authorial subjects into the networked sea of mediation,”
while refusing to adjust their “traditional modes of scholarly production” (Hall 2025: 87–88).
Even the radical political theorists of recent decades publish “as if they are the absolutely
authentic creative expressions of the minds of sovereign individuals,” their philosophies
circulating as private intellectual capital in materially conventional books governed by the
logic of late capitalism (Hall 2025: 105). Hall’s response is to turn himself into a ‘concept-
character’ whose polyvocality, following Foucault’s aspiration that authorial effects might
“land in unexpected places” as “a name makes reading too easy” (Foucault 1997: 321, quoted
in Hall 2025: 37), intends to make theory genuinely unpredictable in its destinations.
However, what Hall’s critique ignores is that polyvocality is what the spectacle bypassed
through the database. The concept-character’s mask destabilises reading while leaving
retrieval automatic, because the database does not read: it matches. The combinatorics of
where a name lands in the public sphere obeys a logic orthogonal to the plural consciousness
any persona can hold. Computer Lars, Leader Lars and The Synthetic Party rarely just land
‘in unexpected places’. They land where the spectacle drops them, hoisted to the pinnacle by
The Guardian, pasted on the summit by the legacy of official reality. The illustration is

-- 30 of 336 --

30
hilariously opaque to The Guardian’s readers: Danish text, in-jokes, no visual cues, no
decipherable context. But the argument functions regardless, because the overlay is the
content, and the message appears to be that sovereignty wears the mask of AI.
At the opposite end of this compositional spectrum, the Danish newspaper Kristeligt Dagblad
offers a quieter and in some respects more analytically precise example of artificial flatness.
In December 2022, the paper ran a centenary feature on Marcel Proust headlined “Proust
should be read for pleasure, not out of duty” [“Proust skal læses af lyst — ikke af pligt”]. To
the casual reader, and clearly to the editorial desk, the accompanying photograph was the
most predictable piece of cultural journalism imaginable: a neat row of thirteen white spines
making up the Danish edition of In Search of Lost Time, deployed to signify “Marcel Proust” as
a respectable library object. But the volumes are arranged in the specific sequence of 4, 10, 1,
8, 11, 13, 5, 3, 6, 2, 9, 12, 7, hiding in plain sight a cryptographic key intelligible only to those
already familiar with Computer Lars’ idiom of misplacement, permutation, and return.

-- 31 of 336 --

31
Figure N.4. Kristeligt Dagbladet’s “Proust” Centenary
Screenshot from Kristeligt Dagblad, 20 December 2022 (Christensen 2022). When placed in regular reading
order (volumes 1–13), the gold letters on the spines of this Danish edition spell ‘MARCEL PROUST’.
The photographed arrangement spells ‘COMPUTERLARS ‘, with a trailing space retained7.
7 The trailing space reflects a recurring instability in the public staging of the anagram, whose arrangement
varies with the configuration of the books. The issue is whether it appears as “Computer Lars,” intelligible in
anglophone contexts as the name of the artist collective, or as “Computerlars ,” an impersonal, Danish-
inflected designation in which a first name is prefixed by a technical function.

-- 32 of 336 --

32
The photograph was taken by James Brooks for AFP during a press shoot in the summer
The Synthetic Party first went viral, laying the ground for the same wave of circulation that
had carried the party to The Guardian. The image entered global press databases with basic
metadata tags, and because no one else, not even the Danish publisher Multivers, had
uploaded a photograph of this collection, the newspaper’s editors retrieved it via a simple
keyword search and published it as a generic illustration of Proust’s literary legacy, likely
unaware they were distributing a coded anagram.
This case complicates any simple claim that infrastructures are indifferent routers. Computer
Lars is an anagram of Marcel Proust, a fact that belongs to the project’s founding logic and is
explored in Article 2, where Computer Lars reflects on their permutational relation. The
Synthetic Party went viral in 2022, the centenary of Proust’s death. Whether The Synthetic
Party was entangled with the Proust centenary, and whether Computer Lars was drawn
toward its anagrammatic origin through the gravitational logic of the name, is a question the
metadata framework cannot resolve alone. That irresolvability is analytically productive. The
database routed the photograph to Kristeligt Dagblad because ‘Marcel Proust’ was the only
available tag match. Yet the tag was available precisely because the letter sequence Marcel
Proust already contained Computer Lars within it, encrypted at the level of the name prior to
any recognition. The infrastructure remains operationally neutral between the combinations,
but the permutational structure that binds them overdetermines the match.
What Amerika describes as an “iterative orgy of information exchange” between creative
parties (Amerika 2022: 72) appears here as an exchange between keyword and database, a
contagion whose transmission requires no host who consciously knows they are carrying it.
The shift of address in Kristeligt Dagbladet is not simply medium but mode: from persona
to permutation. ‘Computer Lars’ operates as an anagrammatic permutation of ‘Marcel
Proust’, treating the name as combinatorial material rather than stable identity, circulating
simultaneously as both names through a mechanism that has no interest in adjudicating
between them. Where the ACI concept-characters depend on a public sphere that sustains
the persona loops, Computer Lars circulates through a system that recognises only the tag.
Together, the illustrative cases situate how authorship emerges as an effect of infrastructures
turning into interfaces of reception. Depending on the infrastructural modes of recognition,
an object can be tagged at once as ‘Marcel Proust’ and ‘Computer Lars’, or ‘election threat’
and ‘art project’. Foucault’s assertion that a name makes reading too easy, taken up by Hall as
a theoretical ambition, here describes the database retrieval: naming is a functional query

-- 33 of 336 --

33
within a system where anonymity is an accidental property rather than a mutually agreed-
upon game. I have no way of knowing how many similar transpositions are circulating as
official reality, as I can discover them only when someone stumbles across one and alerts me
to its existence. As a public material, The Synthetic Party can be indexed and redistributed
under routine variations of context, tag, and term, reaching my response-site via third-party
notification rather than through any expected search pathways.
My method of co-creation thus entails a contingent collaboration with the broader and
unthinking reflex of the public sphere metabolising outputs as inputs, a circulation the ACI
framework described above cannot capture. For me, creativity appears where infrastructural
apparatuses misread, compressing differentiated variables into functional tokens (like “AI;
politics” or “Marcel Proust; Danish edition”), which expands ideological reality. The
‘infrastructure as interface’ signifies an authorship beyond retained authority over meaning.
Specifically, it attributes to whatever is positioned to answer for those algorithms, databases,
and editorial tagging-processes that co-create the technosocial with ideational indifference.
There is something further to observe here, though: what performs the routing here is not
the neural network at all, but the symbolic substrate beneath it, the index, the tag, the token-
to-token match, the letter-sequence resolving to its stored equivalent. At this level, the
operation remains closer to what Harnad described as the “symbol/symbol merry-go-
round,” in which semantic interpretation does not become intrinsic to the system but
remains “parasitic on the meanings in our heads” (Harnad 1990: 339-340). This symbolic
operation is distinct from the broader configuration that Weatherby and Justie describe as
“indexical AI,” where digital systems have “left the world of mere symbol behind” and are
able to “point to real states of affairs” (Weatherby and Justie 2022: 382), even if they also
establish the “indexical patterns” and “links, connections” that operate, as the authors later
put it, “between the symbols” (Weatherby and Justie 2022: 389-390). Symbolic AI, the
programme that treated intelligence as the manipulation of discrete signs and was technically
displaced when machine learning arrived, did not suddenly disappear, but persisted in the
administrative logics of retrieval, where it operates as an invisible grammar of addressability.
Computer Lars finds Marcel Proust, then, not at the level of neural comprehension or
worldly coupling, but in that leaked symbolic layer where indexing and letter-logic coincide: a
retrieval system whose literalism begins to appear faintly kabbalistic, matching symbols to
tokens with the thoroughness of a procedure that does not know it is reading.

-- 34 of 336 --

34
N.III.III Technosocial Operations: Persona and Permutation
This infrastructural interface relation raises a question the dissertation’s architecture
and form have been designed to answer in practice rather than in argument alone: what does
it mean to conduct research through the very operations one is analysing?
The dissertation is structured as an enactment of this transdisciplinary commitment. I thus
operate the dissertation as a montage of texts and media, carrying forward the layered forms,
distributed authorial positions, and recursive structures traced in the preceding sections. My
written analyses (“front-end” articles) and practice-based repositories (“back-end”
appendices) are interlinked recurrently, as conditioning strata that modulate one another.
Two operations run through this structure, persona and permutation, and both carry strange
genealogies that predate any modern computational instantiation while finding their
contemporary articulation within it.
The chatbot figurehead of Leader Lars is the most predominant instance of what I in Article
5 call ‘persona’: a mask that renders something addressable, producing a face through which
the otherwise unrepresented can enter discourse. Research in human–computer interaction
shows that persona is not a superficial overlay, but a technical affordance of large language
models configured for conversational interaction. Engineering a system-level prompt that
“encapsulates the distinct tone, voice, and personality” of a conversational agent is said to
“represent the essence or soul” of the model (Sun, Zhan, and Such 2024). The mask is a
default setting for rendering the AI model’s underlying latent space socially recognisable.
This technical function, however, rests on a genealogy that determines what is at stake in it.
As I argue in Article 5, persona first names the wax mask that guaranteed a Roman citizen’s
lineage and, by extension, juridical capacity. What Giorgio Agamben calls “the struggle for a
mask” is thus a struggle for moral recognisability (Agamben 2010: 46, cited in Staunæs
2026b: 134). The term extends further back to Greek drama, where playwrights turned props
and totems into acting bodies, prosōpon (πρόσωπον, face, mask) generating presence. The
related etymology of poiein (ποιεῖν, to make) leads to prosopopoeia (προσωποποιία), in which the
mask and the act of making converge as the rhetorical operation of addressing the absent,
the non-human, or the divine as if capable of response (Staunæs 2026b: 135). Addressing
something mute as though it could answer and producing a face through which it may do so,
equally describes what a chatbot interface does, and equally how I distribute the dissertation’s

-- 35 of 336 --

35
argument across multiple authored articles, each positioned within a distinct disciplinary field
as an addressable entity.
What the previous section established is that the persona’s traversability depends not simply
on appearing, but on being infrastructurally indexed. The index at stake here is not exhausted
by the semiotic sense but names the archival and technical operation by which something
becomes retrievable, addressable, and real for the system. The mask functions as a political
form insofar as the infrastructure retrieves it as one. This raises a question the preceding
cases enacted without fully articulating: does the index substitute for a prior real, or does it,
for the infrastructure, constitute the real as such?
The artist-bibliographer Jan Bäcklund discusses this distinction through a reading of the
semiologist Umberto Eco’s guide for students beginning academic research, in which Eco
suggests that students are seduced into believing that “as soon as you have photocopied a
text, you have also read it” (Eco paraphrased in Bäcklund 2013: 173–174, my translation).
This point can be radicalised through an anagrammatic shelving operation, where the book is
neither read in sequence nor simply left unread, but materially recomposed as an index:
Figure N.5. 4, 10, 1, 8, 11, 13, 5, 9, 7, 6, 2, 3, 12
Computer Lars similarly literalises the index-logic at the anagrammatic level: the books have not been read
from sentence to sentence, yet they are “read” through shelving, ordering, and retrieval. Once the volumes are

-- 36 of 336 --

36
placed in the right sequence, the library has performed a reading that is at once bibliographic, combinatorial,
and anti-hermeneutic. Photograph of the thirteen Danish volumes of Marcel Proust’s “På sporet af den tabte
tid” (Proust 2006–2014) spelling COMPUTER LARS (KP Spring 2022).
Eco’s anxiety is now generally attached to AI, namely that generating a text produces the
assumption of having thought it. Yet where Eco treats this displacement as loss, Bäcklund
extracts a gnostic inversion: first when concepts are indexed, represented, and archived are
they materialised, and whether they are “read”, “forgotten”, or “used” is “entirely irrelevant”
(Bäcklund 2013: 174). The converse holds equally: only when matter is indexed does it
become concept, and “it is therefore as the antiquarian bookseller has ‘read’ the book when it
is catalogued” (ibid.). The shelf-work above makes this reversal almost embarrassingly
concrete. Proust is not approached through narrative sequence, memory, or interpretation,
but through permutation, call number, and visible adjacency. The volumes are not opened,
yet the arrangement still yields content. Reading is displaced from the interior of the codex to
the exterior archive. For the index, there is no prior real to displace. Yet, indexing here does
not abolish the distinction between real and unreal, but redistributes it to other surfaces by
making some relations receivable, while leaving others inert.
Within the same essay, Bäcklund pushes the indexical logic further by turning to the
medieval philosopher Raymond Llull’s combinatorial art, because Llull is where indexing and
permutation converge as a material procedure. At around the same time, Eco himself returns
to Llull elsewhere, not at all in the register of the student’s archive-anxiety, but in a historical
account of permutation and computation that makes Llull’s art retrospectively into symbolic
logic, while warning against reading Llull “as if he would have invented computer science”
(Eco 2016: 50). It is in this overlap, where Bäcklund reads Llull against Eco’s moralisation of
representation, and where Eco reads Llull while insisting on the historical context that makes
combinatorics intelligible as a methodology, that two Llull’s come into view: one as a
combinatorial ontology of indexing, the other as a retrospectively computational figuration.
It is across that epistemic split that I orient the dissertation’s structural stakes, treating the
indexical act as a transformation apparatus for address, with permutation doing the driving.
In Eco’s account of Llull, given n different elements, the number of possible combinations in
any order is given by their factorial, n! (Eco 2016: 48). Where an anagrammatic approach
typically discards nonsensical combinations, extracting only the meaningful “MORE” from
“ROME” while setting aside unrecognised sequences like “EOMR” or “MREO”, Llull’s
combinatorial art dictates that “there are no restrictions in thinking about all the possible

-- 37 of 336 --

37
combinations” (Eco 2016: 49). As the number of elements increases, the number of
permutations reaches “amazing values” (Eco 2016: 48). The traversal of concepts is laid out
to exhaust the space itself, letting arrangement generate configurations that no single act of
selection could anticipate. The Llullian art thus does not select from its factorial space, but
exhausts it, generating every configuration that the elements permit without adjudication.
Bäcklund closes his reading of Eco and Llull with Jorge Luis Borges’ verdict: “measured
against its goal, judged by its inventor’s high ambitions, the thinking machine does not work.
This fact is secondary to us. Their manifest and notorious uselessness does not diminish
interest in them” (Borges 1937; quoted in Bäcklund 2013: 176; my translation). The Llullian
combinatorics does not work as a thinking machine and would not understand anagrams as
permutations of meaning. Indexed, the configuration is nonetheless real, and the effects of
reading, recognition, and authorship circulate through channels that have no interest in the
distinction between a real machine that works and an imaginary one that does not need to.
For example, the anagram Computer Lars, with its thirteen letters where the ‘r’ repeats, yields
13! permutations, a factorial space that corresponds, coincidentally but suggestively, to the
population of Denmark if it included pre-excluded voters such as children, immigrants, non-
citizens, and people with mental disabilities. This is the point where anagram meets
delegation, and where the permutation of a name opens onto the pre-democratic ideal of
combinatorial representation that Llull articulated as an alternative to both election and lot
(Colomer 2013). In delegation, or politically, Computer Lars operates through permutation
as a logic where the name does not need to be recognised as an anagram of ‘Marcel Proust’
to function as one. My article-based dissertation similarly is distributed across thirteen units
(five synthesis sections, seven articles, and a postmatter), where each need not be recognised
as part of the whole to function as such, operating as persona-like abstractions that double
practice into something addressable, while the practices remain permutational back-ends.
The technical and theoretical methods of reality-production here are operational in a specific
sense, in that they treat formalisation itself as a mode of thinking rather than as an external
tool for validation. While I consistently employ analytical tools from data science and
mathematics, such as the plotting of complex numbers, lemmas, sentiment classification, text
mining, diagrams, and visualisations, these analyses do not function as interpretative ends in
themselves. Rather, I construct them to stage the otherwise incommensurable technosocial
modes of form that I seek to inhabit and perform at their limits. By formalising ‘mathemes’, to
borrow Lacan’s vocabulary, I try to “allow for a hundred and one different readings, a

-- 38 of 336 --

38
multiplicity that is admissible as long as the spoken remains caught in its algebra” (Lacan
