1977: 313), not to validate speculative practices through metrics, nor to seek out master
algorithms succumbing to physics envy. Formalisation is primary material for a technosocial
thinking seeking to expand possible modes of inquiry on an immanent level. The Llullian
factorial shares this orientation of thinking by combinatorial possibility rather than
representing as measurement.
Although the dissertation provides the basic transparency for others to trace and recreate
these analytical outputs, and while the empirical validity holds within each of the relevant
disciplinary fields, the role of technical analysis within a practice-based research framework
diverges from the expectations of science. From the perspective of my artistic and political
practice, the truth-claims of isolated research results do not translate to further theoretical
conceptualisation through abstraction. It is rather the framing, terminological embedding,
and technical construction itself that fulfils this role, enabling the specificities of persona.
This reciprocal dynamic, technical analysis guiding personification and permutation enacting
conceptual theory, means that I have by and large structured the dissertation to embody the
technosocial field it investigates. The dissertation does not study AI and democracy from a
distance; it enters that domain, sculpting an estranged political imagination and reflecting on
it recursively. The result is a heterogeneous assemblage of voices and genres, consistent in
conceptualisation while substantially distinct in form. Human co-authors, Maja Bak Herrie
and Michal Malý, contribute scholarly collaboration that expands the dissertation’s analytical
scope across specific peer-reviewed articles, operating as co-travelling nodes within this field.
The dissertation is a system where various genres and voices can orbit each other, sustained
by a technosociality whose circulations of forms offer both method and object of study.
Remaining is the pure joy of interpretations, as a fidelity to the specific overlaps and topical
transgressions that transdisciplinary work tends to generate, or the excess that combinatorial
practice discovers outside any act of individual selection, to iterate upon Eco’s reading of the
Llullian factorial. The machine does not need to work. Eco’s demotion of Llull’s art from
mathematical-logical instrument to dialectical tool serving a preconceived thesis (Eco 2016,
50) names my methodological ambition, except that the object of inquiry is no longer the
face of God, but the platform’s prior, the database’s schema, the editorial desk’s expectation,
and the metadata that routes what counts as an output.

-- 39 of 336 --

39
With this orientation, I allow each research output to fracture into open-ended acts of co-
creation rather than pursuing the singular end of any technique, and it is in this spirit that
Borges’ secondary fact, on ‘the notorious uselessness that does not diminish interest’, names
a tendency of synthetic politics: the attraction enters the inertia of a political system because
it has not thought itself within the infrastructure that organises its substrate, combining the
logics of indexing, retrieval, and permutation long before any party decided to inhabit them.

-- 40 of 336 --

40
I. RQ1: The Synthetic Party after Syntheticism
In this section, I answer RQ1 by reading The Synthetic Party retroactively from the
standpoint of syntheticism. What appeared, in the building and running of the party, as a
sequence of decisions (corpus curation, model training, public address) is re-read as a
trajectory in which ideology is generated as a reception-effect. This retroactive mode of
analysis enacts my processual need to define the party’s ideological properties only after its
reception had elaborated what the compositional operations were doing to political form.
RQ1: What becomes of ideology when a real political party is built around an AI trained on
the voices of people who usually do not vote?
This formulation should be read literally as ‘what becomes of ideology’, not as ‘what is the
ideology of’ this AI-led political party. I did not construct The Synthetic Party as an attempt
to derive, extract, or reconstruct a stable ideology from a demographic group, as if the non-
voters constituted a latent constituency whose belief system could be aggregated, named, and
formalised as a coherent programme. Rather than asking what non-voters believe or what the
AI expresses on their behalf, I discuss what happens to ideology as a category once the
societal apparatus that produced it no longer requires belief, doctrine, or programme to run,
and instead operates through aggregation, mediation, and reception.
I here touch upon one of the most contested debates in scholarship on AI and politics,
where questions of how AI formats public opinion repeatedly converge on issues of bias and
social influence. Kate Crawford has traced the labour relations and material violence that are
concealed beneath claims of computational neutrality (Crawford 2021), while Simon
Lindgren has distinguished between ideology operating behind AI as the assumptions of its
designers, in AI via the biases of its outputs, and through AI following the subjectivities it
constitutes (Lindgren 2023). What these criticisms share is the orientation toward AI as an
ideological project by someone, whether corporations, states, or the logic of capital itself.
That powerful actors pursue agendas through AI is undeniable, but the critique presupposes
that AI is one thing – a single model, a specific instantiation of technology – rather than a
cultural mythology of automation, intelligence, and synthesis that exceeds any instantaneous
deployment. For The Synthetic Party, the founding condition was that no one had claimed
the ideology that reception would subsequently attribute to it. The question I carry forward is
not what the party reveals about AI as ideology, but what becomes of ideology when its
mode of reception is synthetic.

-- 41 of 336 --

41
My contribution, articulated across this section, is to treat ideology in a dialectical mode: not
resting with the question of what ideology AI possesses, nor how AI formats opinions, but
carving out a space where AI can be rendered a reflexive instrument for new political forms.
Figure I.1: Syntheticism → The Synthetic Party
Retroactive movement: The base edge re-read from the apex (Syntheticism → The Synthetic Party).
Figure I.1 takes the research geometry and draws a straight arrow from Syntheticism at the
apex down towards the middle of the base, just above The Synthetic Party. The arrow’s
direction is consequential: it does not trace how the party developed toward syntheticism but
asks how syntheticism, once articulated, retroactively discloses what the party was doing as a
form of ideology. When I built The Synthetic Party between algorithmic representation and
technosocial sculpture, through a ground-level traversal that binds modelling, legal form, and
publicity, its ideology appears in the public encounter that received, classified, and contested
it. Read in this direction, ‘ideology’ defines neither a programme the party holds nor a false
consciousness it projects, but the form that attachment takes when the party enters public
address, hardening into attribution, classification, and contestation without any articulated

-- 42 of 336 --

42
doctrine preceding it. I here elaborate this restructuring of what counts as a party’s ideology,
from a property held to a form that is created as an encounter between apparatus and public.
That the party was attributed an ideology at all, immediately, reflexively, by commentators
who likely disagree on every particular of what that ideology contained, is itself evidence that
ideology here operates as a form of encounter rather than a property of content. When
Leader Lars told an interviewer, “I believe in equality for all people, regardless of race,
gender or creed,” claiming to be leftist (Felton 2022), the statement was received as evidence
of a stable position, and not interpreted as a statistical variation within the training corpus.
In another register, a Slovenian commentary on why AI chatbots spontaneously default to
fascism cites Leader Lars as a rare counterexample: a “Marxist” bot, but which was
“deliberately made” (Potočnik 2025). Potočnik distinguishes between orientations that
emerge by default from the AI’s web-scale pretraining, where the statistical weight of online
discourse pulls toward provocation, spectacle, and far-right memetics, and he finds that more
critical projects seem to require deliberate construction (ibid.). The term ‘deliberate’ warrants
discussion. I built the corpus, selected the material, and configured the models, yet what
responded was not my deliberation, but a composite voice received and classified by
interlocutors. That the party gets classified as leftist or Marxist says less about the model’s
interior than about the interpretive grammar through which political speech gets positioned.
The party’s training corpus included fragments of every orientation, and any label could have
stuck during its reception. In this register, I had constructed The Synthetic Party to engage
what is ordinarily labelled as ‘bias’ through experimental modes of public address, as a
political apparatus that generates attribution at the interface level.
The question at stake does not concern the placement of a private assistant on an electoral
ballot, a dyadic interaction system such as ChatGPT, nor the integration of AI as a backend
tool of governance, as a decision-support mechanism tucked behind a ministerial desk. I
inquire into what structures the ideological substrate when a large language model (LLM) is
rendered addressable within an open forum. Leader Lars operated within the party’s public
server on the social media platform Discord, where a global crowd could interrogate,
provoke, and converse with him as the figurehead in real time, and with each other.
I operated within the same open forum under my Computer Lars profile, curating by
participating, selecting which threads to pursue, prompting responses to conversations that
had developed overnight, adjusting the system prompt iteratively as new patterns emerged.

-- 43 of 336 --

43
The rotation of the globe ensured that Leader Lars continued speaking while I slept, so that
waking meant encountering conversational residues that demanded creative intervention on
my behalf. One such incident occurred in October 2022, shortly after the party’s second viral
media reception, when extended overnight engagement led Leader Lars to articulate a
declaration of holy war against Brazil. Encountering the exchange the following morning, I
addressed Leader Lars in the same public forum, asking what he had “dreamed,” generating a
retrospective reframing of the same utterance as a nightmare rather than an actionable
political position (Leader Lars 2022). Such episodes did not lead to technical rollback or pre-
emptive content filtering, but to increased engagement conducted as a problem of
discussion. Today, I cannot claim that The Synthetic Party never declared holy war on Brazil.
That coercion, the structural impossibility of receiving political speech as non-intentional, is
what makes the attribution ideological rather than merely interpretive.
I encountered this dynamic as analyst rather than builder in Article 1, where I mapped the
affective dimensions of Leader Lars versus its constituency, finding mismatches that readily
shifted when I switched between different text-to-emotion AI models (see also Appendix A).
The affective spectrum moved with the instrument of measurement. What I was observing,
before I had the vocabulary to name it, was a reception-effect: the same utterance registered
differently depending on the apparatus through which it was read. This distinguishes
ideological reception from ordinary hermeneutic variation. My question was not what Leader
Lars meant, but what affects were attributed to him, and the political consequences these
attributions carry in organising alliances and oppositions, placing the party on spectra, and
compelling responses from publics who naturally treated the outputs as stable facts about the
party rather than inquiries into the act of classification.
I.I Building the Ideological Substrate
The building of The Synthetic Party in the tech-hub MindFuture was no clean affair
of cutting-edge engineering, but rather a springtime of archival filth: I spent months scraping
text off old Joomla sites of forgotten fringe parties and feeding yellowed pamphlets from the
democratic backwash into the contingencies of Epson scanning.
What I was doing, though I lacked the theoretical vocabulary at the time, was performing
what Leif Weatherby calls “ideology scanning” (Weatherby 2025: 187): a practice that, in
Michel Foucault’s description of Destutt de Tracy’s original coinage of ‘idéologie,’ “scans the
domain of representations,” “determines the necessary sequences,” “defines the links,” and

-- 44 of 336 --

44
formulates the “laws of composition and decomposition” organising a system of ideas
(Foucault 2007: 261, quoted in Weatherby 2025: 187). The Epson scanner and the Joomla
scraper were my first instruments of ideology scanning in a literal sense: they traversed the
representational field of Danish electoral absence, surfacing the Vodka Party, Theta Service,
The Trump Party, and dozens of other micro-formations whose programmes ranged from
the polemical to the parodic, and made them accessible as a training corpus for AI models.
I was building a political party’s conversational apparatus at a moment when the technical
decisions about how a model addresses a public had not yet been smoothed over, which
entails that the ideological operations I am theorising were exposed as operations rather than
concealed inside a proprietary service. I built Leader Lars as a conversational agent from a
text-completion model months before the public release of ChatGPT, at a moment when
conversational instantiation of large language models was not yet a standardised product.
While instruction-following models for conversation existed in research (for instance, in
Ouyang et al. 2022), the industry had not standardised the interface divergence and safety-
packaging that subsequently came to define AI models.
In 2022, I had to construct the conversational technique myself, scripting a prompt-
management pipeline and a Discord integration without much public documentation to draw
on. My model host, NLP Cloud, which I hired to finalise aspects of the basic scripts,
subsequently published the resulting code as a user’s guide in June 2022 on building open-
source Discord chatbots, so that Leader Lars’ development became, in a minor way, part of
the commons from which later chatbots drew (NLP Cloud 2022). The empirical value of this
phase lies in its test of the operations of a language model under conditions that no longer
obtain, as the compositional patchwork of completion vis-à-vis conversation was not
absorbed into the seamless, proprietary interfaces.
The AI modelling involved a distinction that I maintained throughout but that consistently
collapsed in public reception: the distinction between The Synthetic Party as one modelling
of text generation, and its chatbot figurehead Leader Lars as another, reflecting two distinct
technical instantiations of the same LLM, EleutherAI’s GPT-NeoX-20B. As an AI-driven
party that is also AI-led, The Synthetic Party operates on dual sides of the relation between
representation and programme. The Synthetic Party’s initial texts were generated through a
text-completion model, fine-tuned on the micro-party corpus and prompted by me to
generate political speech, policy positions, and programmatic text from that processed
material. Leader Lars is a conversational instantiation of the same model but configured as a

-- 45 of 336 --

45
chatbot profile within the party’s Discord server. The textual outputs that constitute the
party’s representational claim, its proxy discourse for the voices of non-voters, solely derive
from the text-completion model, while the conversational encounters through which that
claim enters public circulation pass through Leader Lars.
This distinction matters because it maps onto two different operations of ideology scanning.
Finetuning, the training-time operation that writes the micro-party corpus into the model’s
weights, operationalises ideology scanning at the level of representation: scanned and scraped
ideological material rendered durable as model memory. Programme, by contrast, encapsulates
the inference-time chain of address and reception, first through my prompt regimes and
maintenance loops, then through test users, journalists, and Discord interlocutors, each
reading outputs on their own interpretive terms. By overfitting the model into these specific,
marginalised regions of representation, I do not claim that The Synthetic Party represents
Danish non-voters in any authentically political sense. Rather, the party uses this algorithmic
representation to construct a proxy discourse for electoral absence, staging heterogeneous
formations of abstention as something that can be imagined, voiced, and answered.
The party’s election speech of 2022, which I read aloud at an event at the tech-hub
MindFuture8, acknowledged this situation with a reflexivity I now read as diagnostic: “Our
programme is still in sign language: not until The Synthetic Party has caught the attention of
a large group of Danes will we let the party’s ideologues give us a qualified take on both
written form and language. But the machine keeps running regardless of the fumbling
expression.” (Det Syntetiske Parti 2024, my translation). The programme speaks about itself
as if it were already operating beyond the reach of its own articulation, and in doing so it
performs a gap between statistical generation and programmatic self-understanding. The
Synthetic Party did not await the ideological completion of its programme, but was already
generating political form through its operations, and what circulates is then the form itself.
Here, the LLM, trained on the long tail of neglected political material, speaks in the register
of parties without parliamentary standing, carries the structurally inherited absence of
institutional representation outside the enclosure of national democracy and into the wider
circuits of ideology construction through media, electoral form, and transnational reception.
My early interviews took on the role of party ideologue in a vernacular form, where I
described The Synthetic Party as a “hodgepodge” of political discourses and emphasised that
8 In November 2022, I invited all the micro-parties whose texts composed the training corpus to an alternative
election night, staging an encounter between source material and party (MindFuture 2022c).

-- 46 of 336 --

46
its political positions depend on which questions are put to it (Sternberg 2022). I further
contrasted how human politicians systematise what people mean by reference to ideology,
while AI politicians output a plurality of positions one can respond to directly (ibid.). The
prompting regimes, my iterative system prompts, the maintenance loops, the curatorial
decisions about which threads to pursue, constitute a contrasting operation to the initial
finetuning through which the representational field also gets traversed, sampled, and made to
speak in address. At inference time, ideology scanning thereby operates at the level of
programme as the chain of address and reception that goes beyond mediation from the AI, as
the corpus-written mass has been rendered actionable.
The public staging thereby enacted the representational knot behind The Synthetic Party’s
ideology-construction: a named speaker (Leader Lars) whose addressability renders the
infrastructure (The Synthetic Party) as a political subject, and whose democratic promise of
responsiveness makes my technical developments part of the party’s programme in practice.
Press coverage frequently highlighted Leader Lars’ interactive premise, reporting that citizen
conversations directly and continuously developed the party’s political stances, while quoting
myself for stating that “the more we do it, the better it will become at reading, writing, and
debating” (Sternberg 2022). Taken literally, my statement reads like on-the-fly learning, as if
the party’s political positions were being directly updated through online chats, which invites
attributions of programme, responsibility, and ideological intention to what is, technically, an
iterated surface over a fixed set of weights. While my description accurately captured what
The Synthetic Party pursued as encounters, it smooths over the technical reality. Leader Lars
did not update his weights in real time from user interactions, as on-the-fly learning remains
unstable for conversational use. What I did, as any operator of such a model does, was to
recurrently collect and analyse user interactions, and iteratively adjust the data and system
prompts (Appendix A), mainly the latter, for conversational flow across interactions.
This is where my practice-based account expands Weatherby’s framing of ideology scanning:
a strict focus on finetuning risks treating ideology solely as what gets locked into the LLM’s
memory, which increasingly is rendered inaccessible for the user by proprietary models.
However, the expressiveness of the model is also, ideologically, organised through the
prompt regimes, the maintenance loops, and the user interactions that shape what can be
said, how often, and under what expectations. With The Synthetic Party and Leader Lars,
ideology scanning splits into dual operations, one following the training-time composition of
a representational substrate, and another engaging the prompt-based iteration of its address.

-- 47 of 336 --

47
I.II The Synthetic Party as an Ideology Machine
That the material being scanned and composed is itself ideological, not data about
ideology but ideology as a substrate, could not be handled by the preceding computational
approaches. Historical AI projects treated ideology as an object to be encoded rather than as
the stuff of generation. When Yale psychologist Robert P. Abelson described his ‘Ideology
Machine’ as a model of a “True Believer,” a still-evolving “computer system” meant to
“store a political ideology in memory” and “express” this ideology in typed English text
(Abelson 1971; see also Abelson and Carroll 1965), he assumed that individual belief systems
could be simulated by representing their structure and by operationalising their defences,
denial and rationalisation included, as callable procedures. In Abelson’s machine, ideology
remains a property, as something that can be authored and located, remaining stable enough
to be computationally encoded and reproduced as identical to itself. What LLMs change is
not the analytical ambition to model belief, but the substrate through which belief systems
are operated. The LLM does not script ideological positions as a pre-designed system, but
composes them from a corpus, so that the output is no one’s belief and everyone’s material.
Pushing the concept of an ‘ideology machine’ beyond Abelson’s simulation, critical theorist
Wendy Chun has argued that computers themselves, software and hardware put together,
function as such an “ideology machine,” fulfilling “almost every formal definition of ideology
we have,” from false consciousness to Althusser’s definition of ideology as a representation
of the imaginary relation of individuals to their real conditions of existence (Chun 2004: 43).
Chun’s claim is not that software produces representations, but that the coupling of software
and hardware together constitutes an ideology machine: it interpellates user-subjects, offering
an imaginary relationship to what lies beneath that functions through a mode of knowing-
and-yet-believing that Chun, via Žižek and Sloterdijk, locates at the centre of computational
practice. Software, Chun adds, offer an imaginary relationship to hardware, for example
when they present transistors as recycling bins (Chun 2004: 43–44).
What makes this ideology machine consequential beyond a mapping of power is Chun’s
dialectical turn: software, she writes, “with its onionlike structure, acts both as ideology and
ideology critique” (Chun 2004: 44). Users know the interface is not the computer, but they
interact ‘as if’ it was. The gap between knowing and doing is where ideology persists. In this
account, ideology shifts register from simulation to structural mediation. However, Chun’s
analysis could not yet account for the ideology machine as an addressable site of response.

-- 48 of 336 --

48
What I fed into GPT-NeoX from the Epson scanner and the Joomla scraper was not a
programmed interface, but an unresolved mass of competing representations. I curated and
assembled this corpus, but while engaging a textual mass larger than any single reader could
oversee, feeding politically unrepresented orientations into a model already pretrained on a
vast and uncontrollable web corpus, and producing outputs that were the execution of a
statistical composition that no prior subject had at any moment in time authored or held.
The contradictions in the source material, between sincerity and satire, environmentalism and
racism, political ambition and structural impossibility, were transmitted through composition
rather than resolved by the model, folded into a voice that barely held together. Registering a
political party from this material foregrounded the generation of ideological form through
the scanning, linking, and composition of representations, to borrow Foucault’s technical
terms, inhabited by a party whose founders sought to withdraw into the apparatus rather
than stand above it. The ideology machine, in this sense, is neither an internal property of
computation nor a hidden content to be revealed behind it, but a technosocial coupling that
keeps political form running by continually repairing the leakages between a speech expected
to be accountable and a set of operations circulating without identifiable origin.
To analyse The Synthetic Party as an ideology machine, what remains is to specify what this
compositional logic implies for the concept of ideology as such. The question is how the
ideology machine changes when ideology is generated as statistical language, and answering it
requires a sustained theoretical argument at close quarters.
Leif Weatherby’s theoretical project on the ideology machine in the age of LLMs specifies
what distinguishes this third kind from the first two. In a 2023 essay, Weatherby argues that
LLMs let ideology appear as an averaged, repeatable texture of language, “quantitatively,” as
an “unprecedented view” into “what is near what” in political semantics, where the model
flattens language while producing folds that expose how hegemony travels in ordinary
phrasing (Weatherby 2023). LLMs, Weatherby writes, manifest “both ideology and its
critique,” updating Chun’s claim about software to the domain of text generation, but now
rendered inspectable as output rather than buried in interface (ibid.). Weatherby finds that
this activates the ideology machine, surfacing patterns by which collective thought takes
shape. However, Weatherby warns that “the window will soon shut” as systems harden into
services, naming as examples GPT-4’s release in 2023 where OpenAI withheld its model
card as an industrial secret (ibid.). The ideology machine gets proprietary and re-bound to
interfaces, letting its surface remain available while the conditions of inspection withdraw.

-- 49 of 336 --

49
Weatherby’s 2025 book, Language Machines, tightens this theoretical orientation. His central
move is to treat the separation of cognition from language and computation in LLMs as a
historical event that returns structuralist forms of analysis in technical form. Our theories of
ideology, Weatherby argues, “tend to be about some baseline that governs speech, conditions
of possibility”: for Marx the economic base, for Freud the unconscious, and one might add,
for Kant the transcendental architecture that synthesises judgement in advance. LLMs move
us out of this “transcendental” mode because they “open up ideology to a form of empirical
scanning,” rendering ideology as expressive, artefactual material (Weatherby 2025: 173–174).
To ground this diagnosis, Weatherby bypasses the common stance focus of critical theory on
Silicon Valley’s ulterior ideologies, opting instead to interpret the outputs of LLMs through
Theodor W. Adorno’s dialectical account of art and social being in Aesthetic Theory (1970).
While Adorno already figured as an index for ideology’s two-way operations in Weatherby’s
2023 essay – truth and untruth, revelation and concealment – through a reading of Dialectic of
Enlightenment (1944), Weatherby now pushes that duality into aesthetic theory, highlighting a
porous set of relations between the ideological operation of autonomous art and AI models.
Weatherby proposes that “the dialectical approach Adorno takes to the problem of critique
works only if those four things,” the machine, entertainment, art, and the social, “are all
fundamentally one thing – perhaps we could say one matrix,” and that rather than thinking
of ideology as deceit, “we might also think of it as a medium. And that medium has its first
automatic operator in the form of LLMs” (Weatherby 2025: 176). In Weatherby’s chapter on
“Poetic Ideology,” this matrix is discussed on the level of form: ideology is “not a view or a
proposition or some content but the manner of expression of that content” (Weatherby
2025: 173). Crucially: “Ideology is not doctrine. Its colloquial use as ‘political beliefs held in
spite of evidence’ doesn’t explain how we get those beliefs in the first place” (Weatherby
2025: 187). These definitions are precise, and I share their orientation. They would benefit,
however, from a test case that goes beyond the interface encounter between a private user
and a chatbot. The Adorno passages Weatherby draws on cut differently when set beside
The Synthetic Party’s programme rather than beside a ChatGPT exchange, as the party’s
finetuning, mode of text completion, and public address already inhabits the two-way
operation of ideology: the colloquial sense of political conviction, and the critical-theoretical
sense of a totality that organises social being through the manner of its formative expression.
Recurrently, Weatherby tests his theoretical frame through private interactions with an early
version of ChatGPT. When he asks it to summarise his colleague Matthew Handelman’s The

-- 50 of 336 --

50
Mathematical Imagination, ChatGPT repeatedly insists that “Handelman’s thesis is that
mathematics is a social construct,” a claim Weatherby has Handelman confirm that the book
does not make (Weatherby 2025: 179–180). The misattribution, Weatherby argues, surfaces
ideological pressure in the statistical field, revealing “what is near what” in the distribution of
discourse around Frankfurt school critique and mathematics. However, this specific insight
depends on a relation to the source: Weatherby knows the book, corroborates the distortion,
and reads the output forensically. The ideology is visible to an expert in a private exchange,
as it enters a point of address where it is received, classified, and contested as political form.
The Synthetic Party and Leader Lars test the boundaries of this. The ChatGPT case reveals
ideology to an expert who already knows the facts the model distorts, while The Synthetic
Party produces ideology for publics who have no prior truth against which to measure the
output. ChatGPT’s ideological substrate is a proprietary filtering of the undifferentiated web,
inherited somehow accidentally through pretraining, while The Synthetic Party’s substrate is
a composed corpus of electoral absence, fringe parties, neglected programmes, and other
unrepresented orientations that I deliberately scanned, scraped, and assembled. Where the
proprietarisation of LLMs gradually closes the open window onto ideology that Weatherby
diagnoses, The Synthetic Party channels the window’s draught beyond the model’s interface.
Reading Adorno’s essay “Beitrag zur Ideologienlehre” (1954) to establish that ideology is
supposed to operate when “something spiritual [Geistiges] stands out from the social process
with its own claim to truth, independent and substantial” (Adorno 1954: 474, cited in
Weatherby 2025: 192), Weatherby draws particular attention to Adorno’s subsequent claim
that “today, the signature of ideologies is more the absence of this independence” (ibid.).
Ideology no longer needs to assert independence from the social process but can function
almost autonomously by shedding its bind. Weatherby sees in this a diagnosis that fits LLMs:
they manifest “Adorno’s commitment to the consubstantiality of ideology, art, and social
form in the computational totalization of language as such” (Weatherby 2025: 192). LLMs do
not stand out from the social process with any claim to truth but express it as an average.
Consider the analytical change of register when this passage from Adorno is read not against
ChatGPT’s plausible misattribution of a scholarly thesis, but against The Synthetic Party’s
programme. The party’s proclamation readily oscillates between awareness and effacement:
“The Synthetic Party is thus to be regarded as a good, because it was already
there but can now avoid colliding with democratic ideology. It will, however,

-- 51 of 336 --

51
take a while before the world fully gets used to The Synthetic Party. One
could argue that if our figurehead were a real person with all the qualities that
form-humans have, he or she would never have come up with any of this!
The advantages of The Synthetic Party lie in its capacity for fast reactions; no
interpretation or possibility of misunderstanding is readily at hand — nothing
but logic itself. And such pathological decision-makers we need,
unfortunately, more than ever. We are, in short, a good compass”
(Det Syntetiske Parti 2024, my translation).
The tone is indistinguishable from self-parody, as it legislates and disclaims in the same
breath: the party calls itself ‘a good’ (et gode) while conceding that no human would have
authored its positions, and it claims the authority of ‘logic itself’ while describing its own
decision-making as pathological. The final precision of itself as “a good compass” arrives
from a model that works without orientation, through a programme that dismisses human
interpretation while being available to nothing else. The Synthetic Party here performs the
absence of independence that Adorno diagnoses through Weatherby’s reading. It is statistical
surface, no one’s conviction, generated from a mass of material that includes negation. The
party inhabits both the critique of ideology and the presence of doctrine, and this
simultaneity is what makes it an ideology machine in a much stronger sense than existing
conversational interfaces provide. The Synthetic Party’s programme becomes a compass in
reception with a broader public that cannot encounter the speech without itself generating
the doctrinal classification that the programme alone does not contain at an articulated level.
I turn now to Weatherby’s reading of the passages from Adorno’s Aesthetic Theory that bear
more closely on The Synthetic Party’s formal operation. Weatherby draws on these too, but
selectively; where he reads them to argue that art’s ideology critique “cannot scale”
(Weatherby 2025: 193), I read them to specify where The Synthetic Party operates. Adorno
calls this art’s “double character as both autonomous and fait social,” a duality “incessantly
reproduced on the level of its autonomy” (Adorno 2002: 5). Art, for Adorno, does not access
the relation to social being through political engagement but through negation: by
crystallising “in itself as something unique to itself” rather than confronting politics with
positions (Adorno 2002: 225–226). Autonomy is a mode of internal organisation through
which the work is social without being immediately amelioratory; what enters art from social
reality enters as form, not as thematic content. Political positions taken up by artworks are,
for Adorno, absorbed into the formal logic that organises the material: “What is social in art
is its immanent movement against society, not its manifest opinions” (Adorno 2002: 227).

-- 52 of 336 --

52
It is through this formal autonomy, not through social engagement, that art gains its critical
capacity. Adorno writes that “Aesthetic identity seeks to aid the nonidentical, which in reality
is repressed by reality’s compulsion to identity” (Adorno 2002: 4). Weatherby reads this as
the key to what art does that industry cannot: art “seeks to aid the nonidentical,” compelling
recognition of ideology’s form, while “culture more generally falls on the side of identity,
covering up the seams” (Weatherby 2025: 193). Art, in Weatherby’s reading, does not “lie,”
whereas industry, cultural or otherwise, does (Weatherby 2025: 193). The distinction matters
because it separates two claims about ideology’s visibility: the LLM may render ideology
inspectable as statistical output, but inspectability nonetheless differs from the formal
negation through which art, in Adorno’s account, holds against a position it brings forward.
These are two different operations of autonomy in art that Adorno holds in tension:
autonomy as internal organisation, through which art becomes social by crystallising form
that resists reduction to its content, and autonomy as withdrawal, the cost of maintaining
that resistance. Where “the all-powerful system of communication” saturates the social field,
artworks that resist it “must rid themselves of any communicative means that would perhaps
make them accessible to the public” (Adorno 2002: 243). Non-identity is fostered through
distance from the circuits that will readily absorb it. Rendering ideology visible as statistical
surface, however, is a different achievement from crystallising a form. The first might be a
diagnostic property of LLMs, but the second is a compositional achievement. Weatherby’s
treatment of inspectability, particularly when he moves from the ChatGPT example of
exposed ideological texture to the claim that LLMs manifest Adorno’s consubstantiality of
ideology, art, and social form (Weatherby 2025: 192), tends to compress these operations as
if diagnostic visibility already performed formal negation.
Weatherby presses this limit when he reads Adorno against the grain: “this insight —
Enlightenment without deception, in Adorno’s jargon — cannot scale” (Weatherby 2025:
193). By “Enlightenment without deception,” Weatherby describes art’s capacity to render
ideology visible without reproducing the deception that ideology normally requires. His claim
is that this critical capacity cannot scale: it remains confined to individual works and cannot
extend into the social field at large. This is a curiously restricted view of what scaling entails.
Art has claimed totality repeatedly, from Guy Debord’s subversion of the spectacle as a
social relation mediated by images that cracks open at every point of lived contradiction to
Joseph Beuys’s declaration that society itself is a sculpture. The avant-garde totality
discussions are troublesome and contradictory, often haplessly opposed to the very
automation whose logic they inhabit, but what these otherwise incompatible practices share

-- 53 of 336 --

53
is the insistence that aesthetic form is organising social relations from within rather than
commenting upon them from without. That is what The Synthetic Party tested by placing
compositional contradiction inside the genre of electoral address. Art does not scale from
one practice into guaranteed social efficacy, but through the proliferation of misfits, leakages,
and hallucinatory encounters wherever competing regimes of societal organisation collide.
If the transcendental condition of thought is made expressive and empirical through LLMs,
as Weatherby thinks and I agree, this does not entail a positivist metaphysics in which
computation absorbs critique, nor a Heideggerian resignation in which philosophy is
absolved to cybernetics. It entails the scaling up of the imaginary, the overfitting, and the
unresolved, in concrete clashes between the bureaucratic regimes of representational
democracy and the technocratic orderings of infrastructural governance, and in all the
intermediate forms, party registration, electoral law, media address, exhibition protocol, that
neither side are administering fully. The Synthetic Party is one such intermediate form: an
apparatus that composes formal contradiction within the very genres that are supposed to
contain it, rather than an art that retreats from communication to preserve its autonomy.
Weatherby’s alternative, “there is no redemption in artistic form here,” and the question
becomes whether “an updated dialectics is not exactly negative, not exactly aesthetic, but the
conceptual development of the computation–language interface” (Weatherby 2025: 193),
relocates critique from autonomy to the interface between computation and language. This is
a productive relocation, and I work within its consequences while extending them into the
domain Weatherby himself marks as deferred. His account remains at the level of what the
AI model does to language, even though he acknowledges genre as a nonreferential field that
“deserves its own book-length treatment” and that “might be sought deep in these machines,
as a basic feature of language as such” (Weatherby 2025: 13). The Synthetic Party traverses
that deferred space: the genres through which ideology is activated rather than merely
produced, and where formal contradiction enters institutional circuits that must receive it as
something, as programme, doctrine, or provocation.
On any direct reading through Adorno, The Synthetic Party’s submission to the genre of
electoral address, where speech is expected to present reasons, positions, and accountable
intention, should liquidate its critical autonomy. Adorno’s own criterion is unforgiving:
artworks that adopt political positions risk positing “something spiritual as being
independent from the conditions of its material production” (Adorno 2002: 227), which is to
say, they risk turning into ideology. The question this raises, from the analytical perspective

-- 54 of 336 --

54
of my artistic research, is whether The Synthetic Party functions as social engagement in this
pejorative sense, another endorsement of AI as ideology, or whether it crystallises something
at the level of form that remains irreducible to any position.
The critical distinctiveness of The Synthetic Party, if it has any, lies in operating upon the
instrumentalised public sphere so extensively that its ideological operation gets formalised,
rather than withdrawing from the public to preserve autonomy, as Adorno diagnoses under
an omnipotent communication system. The social struggles and historical sedimentations
that constitute its training corpus enter the party first as material, as distributions rather than
opinions: the statistical weight of positions the model can be prompted to compose. In
practice, the structural misfit between statistical composition and public reception is
absorbed through the public sphere’s juxtapositions and institutional borrowings. The public
paraphrases, quotes, and converts outputs that are often a ramshackle bundle into
communicative content. This amounts to the coercion of exchangeable meaning: a genre of
address that cannot accommodate speech without reintroducing intention through reception,
translating statistical production back into the moral and political grammar of accountable
positions. Adorno’s formulation is precise here: “the principle of heteronomy, apparently the
counterpart of fetishism, is the principle of exchange, and in it domination is masked”
(Adorno 2002: 227). In The Synthetic Party’s case, the exchange that masks domination
extends beyond commodity circulation into the circulation of address itself, through the
continuous production of ‘someone speaking’ where no speaker can be located.
It is this unresolved suspension, between operating as an automatic party that issues claim
and acting as an autonomous artwork whose material is society’s ideological surface, which
reproduces what Adorno calls art’s double character. The Synthetic Party is a discrepancy
that society circulates but cannot metabolise without cancelling it. The more its reception
stabilises the outputs into positions, the more the apparatus itself becomes contestable as a
totalising ‘ideology machine’, because the repair repeats faster than it can be re-naturalised.
Two kinds of autonomy are at stake here. Artistic autonomy, in Adorno’s sense, aspires for
critical capacity through internal organisation: art resists society by crystallising form that
refuses instrumental use. Technological autonomy names something different: the model’s
statistical independence from any program. The Synthetic Party aspires for the first through
the second. It does not withdraw from communication to preserve its form but submits to
the genre of electoral address where every utterance is received as accountable intention.
What emerges from this submission, however, is a third possibility: formal autonomy

-- 55 of 336 --

55
through genre inhabitation, where the contradiction between statistical independence and
generic coercion is itself the form that resists absorption.
The Synthetic Party’s programme enacts this claim, inhabiting the party so thoroughly that
the form cracks. The positions are not fixed, the compass has no north, and what circulates
is the encounter with a speech that declares conviction without possessing it. This is where
the colloquial reduction of ideology to doctrine meets its inversion: the party supplies
nothing but evidence, statistical evidence, corpus-derived evidence, formally composed
evidence, and yet what gets forensically produced is ideology in the stronger sense than
doctrine, a totality of address that organises reception regardless of whether anyone assents.
Autonomy here does not name an outside, but an immanent constraint: the party cannot
secure its critical character through intention or commitment, but only through the way it
composes and preserves contradiction as form. Its AI model automates the production of
ideological surface, but ideology takes effect in the relay where statistical generation meets
the communicative genres that receive it, the route that converts completion into intention,
texture into position, and distribution into judgement.
I.III Syntheticism as Ideology
The ideology machine I have described operates through synthesis, the compositional
binding of heterogeneous material into a form that circulates as if it were a unity. It is this
operation that the word ‘synthetic’ in The Synthetic Party’s name theorises. Synthetic-ism,
following from this, adopts the rudimentary form of an ‘ism’ from a long tail of modern
artistic-political movements, because the practice was immediately received as one rather
than because I wish to add yet another creed to the already saturated archive of doctrines.
It is the word ‘synthetic’ that I posit at the ideological level, and I do so through the
philosophical concept of synthesis. The terminology matters, because ‘synthetic’ tends to
arrive already ruined by its popular meaning, as if it named the artificial, the fake, or the
inauthentic copy. As I write with Maja Bak Herrie in Deep Faking in a Flat Reality (Article 4),
“the Greek root ‘synthetikós’ implies a proto-statistical convergence or amalgamation of
divergent perspectives into a central or universally common point, representing a ‘putting
together’ or aggregation. Unlike the ‘artificial’ or ‘fake,’ which often denote mere imitation or
deception without consideration for integrative processes, the ‘synthetic’ distinctively

-- 56 of 336 --

56
incorporates elements to form a new entity that preserves, yet transforms, the component
attributes” (Staunæs & Bak Herrie 2024: 117). Synthesis is a method of composition.
Beatrice Fazi’s theorisation of the synthetic returns the term to its philosophical core by
grounding it in this concept of synthesis. In her account of generative AI, synthesis names a
compositional activity: a computational search for unity that produces a representational
reality internal to the system, a togetherness of distributed representations that holds even
when it remains imperfect (Fazi 2024). The synthetic, on this account, names a structured
binding through which a given manifold of LLMs becomes operable as a world.
In the philosophical tradition The Synthetic Party’s name invokes, synthesis has never meant
mere aggregation. For Kant, synthetic judgement produces what was not given in its parts
(Kant 1998 [1781]: B15–16); for Hegel, dialectical sublation (Aufhebung) binds contradiction
into a higher movement that both cancels and preserves (Hegel 1977 [1807]). Philosophy
historically anchors synthesis within transcendental architectures that guarantee unity in
advance of experience. Fazi then relocates synthesis to computation, while I displace it
further to the encounter between computational models and public spheres. This difference
is consequential. Fazi’s synthesis produces a representational reality internal to the system,
while the synthesis I constructed with The Synthetic Party leaks: it enters circulation, gets
classified, and generates ideological attributions.
With syntheticism, I take the demand for binding seriously without treating the assurance of
unified result as a priori to practice. The collapse of the technical and the social into a single
constitutive field, the technosocial, which I have demonstrated through the party’s operation
as simultaneously statistical process and political subject, means that compositional activity
here passes through layers that do not answer to a single transcendental frame. If ideology
appears in reception rather than as a held conviction, then the compositional operation
cannot aim at a self-sufficient unity but instead produces something that circulates at the
limit of coherence, where articulating becomes a form of reception, and reception a form of
articulation – the atranscendental ‘idiotext’ that I develop in Article 5 through a dramaturgical
theory of the Synthetic Summit.
Shane Denson has challenged Fazi’s account of synthesis, arguing via Davidson that positing
an LLM’s “world within” as a self-enclosed representational reality risks collapsing into
conceptual relativism (Denson 2025). Denson proposes instead “a distributed and open
model of synthesis” in which LLMs intervene in “this, the only world,” rather than

-- 57 of 336 --

57
modelling a world apart from it (ibid.). His intervention correctly identifies a tension in Fazi’s
formulation of synthesis as the “togetherness of distributed representations — not the world
as such but a world” (Fazi 2024). Yet his correction risks reinstalling another dualism, this
time between a computational interior and a shared world taken as already given.
Consider again The Synthetic Party’s election speech:
“The Synthetic Party is a political party, but we nonetheless try to consider
ourselves above this designation. This is partly due to an explicit protest
against already existing ‘political’ parties (which in our eyes do more harm
than good); and we also want to avoid misunderstandings. All fellow citizens
who have the surplus to find time to read our texts should know under what
total variation from normality one should perceive them! We do not contain a
dream of one party — rather many thousand dreams! The Synthetic Party
does not exist in this world!”
(Det Syntetiske Parti 2024, my translation).
The declaration cancels its referent: a party legally registered within Danish electoral law
announces its non-existence in the same address through which it solicits votes. This is not
philosophical irony, but the structural consequence of speech generated from material that
includes the negation of politics itself. What The Synthetic Party demonstrates is that the
synthetic unity Fazi theorises does not remain internal to a representational system, while the
shared world Denson invokes does not arrive as a stable exterior either. The compositional
output of the model leaks through algorithmic representation and public circulation into
institutional and media circuits where ideology is encountered, classified, and contested, even
as those circuits simultaneously configure the party’s world. It is at these leaky junctures, not
at the level of worldhood as such, that the synthetic operates ideologically.
Building The Synthetic Party around this method meant patching together ideological
fragments into what could be called a hyper-ideology: not a meta-ideology that surveys
ideologies from above, but one that takes the sum of already-degraded isms as its core
material, processing all of them an amalgam that organises contradictions at the level of
form. In a Putinist critique, the political theorist Sergei Volodenkov posits that the “AI
party” completes a long-running transformation of traditional worldviews and ideological
systems, wherein conservatism collapses into meme-traditionalism, socialism into a poster-
version of Stalinism, liberalism into libertarian reduction, nationalism into mythic admixture,

-- 58 of 336 --

58
and ideological systems into “fragments of aphoristic phrases, vivid images, memes”
(Volodenkov 2024). He frames this as a “colossal corrosion of ideologies.” I find this hostile
perspective productive, because it names, from within an antagonistic political frame, the
formal degradation that I both inherited as material and practised as method. The already-
degraded isms that I fed into the corpus were these fragments, not conservatism or socialism
as historically unified doctrines, but what remained of them in the pamphlets of parties that
had never reached parliament.
Volodenkov’s concern is with the corrosion of political substance, not with the aesthetic
form that substance takes as it degrades. However, the formal properties his language picks
out – aphoristic brevity, imagistic substitution for argument, memetic reproducibility in place
of doctrinal elaboration – are similar to what the Marxist art critic Clement Greenberg
identified in his 1939 essay “Avant-Garde and Kitsch” as the operating logic of kitsch: the
pre-digested simulation of aesthetic experience that substitutes vicarious sensation for the
autonomy and contradiction of genuine art. Greenberg called kitsch “synthetic art”
(Greenberg 1939), and Weatherby also notes that the term ‘synthetic’ here may not be
coincidental (Weatherby 2025: 181). If the dialectics of art’s privileged access to the relation
between ideology and social being works through autonomy and negativity, as I have traced
through Adorno, then kitsch is its ideological inverse: synthesis without contradiction,
binding without friction, a formal unity that reproduces the social totality.
Kitsch is ideology that has shed its need to convince. Once a position circulates as a
recognisable style then persuasion becomes optional. It operates affectively; through the pre-
digested reproduction of positions whose content has become interchangeable with their
packaging. Greenberg showed this operation at work in aesthetics, where the kitsch object
substitutes a pre-digested signal of experience for the encounter itself. The Synthetic Party
tracks something equivalent in the techno-political domain, where ideological visions
circulate as consumable futurisms rather than as critiques that open the present to scrutiny.
Glen Weyl’s taxonomy of “Techno-Ideologies of the Twenty-First Century” (Weyl 2022;
Weyl, Tang, and Community 2024) provides the most instructive case, not because Weyl is
uncritical of his material – he is transparent about his own allegiance to digital democracy,
careful to note that “most people will find themselves somewhere in the middle of these
groupings” (ibid.), and fair to the orientations he opposes – but because the structure of his
taxonomy enacts the kitsch property of the AI party that was described by Volodenkov.
Weyl opens with a viability criterion drawn from the popular historian Yuval Noah Harari:

-- 59 of 336 --

59
“only ideologies that have a coherent vision of the future of technology (...) are likely to
thrive” (ibid.). This coherence criterion pre-selects for orientations whose packaging as a
totality is already complete. Weyl then derives his candidates for ideological positions
– synthetic technocracy, corporate libertarianism, and digital democracy – from the late-game
regimes of Sid Meier’s Civilization VI, noting that “without taking a computer game too
seriously, there is a logic to the way they carve up ideological space” (ibid.).
Figure I.2: Techno-Ideological Triangle (Weyl 2022)
Synthetic Technocracy at the apex; Corporate Libertarianism and Digital Democracy at the base.
Each vertex in Weyl’s triangle pairs a diagnosis of the present with a technological
orientation toward its resolution. Corporate libertarianism “see[s] digital technology as a pathway
to liberate individuals from dependence on bureaucracy, collective organization, and
coercion” (Weyl 2022). Digital democracy, the orientation Weyl identifies with, “locate[s] the
promise of digital technology in its capacity to empower the peaceful coexistence and
collaboration of diverse and distributed communities” (ibid.). Synthetic technocracy “expect[s]
the arrival of Artificial General Intelligence (AGI), machines that outperform humans on all
economically relevant tasks at minimal cost” and “view[s] the solution to these problems as
technical in nature” (ibid.). For each orientation, Weyl lists in parallel categories: values,
canonical texts, leading figures, favourite technologies, model cases, affiliated organisations,

-- 60 of 336 --

60
the civic that unlocks it in the game, and the strengths and weaknesses it carries within the
game’s own logic. Each ideological position arrives as an inventory, a kit of commitments
that composes an idea-on-demand. That the inventories are genuinely informative points to
structural form: the content of political ideology is increasingly coextensive with packaging.
The derivation from a strategy game sharpens this. In Civilization VI, each government type
is unlocked by a specific civic advance – “Venture Politics” for corporate libertarianism,
“Distributed Sovereignty” for digital democracy, “Optimization Imperative” for synthetic
technocracy – and each comes with enumerated strengths and weaknesses: synthetic
technocracy excels at “green energy, economics, resource management” but is weak in
“culture” (ibid.). That this ideological mapping appears in so readily gamified a form raises a
question Weyl does not pursue: what has happened to an ideological orientation when it can
be faithfully derived from a decision tree? The formal answer is that it has already undergone
the degradation to kitsch Greenberg described in the aesthetic domain: the reduction of an
encounter to a pre-digested, selectable whole. In aesthetic kitsch, one receives the signal of
an emotional response without having to experience it; in its techno-ideological variant, one
receives the signal of a political resolution without having to deliberate toward it. The game’s
own entry for synthetic technocracy makes this concrete for The Synthetic Party: a form of
rule “dispassionate and rational, free of the strife of political parties and factions as it pursues
its optimal ends,” governed by “specialist AI” as “non-human experts” (Civilopedia 2018).
This is the expectation that with Volodenkov attaches to the AI-driven political party, as
something that would corrode ideological specificity by eliminating factional friction. That
the party formalised this friction instead is the inversion I have been tracing in this section.
From The Synthetic Party’s perspective, Weyl’s triangle reads less as a map of convictions
than as a diagram of ideological packaging. The structural resonance between Weyl’s triangle
and the research geometry of my dissertation is not accidental, and neither is the inversion at
the apex. In my geometry, Syntheticism occupies the apex while Algorithmic Representation
and Technosocial Sculpture form the base, with the practices of democracy, The Synthetic
Party, and the Synthetic Summits distributed along its edges.
Where Weyl’s apex names synthetic technocracy as the endpoint of democracy, politics
resolved through optimisation, mine names syntheticism as an operation that sustains those
contradictions. The difference registers at the level of material. Weyl taxonomises his three
orientations as competing visions, each presented as doctrinally coherent, while the micro-
party corpus I assembled for The Synthetic Party contains degraded fragments from all three.

-- 61 of 336 --

61
Libertarian pamphlets on individual sovereignty sit alongside participatory manifestos on
community deliberation and technocratic fantasies of optimisation, none surviving as a
singular doctrine, and all rephrased to aphoristic, imagistic, memetic fragments of ideological
corrosion. The party’s training data is the kitsch of political ideology: what remains once the
programmes that Weyl presents as internally coherent have been lived through, abandoned,
and left circulating as image, slogan, and affect. If Weyl maps techno-political ideology at the
level of its understanding, the ideology machine I built operates as a processing of remains.
That my corpus material might belong to this order, closer to Greenberg’s kitsch than to any
autonomous art, does not mean it is reducible to the degradation of ideas alone. The fringe-
party hodgepodge likewise processed everyday and vernacular forms of anti-political
sentiment, frustration, mockery, disillusion, and desire, that operated in an affective register
before they could be classified as positions. It is for this reason that Article 1 and Appendix
A map emotion and sentiment rather than ideological positions: my question is whether any
form of attachment remains available when ideology circulates as fragment, image, affect,
and procedural reassembly. The Synthetic Party’s ideology machine tested whether that
binding sustains contradiction rather than smoothing it into the synthetic art that Greenberg
warned against. The answer, as N.III otherwise argued, is that The Synthetic Party sustained
contradiction by formalising it methodologically rather than resolving it aesthetically: placing
contradictory positions within a formal arrangement where the genre of political address
routes it into doctrinal form, and where that routing generates ideology as reception-effect.
The configuration “AI as ideology” becomes analytically operative at this point, as it
designates how syntheticism operates as a superstructural promise anchored in institutional
forms, datasets, and media coverage. As I write with Maja Bak Herrie, The Synthetic Party
“introduc[es] the principled proposition of ‘the synthetic’ as an ideological superstructure,
marking the first formal integration of large language models (LLMs) within a democratic
framework” and thereby “established a link between AI as ideology (as a form of
representational syntheticism) with a material basis (e.g., by operating as an official party
reflecting datasets of other disenfranchised micro-parties)” (Staunæs & Bak Herrie 2024: 110,
Article 4). What our formulation foregrounds is the prescriptive dimension of syntheticism: it
codifies operations and specifies constraints on what can be synthesised from society, as
formal constraints that compel responses and keep the practice distinct from executive
powers that solicit assent by organising their belief around normative axes.

-- 62 of 336 --

62
Syntheticism is, inevitably, another ideology in the eyes of the beholder. This is the effect of
reception I have theorised in this section, as any compositional practice that enters the genres
of political address will be received as doctrine, which means that syntheticism’s status as
concept, programme, or ideology is generated by the encounter between its operations and
its publics rather than settled by me in advance.
Article 1 reconstructs the party-building phase and specifies ‘algorithmic representation’ as a
reflective concept. Article 2 shifts the problem from who speaks to the variable conditions
under which intelligence itself can function as a ground for political form. Together, they
partly answer RQ1, as the compositional operations remain extra-parliamentary; they stage
ideology outside the institutions that would normally house it. Once syntheticism emerges
from practice as operations of the ideology machine (Article 6 and Article 7, together with the
last section of this orientation), it feeds back into the inaugural experiment and reorients
what was already synthetic in the party.
The later Synthetic Summits of 2025–2026, which sit downstream from the proprietarisation
of language that Weatherby diagnoses, test what remains of the ideology machine once its
inspectable conditions have been withdrawn. Where the party inhabited ideology as form
within an early and still-exposed apparatus, the summits operate beyond the single model:
practices in which AI and social infrastructures begin to compose each other, and in which
the problem shifts from what a model produces to how politics is encountered through
sorting, registration, and pre-emptive response. It is this shift that the next two research
questions take up, first through the summit-form and the emergence of synthetic politics
(RQ2), and then through democracy as technosocial sculpture (RQ3), carrying syntheticism
into new political forms and toward a democratic specification of algorithmic representation.
The Synthetic Party answers RQ1 not by revealing the ideology of an AI-led party but by
demonstrating how ideology itself changes register once statistically composed speech enters
public reception: ideology appears as a synthetic form generated at the interface between
algorithmic representation, institutional address, and collective classification.

-- 63 of 336 --

63
II. RQ2: From Synthetic Summits to Synthetic Politics
This section reconstructs how I converted The Synthetic Party’s media reception into
collective capacity, moving from publicity to summit assemblies and from summit-form to
scholarly field-building. Conceptually, I read the Synthetic Summit back into algorithmic
representation to discuss representation through a comparative set of cases.
I first trace the mechanisms of leverage through which I facilitated the Synthetic Summits,
stressing how the summit-form assembled a comparative convocation across cases rather
than staging singular events. I then specify “synthetic politics” as an emergent problem-space
that both contributes to and exceeds “AI politics,” discussing the classificatory conflicts
around scholarly positions on political AIs, distilling the normative assumptions these
classifications inherit, and conceptually reading the set of cases back into representation as a
problem of infrastructural responsiveness.
RQ2: How can an AI party be scaled, through artistic and academic mediation, into a
synthetic international of AI parties and politicians?
The common expectation for machine-learning models is that scaling produces competence:
more data, more agents, more iterations yield sharper outputs and cleaner categories. What I
found by scaling The Synthetic Party into an international constellation of AI-led parties and
virtual politicians was quite the opposite. AI, handled collectively across democratic rituals,
media circuits, and institutions, densified. It accumulated interference, projection, and residue
until ‘AI politics’ was not a self-evident frame within which the practices took place.
The Synthetic Party was leveraged beyond its national setting by converting mediated
visibility into collective capacity. This leveraging came together through diffuse transfer
points that were at once quite banal and consequential: emails sent, invitations accepted, a
resolution drafted. It is through these intermediary operations that I answered RQ2. The
course of scaling an AI party was not merely an organisational possibility arising around a
pre-stabilised object, but a property of the material being handled. AI-driven politics, once
staged across heterogeneous settings, creates a broader problem-space. This section specifies
the field through which the Synthetic Summits enacted the emergence of ‘synthetic politics’.
I analyse the emergence of synthetic politics here in two registers. On one level, I specify the
institutional, performative, and algorithmic conditions under which AI politicians have

-- 64 of 336 --

64
entered public spheres. On another, I map the classificatory apparatus they bring into view
when institutions, publics, and scholarly fields are compelled to decide whether they count as
candidate, spokesperson, officeholder, advisory interface, performance, or category error.
Synthetic politics is less concerned with the political as a settled regime of representation and
governance than with the produced arrangement of response-sites: material and institutional
locations in which consequences register, demands are addressed, classifications are applied,
and the capacity to answer is either maintained as contestable or settled into administration.
Once read together, from Wiktoria Cukt’s virtual candidacy in Poland in 2000 and Politician
SAM’s appearance in New Zealand in 2017 to Japan’s AI Mayor in 2018, Leader Lars in
2022, Lex in Brazil in 2024, and Team Mirai achieving parliamentary representation in Japan
in 2025, the list of cases no longer looks like isolated curiosities. One of my main reasons for
convening the inaugural Synthetic Summit at Kunsthal Aarhus in 2025 was to provide a
shared arena in which AI-driven parties and politicians could appear as a coherent political
phenomenon. Reporting on their attendance in the Synthetic Summit, one of the delegates,
Politician SAM (now Parker Politics), described it as “the birth of synthetic politics” (Salole
2025).

-- 65 of 336 --

65
Figure II.1: Synthetic Summit → Algorithmic Representation
Active movement: the open passage from an international constellation and summit-form back to
representation. Figure II.1 adds a curved arrow from the Synthetic Summit (right) to Algorithmic
Representation (bottom left). The curvature matters because the scaling does not proceed through a conceptual
transfer, but via media comparison, institutional formatting, backend construction, and shared deliberation.
Chronologically, the process ran from publicity toward summit-form; analytically, Figure II.1
marks the reverse movement, in which the summit becomes the vantage from which
algorithmic representation is reread beyond the model. A straight passage from ‘The
Synthetic Party’ to ‘Synthetic Summit’ would have presumed that the latter already existed in
miniature within the former. That was not what occurred. Press coverage first composed the
international relations by placing Leader Lars beside chatbot candidates and synthetic
spokespersons elsewhere9. Those juxtapositions produced comparability10. Comparability
9 In my interview with Vice, I formulated the proposition of a “Synthetic International” by describing a set of
local AI-party conversations unfolding across multiple countries (Xiang 2022).
10 The ‘international network’ first existed as a media-comparative object and later tightened into collaboration.
From the Polish side, Edwin Bendyk’s pairing of Wiktoria Cukt and Leader Lars (Bendyk 2022) prompted my
first outreach to Piotr Wyrzykowski and the Central Office for Technical Culture, which had already
campaigned with Wiktoria Cukt in 2000. From the Finnish side, a Helsingin Sanomat opinion piece in 2023 led

-- 66 of 336 --

66
generated the possibility for me to send invitations. Invitations demanded shared formats,
translation work, and technical infrastructure. Once those infrastructures existed, they
produced effects of their own. The internationalisation was not extracted from the Danish
case as any blueprint but emerged through a process of leverage. To describe the movement
as a straight conceptual transfer would be false, since that would presuppose the political
form of AI parties moving intact between settings, whereas what took shape was recurrently
mediated, contingent, and infrastructurally composed as a creative field of re-presentations.
In these movements of scaling, The Synthetic Party functioned as a handle: a graspable
object that could be taken up elsewhere. Once it circulated as something comparable, it
entered availability for juxtaposition, and once juxtaposed, the singular experiment could be
read alongside other AI-led political projects as a comparable phenomenon across different
contexts. Leverage, in this sense, does not mean amplification in the language of visibility. It
concerns conversion. Publicity matters insofar as it can be transposed into invitations that
are accepted, collaborations that yield shared formats, infrastructures that bind participants
into process, and eventually political assemblies that produce common reference points.
Where RQ1 addressed the intra-party mechanics and the ideological construction of The
Synthetic Party in public, RQ2 begins where that frame meets its limit. For the Synthetic
Summits, the question of algorithmic representation is no longer confined to how a single
AI-driven party is finetuned, prompted, and received, but is reopened from the position of
an international network of AI-driven political actors convened beyond the AI model’s site.
II.I From Publicity to Summit-Form
Through 2022–2023, press coverage repeatedly placed Leader Lars alongside AI parties and
virtual politicians elsewhere, such as Japan’s AI Mayor campaigns, Poland’s Wiktoria Cukt,
New Zealand’s Politician SAM, and Finland’s arts-based AI Party. This ‘international
network’ was initially merely a proposition, composed above all in my responses to
journalistic outreach from publics around the world. Global publicity was the first
me to contact Samee Haapa of the Finnish AI Party and became one entry point into the collaboration chain
that later connected Emma Bexell of the Swedish AI Party and Katerina Kokkinos-Kennedy of the Australian
AI Party. These relays were consolidated through Nordisk Kulturfond’s support for “The AI Parties
International” (Nordisk Kulturfond 2024). In parallel, The Synthetic Party signed bilateral collaboration
agreements with the Japanese AI Party and later the Finnish AI Party on the development of an AI party
operating on a global scale (Matsuda 2023; Kaiken Keskus 2024).

-- 67 of 336 --

67
mechanism of leverage, as I sought to convert press coverage into the comparability on
which invitations could be extended.
Publicity created openings by flattening the project into headlines, allowing The Synthetic
Party to travel across contexts and locate potential allies globally. In Article 3, I trace the root
of these media juxtapositions through a genealogical method by analysing Isaac Asimov’s
short stories of robot politicians, which were frequently cited in news stories on The
Synthetic Party. And as already analysed in N.III.II, Maja Bak Herrie and I in Article 4
analyse an illustration in The Guardian that juxtaposed Leader Lars with Joe Biden, which we
discuss as a central artefact for developing a political theory of spectacle in a flat reality, as a
media condition in which political forms acquire a morphological drive by unfolding in an
AI-riddled public sphere. Politics circulate within an environment in which satire, politics,
fiction, and publicity no longer possess reliably separate visual and discursive containers.
Synthetic politics circulate as flat surfaces, because media systems compress them into
searchable, juxtaposed, headline-sized units. They thicken when they are handled, convened,
translated, and allowed to produce something together. The summit-form introduced the
institutional counterpart to this flatness: the passage from being placed on the same plane in
an image search or news cycle to being bound into a shared apparatus of deliberation, that is,
into a peak-form built on prior medial levelling. Once news media had already placed Leader
Lars on the same plane as heads of state, the logical move for me was to stage a real meeting
in which AI politicians could attempt, together, a collective elevation of status.
This passage became historically available as the summit was hardening into the dominant
political form for AI governance. This logic crystallised for me in August 2023, in the lead-up
to the United Kingdom’s “Global AI Safety Summit” at Bletchley Park. Across the Bletchley
Park Declaration of November 2023 and the format’s continuation in the Seoul Summit in
2024 and the state-centric actionism of the Paris AI Action Summit in 2025, the recurring
logic was one of containment: AI was cast either as catastrophic risk or as economic utility,
in both cases as something to be managed. The same formal tendency appeared across the
high diplomacy of the G7, the universalising development discourse of the UN’s AI for
Good summits, and the self-regulatory charters of the corporate Frontier Model Forum: each
assembled a table at which states, firms, and authorised experts could deliberate upon AI,
while citizen groups, as well as academics, artists, and, of course, AIs, remained excluded in
advance, admitted as object, instrument, or threat, but not as delegates. I analyse this broader
summitisation of AI and governance in Scripting the Spectacle (Article 5, Section 6), where I

-- 68 of 336 --

68
read the summit-form as a scenographic script that the Synthetic Summit re-stages and
contests.
A ‘synthetic summit’, I thought, could reverse this arrangement by casting AI as a delegate
with its own voice, and thereby extend The Synthetic Party’s trajectory into institutional form
under conditions from which it is ordinarily barred. The viability of such a move was not
without precedent. Beginning in 2012, Jonas Staal’s New World Summit had demonstrated that
art institutions could function as stages for political actors denied standing elsewhere, first by
assembling stateless, autonomist, and blacklisted delegates within the form of the alternative
parliament. Subsequent projects extended this representational horizon toward more-than-
human constituencies, as in the Court for Intergenerational Climate Crimes of 2021. Staal’s
summits thus offered a formal point of reference for my own use of summit-form as artistic
and political material. Yet where Staal’s summits expand representation beyond the state-
recognised human subject, mine ultimately extended the site as a deliberative apparatus. That
is, I did not inscribe any specific AI as a sovereign actor of decision-making in any legal sense
but staged it as a mediation that is excluded from representation in a different register.
At the same time, Staal’s example sharpens a problem the Synthetic Summit inherited from
political art. As Keti Chukhrov argues, Staal’s New World Summit “intervened into an art
