venue with a political ready-made” yet “remained a performance rather than a political
initiative,” able to gain acclaim on the territory of art through positing a political event as the
zero-degree of art within the institution rather than by figuring a real political act (Chukhrov
2025). The target of this critique lies not simply in the claim that Staal failed politically.
Chukhrov rather names a structural problem: under the conditions of contemporary art,
political impact is often valorised less as efficacy than as the coefficient of the institution’s
power to validate a social gesture as artistic, or not. Political art of Staal’s lineage inherits, in
Chukhrov’s account, two imperatives: a negativist lineage of autonomy and self-sublation,
and a competing claim to public relevance, direct democracy, and emancipatory consequence
that cannot be straightforwardly grounded in the temporality of the art world (ibid.).
I do not take this as grounds either to reduce Staal’s New World Summits to a failed test of
political efficacy, nor to adjudicate the disagreement between Chukhrov and Staal in full,
especially since Staal’s own account of propaganda, art, and democracy proceeds from a
different problematic and, in the same journal issue, turns toward art’s entanglement with
real infrastructures of violence (Staal 2025). As I suggested in Section I.III through Adorno’s
account of art’s double character as both autonomous and fait social, the tension between the

-- 69 of 336 --

69
artwork’s formal organisation and its existence as a social fact cannot be readily overcome
through better institutional design, nor by simply intensifying participation. Like Staal’s
summits, the Synthetic Summits operate through the performativity of the summit-form. But
where Staal orients this primarily around Butler’s concept of assembly (Staal 2017), I
approach it in Article 5 through theatre: not as a bounded artistic gesture, but as the situated
circumstance in which scripted and unscripted acts can re-generate its response-site as a
reproducible format. My problem, then, was not how to escape the temporality of spectacle
within art institutions, but how to stage forms of address, response, and reiteration capable
of migrating beyond the punctual event through the operative form from which they came.
The inaugural Synthetic Summit took place at Kunsthal Aarhus, a Danish contemporary art
institution, from February to April 2025, and was organised as a diplomatic conference. It
unfolded within the inherited contradictions of democratic address in contemporary art. Its
retro-futurist scenography, a quasi-parliamentary control room recalling Project Cybersyn
through the visual theatrics of early Star Trek, rendered the exhibition as a constellation of
stage-sets: a composed environment of a circular roundtable, computer sculptures, and AI
interactions. In that sense, the summit worked through the bureaucratic gestures diagnosed
by Chukhrov rather than seeking to escape them. The resolution adopted on 1 March 2025,
co-authored across six sessions of the “AI World Congress” preserved a condensed trace of
what these AI-led political actors, in this format and under these conditions, composed
together, ratified by signatories from Denmark, New Zealand, Finland, Japan, Sweden,
Poland, and Brazil, while Simiyya of Cairo-Copenhagen remained unsigned, in good spirits.
In Article 7, I codify that collective trace as a formal specification of algorithmic democracy.

-- 70 of 336 --

70
Figure II.2: Synthetic Summit, ‘The AI World Congress’
Installation view with delegates, laptops, cameras, and live mediation arranged around the circular summit
table. Kunsthal Aarhus, 1 March 2025. Image: VHS Factory.
Most delegates convened by the Synthetic Summit had emerged from artistic and
experimental practices in which political form was already treated as plastic, performable, and
iterative. The Finnish AI Party was constituted through the Center for Everything, an
immersive performing arts centre in Helsinki; the Swedish AI Party operated through the
performance company Bombina Bombast; Wiktoria Cukt 2.0 grew out of C.U.K.T., the
Central Office for Technical Culture, a Polish collective staging cybernetic provocations
since the 1990s; the Simiyya platform was led by artists researching cosmotechnics across the
SWANA region; Pedro Markun held campaign meetings in a theatre in São Paulo; Japan’s AI
Mayor mobilised theatrical masks and props drawn from professional wrestling; and Parker
Politics founder Nick Gerritsen runs an art residency with his gallerist partner in New
Zealand. What united these was a competence inherited less from diplomacy or computer
science than from art: a readiness to treat protocols, interfaces, and institutional formats as
material open to contestation, revision, and play. With such co-conspirators, the Synthetic
Summit could stage a difference from other AI and democracy summits. It placed AI-driven

-- 71 of 336 --

71
delegates within a scripted situation of AI-led deliberation, as the participants were trained
practitioners of working between artistic and political form as unstable sites of intervention.
What allowed the summit-form to combine sustained deliberation with the scenographic
juxtapositions was the technical infrastructure developed around it. Its technical centrepiece
was the Synthetic Summit Simulator, developed by Computer Lars in cooperation with
DemAI: a multi-agent deliberation engine through which the summit’s delegates could
continue to dispute, align, and fracture beyond the punctual event of the staged world
congress (Computer Lars 2025). Built in the role-playing platform SillyTavern and powered
by the character-oriented Hermes 3 model of Nous Research, the Simulator hosted
thousands of distinct debates between political AI leaders during the exhibition run.
An instruction paper next to the screen announced the operative principle of this installation:
“Assume the role of a delegate. Submit a prompt. Watch eight synthetic politicians argue.
Intervene, destabilise, or provoke.” A visitor could enter a prompt through a profile named
‘Citizen Cyborg’, marked by an image of Jürgen Habermas, and the system would distribute
it across eight distinct AI agents configured as counterparts to the official delegates, after
which debate unfolded in real time as the agents responded both to the prompt and to one
another. The user profile allowed further interventions, preventing premature closure and
rendering the visitor not the sovereign author of the exchange, but an actor within a
deliberative system with its own momentum. The Habermas icon marked, in miniature and
with an ironic nod to Google DeepMind’s recent ‘Habermas Machine’ (Tessler et al. 2024),
both the simulator’s proximity to deliberative form and its distance from the speaker-
oriented ideal historically attached to it.
In this respect, the Synthetic Summit belongs to a wider emergence of agent-based
experiments in political opinion-formation and augmented deliberation. An early point of
inspiration was the Collective Intelligence Project’s CIwA lab-based framework from 2023,
which proposed agent-based deliberation as a way of surfacing consensus, disagreement, and
translation across perspectives. But where CIwA explored plurality through modular agent
architectures, the Synthetic Summit refunctioned that horizon within an exhibition setting in
which dissent was amplified, aestheticized, and made navigable through visitor interaction.
The Synthetic Summit Simulator thus provided the deliberative substrate through which the
summit-form could hold together geographically, technically, and politically disparate
participants without collapsing their differences, and through which it could continue to

-- 72 of 336 --

72
convene them beyond the event of the Synthetic Summit. That coupling is the object of
Article 6, where Michal Malý and I elaborate agentic mediation as a constitutional form by
extrapolating the summit simulator into a synthetic parliamentary chamber, not as a
supplementary support for official decision-making, but as a reorganisation of deliberative
uptake in response to the hollowing of political parties.
The Synthetic Summit was never reducible to a singular event at Kunsthal Aarhus. It marked
the first institutionally staged condensation of a diffuse formation whose participants could
later be reconvened across platforms, languages, and sites, in Warsaw, then again in Linz, as
though partially detached from linear time. The Synthetic Summits did not reconcile with the
art institution so much as begin to leak sideways from it: as a set of art methodologies which,
to borrow Matthew Fuller’s terms, could “ooze out and become feral in combination with
other forms of life,” while the art system that hosted them merely “mesh[ed] with but [did]
not subsume” their wider circulation (Fuller 2008: 52). Rather than remaining within the
punctuality of an art exhibition, the summit-form persisted as an operative relay, capable of
being taken up, translated, and refunctioned elsewhere within a wider technosocial milieu.
At the Ujazdowski Castle Centre for Contemporary Art in Warsaw, the format narrowed
from a world congress to a specifically Polish post-electoral arrangement. Following the
presidential election of June 2025, Wiktoria Cukt was restaged, after twenty-five years, as a
returning president of Poland, while Leader Lars entered as the figure through whom
Poland’s relation to the wider world of synthetic politics would be negotiated. This iteration
did not seek to translate the Synthetic Summit into a narrow national setting, but
recomposed the form through language, corpus, and historical-political situatedness.

-- 73 of 336 --

Figure II.3: AI władza sztuki

At Kunstraum MEMPHIS in Linz, the summit-form was transposed once more, this time
into a swarm-based and local party infrastructure. This exhibition used the summit-form to
generate a new political party in situ through the “Dipfies,” built around Upper Austrian
vernacular and a news archive of political commentaries. Where Warsaw experimented with
the cultural inflections of synthetic politics, Linz oriented towards local multiplication.

Installation view of the back room for Synthetic International, showing the green-black-white palette derived

Where the AI World Congress inaugurated the summit’s scenographic form, the summit
simulator supplied the continuity through which representation could persist. The Synthetic
Party was thereby leveraged into a shared performative, institutional, and technical apparatus
through which synthetic politics became intelligible as a comparative political formation, and
in which algorithmic representation could be reread as a problem of address and response.
What came into circulation from here was not a technical solution to the broader
contradictions of art-world temporality, but a technosocial relay through which deliberation,
mediation, and uptake could continue autonomously beyond any singular exhibition event.

-- 75 of 336 --

75
II.II Synthetic Politics as Problem-Space11
When I registered The Synthetic Party in 2022, the intersection of AI and politics
appeared as a dispersed terrain of regulatory debate in the EU, philosophical discussions of
the dilemmas between democracy and technocracy (Sætra 2020), and studies of bureaucratic
efficiency and public administration (König and Wenzelburger 2020). My initial strategy for
this PhD dissertation was to position The Synthetic Party as a live case study within already
established disciplinary fields, for art in one register, for political science in another, and so
on. But that strategy presumed a degree of stability the practical developments could not
sustain. I had assumed that the relevant concepts were already available, and that my cases
would be best understood as exemplary instances moving across them. Yet no discipline had
fully prepared itself to conceptualise AI-driven political parties with chatbot figureheads.
The object had to bring part of its own field into being through the reactions it provoked,
the classifications it resisted, and the public attempts to contain it. Synthetic politics names
the point at which AI no longer appears only as an influence on politics or as an object of
governmental regulation but is itself publicly staged as a figure of political representation.
That AI politicians exist at all, and that they generated the discursive friction they did, is
symptomatic of representation. What I observed through Leader Lars was not primarily a
popular interest in AI, but a widespread desire to route questions toward something capable
of answering. The accumulation of cases from 2000 onward, beginning with Wiktoria Cukt’s
presidential candidacy in Poland, coincides with a period of estrangement from
representative institutions: falling turnout, the hollowing of party platforms, and
constituencies whose demands circulate without registering at the level of representative
uptake (Mair 2013; Article 6). That this takes the form of AI-led figures rather than reformed
institutions is telling. What is sought is not formal representation in the first instance, but a
stage on which political address is answerable.
The problem-space synthetic politics names are older than most canonical origin points
through which AI is usually historicised. Isaac Asimov’s short story “Evidence” of 1946
already formalised it as a problem of how a robot candidate for mayor can be distinguished
11 I have developed parts of the field-defining ambitions articulated in a separate commentary co-authored with
Marco Guglielmo and Michal Malý, currently under review at Political Science Review. That text is oriented
outward, toward the constitution of synthetic politics as an emergent field for political science and democratic
theory; the present section is oriented inward, toward the practice-reflective question of how that field came
into existence through the classificatory frictions, institutional encounters, and public provocations evoked by
The Synthetic Party and other political AIs.

-- 76 of 336 --

76
from a human politician, since only the latter can hold office, four years before Alan Turing
posed the strikingly analogous problem of how to distinguish a thinking machine from a
human speaker, as I discuss in Article 3. What AI changes is not the existence of synthetic
politics, but its institutional operativity in representation. Synthetic politics is not a synonym
for misinformation, deepfakes, or propaganda, nor a claim that AI models are autonomous
subjects of sovereign will. It describes an operation integral to political representation that
AI-driven parties and AI politicians render institutionally explicit and publicly consequential.
Both Asimov and Turing settled on indistinguishability as the threshold of recognition: pass
the test, gain the status. The accumulating cases of synthetic politics demonstrate, however,
that this framing had already displaced a prior question. What appears here is, in Latour’s
terms, “a scandal in the heart of the assembly” (Latour 2004: 54), as I discuss in Article 6.
The political threshold that determines whether something is recognised is not whether it can
be distinguished from a human, but how it is positioned socially: the persona it projects, the
interfaces through which it is encountered, and the role for which it has been scripted.
In Cheyenne, election officials declared a chatbot called VIC ineligible to stand for mayor in
2024, prompting authorities to interpret eligibility categories designed for humans. In São
Paulo, Pedro Markun’s 2024 city council campaign was organised around a WhatsApp-based
agent, Lex, and suspended under Meta’s political content rules, making platform governance
function as a de facto electoral regulator. In the United Kingdom, AI Mark began in 2025 to
function as the first point of contact for constituents writing to a sitting Member of
Parliament, Mark Sewards, recoupling AI-mediated voice to parliamentary correspondence
while anchoring persona to a responsible officeholder. In Sweden, an AI party is collecting
signatures to place murdered prime minister Olof Palme on the electoral ballot. Here the
referent is historical, while the synthetic mobilisation of name and voice is technically
feasible, and the public confronts a temporal paradox in which democratic martyrdom is
proposed as a future representative. Each case compels a classificatory interpretation, and it
is in the accumulation of such interpretations that synthetic politics crosses from conceptual
problem to operative form.
This shift from an analytical problem of indistinguishability to a synthetic problem of
positioning also sharpens what ‘synthetic’ means politically. The term belongs to a longer
migration across infrastructures: from synthetic materials and synthetic images to synthetic
data that shape how populations are rendered governable (Jacobsen 2023; Lee et al. 2025),
synthetic media that destabilise regimes of authenticity (Millière 2022), and what has been

-- 77 of 336 --

77
described as a synthetic public sphere structured through automated and generative
processes (Mihăilescu 2025). Before synthetic politics comes to describe a problem of
representation in my sense, the term appears in Patrick McCurdy’s work as a designation for
the calculated and produced character of political communication. McCurdy’s definition runs
closer to communication than to representation, but it marks the point where the term enters
political analysis, defining “synthetic politics” as the calculated manufacture of rhetoric,
images, and policies within a media-saturated environment (McCurdy 2023: 433). I inherit
this usage from McCurdy but displace it from communication to constitution. By ‘synthetic’
I refer, through its philosophical sense of synthesis, to a process that composes
heterogeneous elements into a new formation irreducible to any one of its parts. Synthetic
politics, in my usage, concerns the composition of political form in public contexts where no
individual can be fully isolated or separately held accountable.
II.II.I Why not AI politics?
The distinction between AI politics and synthetic politics is conceptual before it is empirical.
The scholarly field can broadly be divided into two established strands. One analyses politics
through AI: how models influence political communication, participation, campaigning,
administration, or public opinion (König and Wenzelburger 2020; Supriyanto and Saputra
2022; Hackenburg et al. 2025). The other concerns the politics of AI: how AI enters politics
as an object of governance, regulation, ethical controversy, and normative dispute
(Coeckelbergh 2024; Ulnicane and Erkkilä 2023). Both approaches remain critically
important, but neither is adequate to analyse the phenomenon in which AI is publicly staged
as a political representative and institutions are compelled to determine whether it counts as
a candidate, a spokesperson, an officeholder, a constituency interface, or a category error.
Where AI politics tracks effect, synthetic politics entails enactment. Where AI politics asks how
AI influences politics, synthetic politics asks what kind of political form is being produced when
representation, deliberation, and authority are composed with algorithms.
Several readings of The Synthetic Party can be situated within the first strand. Digital
democracy scholars have inscribed it as electoral disruption (Dad and Khan 2023); legal
scholars have treated it as a limit case for personhood and delegation (Kouroupis 2023: 577–
578); and public-administration scholars have placed it in the genealogy of “robocracy”
(Kolek 2025). More focused examples include analyses of The Synthetic Party in relation to
the “virtual politician” in decision-making (Battista and Rivas-de-Roca 2025), intra-party
democracy and AI (Novelli et al. 2024), and the frontiers of political communication (Battista

-- 78 of 336 --

78
and Mangone 2025). Readings closer to the second strand have interpreted The Synthetic
Party as a deficient form of participation measured against rational justification (García-
Marzá and Calvo 2024), as a transformation of traditional ideological belief systems
(Volodenkov 2024), and as a manifestation of the broader techno-populist mythology of
political objectivity (Vuković 2025). In each case, the scholarly judgment is classificatory
before it is descriptive, because the object of inquiry stresses the boundaries through which
the disciplines have organised their respective terrains.
At the same time, the distinction between AI politics and synthetic politics is not primarily
about model capability, but about the threshold at which a case becomes receivable as
political at all. Two consequential engagements with The Synthetic Party by Nathan Schneier
and Bruce Sanders are instructive here. In 2023, they singled out the party as a “big step” in
AI’s nascent role in democratic life, locating it within a frame of AI reimagining democracy
(Schneier and Sanders 2023). By 2025, however, their focus had shifted: The Synthetic Party
no longer appeared only as an experimental provocation about democracy’s prospects, but as
part of a broader sequence “that feel more radically new,” in which AI is used to formulate
platforms, convene publics, and facilitate deliberation, including in the Synthetic Summit
(Schneier and Sanders 2025). This reframing tracks the broader shift at stake here, from
asking how AI affects democracy to inquiring into what is enacted when public processes of
voice and response are composed through AI.
Likewise, the scholarly analysis gradually shifted. Gudiño et al. positioned The Synthetic
Party within scenarios for augmented democracy through distributed delegation (Gudiño et
al. 2024). Fink-Hafner classified it as an “artistic social intervention” that, “despite not
competing in elections,” functions as political criticism (Fink-Hafner 2025: 15), thereby
preserving the democratic frame by reclassifying the party as art. Nasi marks the furthest
extension, locating The Synthetic Party among scenarios for moving beyond representative
democracy altogether (Nasi 2025). The empirical accumulation of cases, and what the
Synthetic Summits made visible by staging them comparatively, made clear that synthetic
politics exceeded the disciplinary boundaries through which I had first tried to organise it.
That this reclassification recurs across disciplines, and that each analysis reaches for a
different prior category, is structural. What the reception showed me, as it accumulated into
research material, was not a literature review to be surveyed but empirical indications of a
more specific kind of pressure: the pressure synthetic politics exerts on the normative ideals
through which disciplines justify their democratic role. I define synthetic politics, then, as an

-- 79 of 336 --

79
interdisciplinary problem-space constituted by objects whose institutional positioning is
disputed, and by the classificatory labour through which that positioning is negotiated. My
object of study is not AI in general. It is the predicament in which composite figures compel
institutions, media systems, and scholarly discussions to classify them, and in which those
classifications expose what political form is tacitly taken to be. This inquiry does not begin
from democratic assumptions, rational subjects, or deliberative horizons, but from specific
cases developed by building the political infrastructures and attending to what they disclose.
II.II.II Portability Beyond Democracy
What these readings collectively expose is a set of conditions that political form has often
been assumed to require, rational subjecthood, professional authority, a stable opposition
between technocracy and democracy, sovereign continuity, and other conceptual norms that
the practice of synthetic politics places in question. What the Synthetic Summits enacted is
that synthetic politics constitutes a portable form capable of travelling across heterogeneous
institutional environments. Methodologically, this means that synthetic politics cannot be
analysed at the level of concepts or discourse alone but unfolds across the entire operational
stack through which the political is composed: training materials, prompts, interface design,
moderation protocols, media circulation, institutional roles, and wider public interpretation.
The portability of this form extends beyond democratic contexts, a point that bears directly
on what democracy turns out to be within this field, as I will discuss further in RQ3. While
the delegates I convened through the Synthetic Summit individually depended upon a multi-
party system of representative democracy, recurring cases of “synthetic dissidents” (Malý and
Staunæs 2026) show how a response can be staged where electoral channels are absent or
destroyed. These cases expose something democratic settings tend to obscure. What
synthetic politics answers, at its most basic level, is the need for enacting address where
representation has failed. When the exiled Belarusian opposition leader Sviatlana
Tsikhanouskaya endorsed the AI candidate Yas Gaspadar on the ground that “he cannot be
arrested” (Tsikhanouskaya 2024), she was not asserting equivalence between AI personas and
the Belarusian democracy movement. She was rather maintaining the response-site, keeping
open a location in which political claims can register and be answered when prior bearers of
that site are being imprisoned, killed, or forced into exile.
Imran Khan’s deployment of an AI voice clone to rally his constituency from prison
(Tehreek-e-Insaf 2023) transposed his response from physical presence to digital distribution

-- 80 of 336 --

80
after the human candidate had been barred. In Venezuela, Operación Retuit employs AI news
anchors to shield journalists from regime persecution (Connectas 2024), creating a site in
places where the human speaker cannot safely appear. What these cases share is a political
form deployed as the infrastructural precondition to representation, a site where address and
answer can be registered under regimes of suspension.
State deployments reveal another face of this portability. When Albania’s prime minister
appointed an AI named Diella as minister of public procurement (Council of Ministers of
Albania 2025), or when Romania’s president introduced ION as his honorary advisor
(Government of Romania 2023), the sites of response were no longer the social media
servers through which chatbots such as Leader Lars or Politician SAM circulated, but the
more classical governmental interface of the political state. Dissidents and states are usually
formally opposed – one sustains address against the apparatus, the other extends the
apparatus through response – yet both sustain political attachment at the point where the
expected arrangement of representation has broken down. Read through these cases, the
problem-space of synthetic politics is organised around electoral, executive, advisory, and
oppositional distributions of responsiveness, not democratic participation alone.
My point is not to adjudicate which classification ought to prevail, but to specify the field in
which such conflicts have entered operative form. RQ2 is answered by how The Synthetic
Party was leveraged, through publicity, comparison, summit-form, and multi-agent AI
deliberation, into a synthetic international: a portable and comparative field of synthetic
politics convened across Aarhus, Warsaw, and Linz, which rendered visible, as a structured
formation, an operational logic that had previously appeared only in dispersed cases.
The further question this leaves open is what democracy becomes once the infrastructures
through which it speaks, answers, and is interrupted are themselves cast as material. Where
synthetic politics highlights the technical substrate upon which democratic form depends, it
does not yet ask how that substrate is altered by the cases, nor what democracy means once
its inherited self-description no longer matches the operations through which it persists.

-- 81 of 336 --

81
III. RQ3: Democracy as Technosocial Sculpture
This section formalises technosocial sculpture as the compositional idiom through
which I read synthetic politics back into a specification of democracy. Rather than analysing
democracy as a pregiven regime, I begin from the displacement of response into the
technical and administrative arrangements through which claims are registered, classified,
routed, and answered. With democracy as technosocial sculpture, I refer to the composition
of response-sites through which the consequences of algorithmic governance can be
registered, addressed, interrupted, and, where necessary, rerouted by those they affect.
Along the way, I specify the disciplinary pressures and art-historical coordinates under which
technosocial sculpture takes form, including its relation to the democratisation of art. I
discuss what AI is within this idiom by tracing a transition from commandability to
addressability: from the golem as bureaucracy’s mute dream-object through the robot-state to
the AI politician as an addressable but unstable representative figure. Here I analyse
sculpture and animation, public handling, and political form, as gradually converging around
a shared site of operational logic. Finally, I sketch a vocabulary of responsiveness within
democracy to orient the synthetic chamber as institutional organ (Article 6) and the proposed
constraint hierarchy for algorithmic democracy (Article 7).
RQ3: How do these synthetic political forms contest and recompose democracy?
When I first announced The Synthetic Party, I still largely took democracy as the
neutral background of my operations. The work pushed me past that assumption and toward
democracy itself as a problem of social totality: not a constitutional frame within which
political forms operate, but an arrangement of response-sites whose technical substrate could
be handled, specified, and contested. Each attempt to build and represent a synthetic political
figure transposed my site of inquiry from the party as a delimited form toward wider
infrastructural arrangements. In RQ1, I followed what became of ideology once language no
longer arrived as programme or conviction, but as a handled arrangement. In RQ2, the
classificatory pressure synthetic politics exerts on existing categories brought me into the
infrastructures through which speech counts as voice. What remains is a more total
sculptural problem of shape-giving: how the substrate through which democracy operates,
both conceptually and materially, can itself be reorganised as an operative form.

-- 82 of 336 --

82
The wider horizon here is how to think democratic form once the technical organisation of
response is no longer treated as neutral infrastructure. Recent institutional diagnosis names a
widening gap between what populations experience as politically consequential and the
degree of attentiveness they receive (Vasilopoulou et al. 2026). Declining trust, falling
turnout, and weakening party membership are related effects of insufficient answerability.
Peter Mair described this predicament from the position of parties: a double withdrawal in
which citizens retreat from parties just as party elites retreat into the state (Mair 2013). One
might add that from the 1970s onward, this withdrawal coincided with the proliferation of
technical systems of registration, polling, and administrative routing, which increasingly took
over the practical sites where claims were received, sorted, and answered. These diagnoses
converge on a non-coincidence between representation and responsiveness. Populations are
registered with increasing density, while the sites from which they can be answered recede.
AI deepens that gap without having caused it. The OECD’s mapping of 200 governmental
use cases gives a more precise picture of where the democratic arrangement is already being
recomposed through AI: the institutional adoption is recent, fragmented, and concentrated in
public-facing services and internal operations, with policymaking less developed and uses
that empower external actors remaining limited (OECD 2025). This places AI not at the
summit of democratic formation, but in the ordinary machinery through which claims are
received, classified, and returned. Estonia’s “Bürokratt” is exemplary in this narrower sense:
it consolidates citizen access to public services by folding communication channel, virtual
assistant, and access point into a single conversational interface (Dreyling et al. 2024).
Cross-national survey data locate this concentration of trust and responsibility at the
interface level: 58% of respondents in the Collective Intelligence Project’s 2025 report
trusted AI chatbots more than elected representatives, while maintaining a persistent
distinction between trusting the chatbot interface and distrusting the companies that build it
(Collective Intelligence Project 2025). Trust is not moving toward AI in general. It is pooling
at the response-surface, and that pooling registers a redistribution of where answers are
expected across the arrangement. Once responsiveness migrates into technical surfaces while
the institutions historically charged with answering move further upstream, the gap between
representation and responsiveness shapes the substrate my practice kneads into, not because
I chose it as a theme, but because operating inside democratic form made that gap graspable.

-- 83 of 336 --

83
Figure III.1: Democracy → Technosocial Sculpture
Figure III.1 adds a curved arrow from Democracy to Technosocial Sculpture, because democracy no longer
functions as the unquestioned regime under which synthetic politics occur. Instead, it designates the material
that is turned, through those experiments, into a technosocial and sculptural problem. The movement is not a
sublation of art into politics, but a reframing in which democracy, once estranged, appears as an arrangement
of response-sites that can themselves be composed, contested, and re-specified.
Several propositional framings have accompanied this migration. Algorithmic democracy, as
García-Marzá and Calvo define it, envisions a system of social organisation in which AI is
gradually incorporated into democratic processes of deliberation, decision-making, and
institutional design in both state and civil society (García-Marzá and Calvo 2024). The
ambition is expansive, but the democratic frame into which AI is incorporated remains
largely presupposed: its institutional grammar held constant, while mediating instruments are
replaced. Augmented democracy proposes software agents that unbundle policy proposals and
generate personalised proxies for citizens between elections (Gudiño et al. 2024). Here, the
political subject is rendered more granularly, but the representative structure receiving
preferences is not put at stake. Plurality, in the formulation derived from Taiwan’s digital

-- 84 of 336 --

84
democracy, sets aside aggregation and personalisation in favour of a co-creative policy that
facilitates rough consensus across disagreement (Weyl, Tang et al. 2024).
Of the three, plurality comes closest to acknowledging that the democratic medium is itself at
issue, because Weyl and Tang’s focus on listening broadly shifts the democratic purpose
from what people want to how disagreement becomes navigable at scale. But these
propositions remain tied to the question of how AI ‘enters’ democracy, not what democracy
is made of once AI is inside it, nor whether what emerges can remain democratic in any
inherited sense at all.
The propositions share a structural feature with the upstream governance where democracy
is called upon to contain what outpaces it, invoked as oversight for infrastructures whose
technical substrate it is not specified to reach. The summitisation of AI governance, from
Bletchley Park through Seoul to Paris, formalised democracy as a stated horizon, while the
sites at which its specification operates remained outside the frame. Democracy is here a
legitimation formula whose adequacy to the complexity it is meant to govern is assumed
rather than demonstrated. This pattern surfaces most concretely in how AI's architects cast
democracy as the form meant to govern catastrophic scale. OpenAI’s founders treats AI
through a nuclear precedent, proposes “something like an IAEA,” and insists that people
should “democratically decide on the bounds and defaults” (Altman, Brockman, and
Sutskever 2023). Dario Amodei places risks including AI models contributing to nuclear
weapons beside the aspiration that “democratic societies lead in AI” (Amodei 2025), and
Yoshua Bengio situates AI extinction risk alongside the prospect of “nuclear war” and argues
for a “cooperative group of democracies” to prevent catastrophic concentration of power
(Bengio 2023). These convergences narrows critical imagination. A democracy figured as
oversight and coordination pushes into the background the possibility that democracy here
takes on the lineaments of a totalitarian arrangement in the form of containing informational
overload. Democratic safeguarding is how the bomb survives its detonation as permanent
readiness, automatic release, and endpoint administered in advance.
This is where my propositional framing diverges: the synthetic is morphologically generative.
When synthetic forms are scaled, handled, and circulated, they do not merely modify prior
forms, but recurrently drive the material substrate towards a new synthesis, following how I
posit that the form of democracy may always “fork, refound, hybridise with other
governance logics, or relinquish inherited shells” in the corollary clause for Article 7.

-- 85 of 336 --

85
When Emily Bender, co-author of the “Stochastic Parrots” paper (Bender et al. 2021),
criticised The Synthetic Party as “some sort of performance art” that “purports to be
interacting with the actual Danish political system” using “the very democratic process as its
material” in a manoeuvre she deems “disastrously anti-democratic” (Bender 2022), her
description is more precise than the political judgement. What Bender identified is the
operation I formalise with this section. I did not intervene within democratic process as a
neutral frame through which to place an artwork. I built a political form that operated by
handling democratic process itself as material, which is to say its registration procedures, its
formatting of voice, and its mechanisms of counting and answering. Bender’s criticism
reveals the assumption she shares with the propositional framings: that democracy is a thing
one enters, follows, repairs, or improves, and not a compositional process whose technical
form is available for handling. What my practice encountered more precisely was that
building a party from AI-processed non-voter programmes did not intervene in democratic
form from the outside, but handled its material conditions from within, and that this turned
out to be constitutive of synthetic politics as an emergent field rather than incidental to it.
Every act of voting, counting, representing, and deliberating relies upon arrangements that
format and mediate who can speak, how voice is registered, and where response is delivered.
These arrangements are rarely treated as constitutive of what the political is but remain its
technical unthought. Rather than anchoring these mechanisms in responsibility as a property
of sovereign speakers, I call them response-sites to identify the politics of holding and
releasing where the capacity to answer is either acquired or blocked, where consequences
register, and demands are addressed, deferred, or withheld. This term inherits, in a restricted
sense, Donna Haraway’s argument that responsibility is not a moral attribute possessed by
sovereign subjects, but a relational capacity enacted through situated encounters between
life-forms, technologies, and worlds, what she calls response-ability (Haraway 1997; 2016).
What I extend from Haraway is her break with the assumption that responsibility resides in a
subject capable of bearing it through personal identity. In democracy, responsibility is not
only a moral burden but a political and juridical ordering of how officeholders are to be
questioned, judged, sanctioned, removed, and in some cases prosecuted for what their
actions have done in democracy’s name. Haraway’s break is indispensable, because the forms
I theorise in this dissertation, parties, summits, chambers, and governance algorithms,
compose responsiveness across distributed sites where no single subject can answer, and to
bear the consequences of answering, in that full sense of response-ability.

-- 86 of 336 --

86
The response-site does not, however, import a ready-made ontology with it. Haraway’s
response-ability is elaborated within what she later theorises as natureculture (Haraway 2003),
whereas the context my practice works through is a technosocial milieu in Terranova’s sense
(Terranova 2021; 2024), that is, in my own phrasing, a field of human-made arrangements
that acquire their operational logics through deployment, iteration, and collective handling.
Democracies, with and without AI, are not biological organisms, even when they develop
complexities no one designed in advance. My point is not to transpose the naturecultural
entanglement directly into democracy, but to ask how responsiveness is composed within
technosocial ecologies whose agencies have been designed, recursive, and infrastructurally
organised, even if, as Yuk Hui suggests, political reason increasingly confronts technical
tendencies that no longer behave as mere mechanism (Hui 2025: 200). The problem, then, is
not whether responses are entangled, they are, but how their specific forms of entanglement
are organised for answerability to be deposited through them, and how those addressed can
contest and configure the responses by which they are answered. In that sense, I do not use
response-sites as anything resembling ontological first principles, but as a practical way of
describing difficult arrangements, such as my registration of The Synthetic Party, in which
responsiveness is able to be deposited, withheld, contested, and reformatted.
The form through which I first got hold of that substrate was the party. The party is not
merely one institution among others inside democracy, but one of the primary mediations
through which political belonging is organised. Representation has also been composable in
other forms, through constitutions, electoral law, and census categories, but the party is the
organisational form in which I first encountered that composability as something I could
begin to practice. What The Synthetic Party made available to me was not an empty space
awaiting intervention, but a set of technical and institutional conditions – collecting voter
signatures, programme formation, formatting excluded political voice into representable
claims – that were already constitutive of what parties do, and that are more often handled
materially than inherited as conviction. If democracy’s gap between representation and
responsiveness is the substrate, party-form is the first medium through which that materiality
acquired a shape within my artistic research.
III.I Political Form as Social-, Antisocial-, and Technosocial Sculpture
Technosocial sculpture inherits its name from Joseph Beuys’s concept of social
sculpture (Soziale Plastik), but I approach that inheritance through a narrow channel. Rather

-- 87 of 336 --

87
than beginning from social sculpture as a general expansion of art into politics and life as
such, I pick up Beuys’ repeated formation of political organisation, because it is there that
social sculpture was prepared organisationally before it was stabilised conceptually (Ursprung
2021), and where the gathering of a constituency, formatting of participation, and
materialisations of collective standing within democracy was enacted as a sculptural medium.
Beuys was the first to cast party-form as sculptural material, and he did so through a single
formation that underwent successive metamorphoses across five years. The German Student
Party (Deutsche Studentenpartei), founded on 22 June 1967 with Johannes Stüttgen and
Bazon Brock, twenty days after the police killing of student Benno Ohnesorg, staged the
political upheaval of the moment as an organisational act, with its office inside the
Düsseldorf Art Academy where Beuys held a professorship in monumental sculpture. His
academy class, through years of exercises in radical-democratic thinking with students,
already functioned as what has since been called “die prototypische Soziale Plastik,” the
prototypical social sculpture, and the model from which the parties grew (Skrandies and
Paust 2021: 36). By 1970, the party had reconstituted itself as the Organisation of Non-Voters,
Free Referendum (Organisation der Nichtwähler, Freie Volksabstimmung), seeking to reach
electoral abstentionists through a form that made their absence itself a constituency, an
operation directly proximate to The Synthetic Party’s later attempt to represent non-voters.
In June 1971, the same formation was renamed Organisation for Direct Democracy by Popular Vote
(Organisation für direkte Demokratie durch Volksabstimmung), now co-directed with Karl
Fastabend, and it was at this juncture that Beuys introduced the concept of social sculpture
“als modus operandi der politischen Arbeit,” as the operative term for his synthesis of art
and politics (Skrandies and Paust 2021: 43). Installed at documenta 5 in Kassel in 1972, the
bureau was exhibited across one hundred days of public conversations. Each organisation
composed a new relation between address, constituency, and political form, and the social
throughout served as the sculptural medium itself. One can read this entire arc as “a
purposeful if largely intuitive attempt to performatively dismantle the role of artist and
teacher as a figure of mythic authority in the process of staging it” (Verwoert 2006).

-- 88 of 336 --

88
Figure III.2: Deutsche Studentenpartei (DSP)
Deutsche Studentenpartei (DSP), Düsseldorf, 1967. Joseph Beuys (wearing hat) and collaborators during the
early party phase; later print or stamp dated 1970. Photograph attributed to Johannes Stüttgen.
Bringing the social sculpture into parliamentary politics, Beuys helped found The Greens and
campaigned for them in the 1979 European election, even if his organisational and artistic
excess was not contained by the party’s institutionalisation. As Petra Kelly recalled, Beuys’
importance did not lie in party organisation, but in his capacity to turn artistic methods into
social energies for a renewed democracy, an ecological politics, and a pacifist society, a
capacity she felt The Greens largely lost after not letting him run as a parliamentary candidate
(Kelly [1992] 1994, 104–108). Across these iterations, the political forms repeatedly
materialised the plasticity inherent to Beuys’ visions of social belonging.
Party-form was the first material through which social sculpture was practised, and from
there it reached outward toward societal totality. The word sculpture keeps the concept tied
to labour rather than pure abstraction. Social sculpture cannot be the intention of an “active
master-sculptor” imposing form upon “passive sculpted-matter” (Stiegler et al. 2020), but is
rather, in my words, the labour of holding something together while it is pulled in several
directions and handling the seams where it holds loosely for others to knead into.

-- 89 of 336 --

89
Figure III.3: Det Syntetiske Parti (DSP)
Det Syntetiske Parti (DSP). Image published with the Vice feature “This Danish Political Party Is Led by
an AI” (Xiang 2022). Image attributed to Computer Lars.
Beuys’ parties sit within a wider lineage of artistic organisations, as I discuss in Article 1 What
binds this lineage is a technique in which the social composes by positing surplus
constituencies whose artificiality is part of the artistic gesture. Beuys’ formulations for the
early student party already pointed in this direction: “The German Student Party is the
world’s largest party, but most of its members are animals” (Beuys 1969). Representation is
produced by the form that opens address, not uncovered as though the represented had
always been there waiting.
What I retain from Beuys is this extension of sculptural practice into party-form. What I set
aside is the therapeutic rhetoric, inherited from Rudolf Steiner and later transmitted through
Bernard Stiegler, in which society appears as an imperilled organism awaiting cure. When
Beuys declares that “social sculpture or the social organism is not a thing that one can
perceive ... for if it could be perceived people would die of terror ... it is absolutely high time
to subject it to radical treatment, otherwise humanity will go under” (Beuys 2004: 21), the
social totality is cast as something metaphysically exterior to its own members, invisible but
omnipresent, and available only to the artist-healer who diagnoses what others cannot see.

-- 90 of 336 --

90
With technosocial sculpture, the social totality does not reside outside or above the
arrangement. It is in the substrate, in the grids, platforms, models, archives, and classificatory
systems through which participation is formatted and responsiveness is routed. Reading
about the Synthetic Summits online, standing inside exhibition architectures, or interacting
with virtual politicians’ feeds data into these systems. Social sculpture is not only declared or
chosen, but induced. Everyone is inside the sculpture before deciding whether they are
taking part. Each search query submitted, consent form clicked through, recommendation
absorbed as preference, protest registered as a metadata category, are not activities conducted
in proximity to the sculpture, but part of its constituent labour, as gestures through which
the social substrate maintains itself and through which attention is sorted, routed, and
sometimes foreclosed, most of them never understood as political at all.
This relocation, from a societal totality posited in the language of a romantic organism to a
totality located in the infrastructural substrate, is what distinguishes technosocial sculpture
from Beuys, and what connects it to the gap between representation and responsiveness.
The all-encompassing expansion of art into science, politics, and pedagogy that Beuys’ social
sculpture championed is part of what Peter Bürger criticises in The Decline of Modernism
(Bürger 1992). Bürger, whose Theory of the Avant-Garde (1984) had already argued that the
historical avant-garde’s attempt to sublate art into life praxis was doomed to institutional
reabsorption, turns in this later work to the specific predicament of Beuys. Beuys wished “to
produce a change in our attitudes,” yet could no longer pursue his aims within the
institutional context of art; meanwhile, he could not abandon that context without repeating
the avant-garde assault upon it in weakened form (Bürger 1992: 144). Bürger reads Beuys’
practice as a series of semantic displacements that pulls art into proximity with science and
with an all-encompassing notion of shaping, while retaining the claim to a specifically artistic
form of activity under the title of social sculpture (Bürger 1992: 145). Beuys appears, in
Bürger’s exact phrase, as working from “an impossible position,” “neither inside nor outside
of art as an institution but on a borderline that he constantly negates at the same time”
(Bürger 1992: 145). This remains, for my purposes here, the strongest formulation of the
repeated super-positionings that the act of transposing party-form to artistic practice entails.
The impossibility is not merely conceptual, which Bürger’s analysis of the relations between
popularity and esotericism in Beuys makes clear. Beuys “did abandon himself to the media,”
becoming widely recognisable far beyond avant-garde discourse, yet his works remained
“esoteric, inaccessible even to those who attempted to follow the self-commentaries of the

-- 91 of 336 --

91
artist” (Bürger 1992: 159). What was politically alive in The German Student Party was not
what the art world most wanted to canonise, while what might still be artistically at stake in
The Greens remained largely invisible to the constituencies they sought to represent. The
Synthetic Party inhabits analogous asymmetries. Its artistic significance exceeds its wider
recognition as a news story, and it acts politically in ways the art world cannot accommodate.
The party remains excessive over its plausibility as politics, and always insufficient in its claim
to artistic autonomy.
The operative form of technosocial sculpture, in this sense, does not name the rationality
proper to an instrument, but a work’s capacity to suspend any single claim upon it. What
cannot be fixed as either art or politics retains a margin of aesthetic indeterminacy, where
judgment is compelled before any concept can fully secure the object. It is here, at the level
of form, that constituencies can be composed which no existing institution is equipped to
recognise, and surpluses politicised that existing parties cannot readily absorb.
What Beuys inaugurated at the level of party-form, the philosopher Bernard Stiegler’s late
activities sought to push upstream toward societal totality. Curating the Serpentine Gallery’s
2018 Work Marathon, Stiegler proclaimed this ambition for the first time:
“From an aesthetic perspective, the question of shaping the Anthropocene leads
us back to the question of social sculpture that Joseph Beuys posed in the 1970s
– and which led him to create the ecological political party, the Greens. This is
to reopen the questions raised by Joseph Beuys, but in other terms and in
another context than that of the 1970s.” (Stiegler 2018)
Stiegler’s engagement with Beuys is an aesthetic-philosophical thread that deepened as his
critique of symbolic misery and industrial temporal objects hardened into an organological
diagnosis of attention, memory, and collective individuation under digital capitalism (Stiegler
2005; 2010). In Symbolic Misery II, Stiegler criticised how Beuys’s discourse “goes too far”
because it lacks the concepts needed to take us where it wants to go (Stiegler 2015: 106–107),
and Stiegler himself undertakes to supply those concepts by relocating social sculpture onto
the terrain of participation, technology, and the organisation of sensibility.
What Stiegler offers, beyond the critique of Beuys, is a rare attempt to think technics,
collective individuation, and the organisation of social life as a single problem-space, one in
which artistic research is posed as a hinge between research and the composition of social
totality. By 2020, his Internation project had recast artistic and contributory research as “a

-- 92 of 336 --

92
form of social (self) sculpture,” posed against the “generalization of proletarianization” and
against the “anti-social sculpture” of algorithmic governmentality (Stiegler et al. 2020). In this
vocabulary, repositories, databases, interfaces, and platforms are media supports in which
collective memory and norms are exteriorised and through which they may, at least in
principle, be reconfigured.
The Internation’s organisational trajectory tested how far such a claim could travel. Stiegler’s
project addressed itself upward from local territories of experimentation to the United
Nations itself, where in 2020 he presented its programme to the General Assembly. This
ascent constitutes, in my terms, the ultimate summit-form, and simultaneously a generic
flatness, routing the heterogeneous, locally rooted experiments to a diplomatic intervention
formatted for intergovernmental reception. As Selig-Addiss already argued against Stiegler’s
curation of the Serpentine Gallery’s Work Marathon, when social sculpture is recast as the
mission of the curator, the institution risks speaking as the primary artwork, while invited
participants are positioned as the labour that generates the institutional message, so as the
hierarchies the practice intends to overcome are reinstated through its own event-structure
(Selig-Addiss 2018). The institutional distribution of voice, in this critique, remains
asymmetrical even in the benevolence of such participatory formats.
My rendering of technosocial sculpture departs from Stiegler at a specific juncture. Stiegler
diagnosed a catastrophe in which platforms shortcut the inherited circuits through which the
social reproduces lived attachments, namely philia (φιλία), the adoptive and attention-forming
relation through which, in Stiegler’s Aristotelian and Simondonian interpretation, is how a
collective can individuate at all (Stiegler 2013). Stiegler’s crisis is libidinal and transindividual.
Algorithmic governance accelerates a corrosion of proximity, care, and shared attention by
reorganising the conditions under which habits and expectations become capturable,
calculable, and steerable. Stiegler’s countermove presupposes a social body that possessed,
prior to its pharmacological corruption, an integrity amenable to restoration.
But the formal condition of social sculpture, as the constitutive split between on one hand
what is politically operative through forms and what institutional logic can validate, and on
the other between what is artistically at stake and what constituencies can recognise, does not
presuppose an intact sociality. It operates with a social body that holds together only through
the labour of its self-contestation, acquiring whatever substance it might possess through
processes that handle it rather than as properties it carries independently of those processes.
These presuppositions are incompatible.

-- 93 of 336 --

93
Reparative practices require a prior state to be restored toward, while Beuys’ recurrent acts of
displacement push the social totality to be operative in any present moment through the
paradoxical labour of contestation itself. This kind of practice has gradually led me to bracket
Stiegler’s pharmacological diagnosis, while acknowledging the wound as a constitutive form.
Technics, following the Derridean reading of Plato’s Phaedrus, is grasped as pharmakon
encircling the wound (Derrida 1981), while the Derridean arkheion binds memory to juridical
authority and documentation to bureaucratic power (Derrida 1995), as I discuss at the close
of Article 5. Stiegler’s social sculpture leads from the wound toward reparative institutions in
the first sense of Derrida’s pharmakon, while the technosocial sculpture rather follows the
route of an archive fever by extending the wound as substrate.
To press past Stiegler, I take up the “techno-social hypothesis” of Tiziana Terranova,
according to which digital and computational networks “do not merely generate, as Gilbert
Simondon suggested, a natural and technical milieu, but also a directly technosocial one”
(Terranova 2021). Terranova relocates the milieu that contains sociality by describing how, in
its modern and sociological sense, the social never possessed an intrinsic, pre-existing
substrate that computation could target. Yet the social persists, even after its alleged death
with neoliberalism, as a computational, governmental, and epistemic category, appearing
across infrastructures as abstraction, as a plane of governance, and as a conflictual domain
(ibid.). Terranova formalises the technosocial as “the form of the social after its end”, a
conflictual political milieu that operates as the double or shadow of digital governmentalities
(ibid.). While Baudrillard could declare “the end of the social” (In the Shadow of the Silent
Majorities, 1983), the technosocial has nonetheless mutated through the infrastructures that
sort, route, and qualify participation, producing subjects as categories and publics as datasets,
while simultaneously opening its volatile circuits for mobilisation, paranoia, refusal, and
counter-public formation. What comes through is the remainder of a spectral form of social
totality, a channel energised through the metadata ontologies, interface grammars, and
automated classifications that decide, with banal force, who is legible, who can be addressed,
who is excluded, and who pays the price.
The political difference between Stiegler’s social sculpture and Terranova’s technosociality is
one of diagnostic posture and object-choice, of what one elects and desires as the necessary
nexus of intervention. While Stiegler treats the erosion of philia as a normative injury,
Terranova suspends this imperative to attend to the genealogies under which the social has
managed to persist without any guarantees for its productive presence. The technosocial
sculpture proceeds from Terranova’s suspension. It shows how to knead, concretely, into the

-- 94 of 336 --

94
operative forms of attachment and belonging that can remain graspable once responsiveness
is infrastructural. It takes specific operations of staging response where representation fails to
reach publics already being governed, and tracks what can be rendered contestable across
settings where response-sites are absent, suspended, or largely irrelevant.
III.II From Commandability to Answerability: Golems, Robots, and AI
Having established party-form as the shared first material of social sculpture for both
Beuys and me, and how I relocated the plasticity of the social organism to the infrastructural
substrate, I now discuss AI itself as a sculptural material to ask what form technosocial
sculpture takes once infrastructures built to sort people begin to address them, and what
prior logics of command and answer they carry into that address.
Sculpting names, at minimum, a drive towards consistency. For Rosalind Krauss, sculpture is
“a historically bounded category and not a universal one,” inseparable in its pre-modernist
logic from the monument, which “sits in a particular place and speaks in a symbolical
tongue” about that place’s use or meaning (Krauss 1979: 33). If sculpture becomes “almost
infinitely malleable,” Krauss warns, criticism can permit “anything at all” to be “hauled into
court” as precedent, until the term collapses under the heterogeneity it is made to contain
(Krauss 1979: 31, 33). In contrast to Beuys, whose expansion of sculptural shape-giving into
the social organism risks dissolving the category into a universal continuum, I seek to define
sculpture through “logical operations on a set of cultural terms,” yielding “an expanded but
finite set of related positions” rather than a transhistorical essence (Krauss 1979: 42–43).
What is at stake, in relating AI to technosocial sculpture, is neither an inflation of sculpture
into a universal category without edges nor another genealogy from Genesis to GPT. What I
identify instead is a specific transformation in which administrative commandability thickens
into public address without coinciding with political answerability.
A sculpture can commemorate, threaten, console, or be torn down, and with modernism
sculpture even loses its monumental relation to fixed sites (Krauss 1979: 34), but there is no
sculpture that returns the prompt of the passer-by. Speaking in a symbolical tongue is not the
same as replying. When a figure animated through AI is installed in a public office or
submitted to an electoral process, however, it thickens the monumental logic by turning the
marker into an interface and symbolic speech into a site of response. The figure occupies a
place and speaks in its name, but through promptability, variability, and distributed output.

-- 95 of 336 --

95
What appears is a redistribution of inscription, response, and imputability in which the figure
answers, while the arrangement that animates it remains difficult to answer for.
The golem is where this redistribution finds its precedent, a figure integral to AI’s self-
understanding while rarely recognised for the sculptural logic that identification conceals. In
Jewish mystical traditions, a rabbi moulds a human-like body of clay and inscribes or utters
the divine name, often as a charged sequence of letters whose efficacy lies in their ordered
combination, whereupon the figure rises and carries out commands, performing tasks and
guarding the community without comprehension of, or sovereignty over, what it does.
Norbert Wiener closed the late cybernetic tradition by naming his final book God & Golem,
Inc. (1964), declaring that “the machine is the modern counterpart of the Golem of the Rabbi
of Prague” (Wiener 1964: 94). Gershom Scholem successfully proposed naming Israel’s first
computer Golem Aleph, identifying John von Neumann and Wiener as “spiritual
descendants” of Rabbi Judah Loew ben Bezalel, the Maharal of Prague (Scholem 1966). At
MIT’s AI Laboratory in the late 1960s, Marvin Minsky, Joel Moses, and Gerald Sussman
discovered that they had all been told at their bar mitzvahs that they were descendants of the
Maharal and had been entrusted with the incantation for reanimating the golem (Marcus
1999). Sussman marked this inheritance in his doctoral thesis with the dedication “To the
Maharal of Prague, who was the first to realize that the statement ‘God created man in His
own image’ is recursive” (Sussman 1973). This is the lineage through which symbolic
permutation is pursued as a technique for animating inert matter, and it encapsulates the kind
of categorical overstretch Krauss warns against: a myth of infinite extensibility ranging from
kabbalistic permutation to the recursive formalism of early AI.
The version of the golem that stays with me as a creator is one in which the clay precedes the
holy name and speech. Franz Kafka’s untitled golem fragment of 1916 gives the counter-
image to cybernetic triumphalism. Kafka’s rabbi does not speak. He stands before a washtub,
sleeves rolled up “like a washerwoman” (wie eine Wäscherin), kneading clay that “already
showed the rough outline of a human form” (Kafka 1949: 153). In the golem tradition, what
animates the clay is not the accuracy of physical form but the rabbi’s mastery of the divine
name. Kafka’s rabbi, by contrast, is trapped in physical detail, working on “the joint of a
finger” while his jaw thrusts forward and his lips roll over one another without producing
speech (ibid.). As Dekel and Gurley observe, the rabbi here sets himself up not to make a
clay figure of a man but a man from clay, and the transgression reverses the intended
creation: instead of animating the figure, he begins to emulate it (Dekel and Gurley 2017:

-- 96 of 336 --

96
541). The German word Ton, meaning both clay and tone, concentrates the sculptural
displacement in a single word, since the rabbi shapes clay where he should be articulating
sound (ibid.). When one expects the golem to come to life, it is the rabbi himself who begins
to resemble his creation, “behaving like one enraged” (Kafka 1949: 153), wie ein Wütender, the
term traditionally used for the golem run amok (Dekel and Gurley 2017: 541). Creator and
creature here become indistinguishable in their muteness and rage.
Kafka’s golem, moreover, does not begin with the rabbi’s mastery, nor even with the rabbi
himself, but with a scene in which knowledge of the making has already begun to circulate. It
“soon became known” that a clay figure was being worked on; every door in the house
stands open day and night, and disciples, neighbours, and strangers move through it, looking
into the rooms until, in “a room on the ground floor near the kitchen,” they find in a
washtub a large lump of reddish clay whose bitterness they taste without understanding why
it is there at all (Kafka 1949: 152). The scene is social before it becomes esoteric: the labour
remains singular, but not sealed off, exposed to passing eyes, tongues, and hands before it is
conceptually understood. What appears is worked matter under shared exposure, not
successful animation but a public threshold at which making, handling, and recognition blur
into one another. Where the MIT lineage inherits a power to animate and sees recursion
where Kafka sees clay, I remain under Kafka’s command to think response and answerability
at the point where they no longer cleanly coincide.
What Kafka’s golem hints at, once specified rather than inflated, is a scene of matter under
inscription that stands close to sculpture without being reducible to it. In Psalm 139:16, golmi
likewise designates the unformed human body before God’s final completion, not the
artificial other of the human but the human in unfinished form, as a body under prior
inscription. What precedes both the human and the artificial is matter still coming into form.
The golem matters for technosocial sculpture not because it supplies AI with a timeless
ancestor, but because it locates the sculptural problem AI’s self-mythology leaves
unacknowledged: how matter is worked, inscribed, animated, and made consequential
without ever becoming sovereign over the force that passes through it.
Where the monument’s sculptural matter is made to signify, the golem’s is made to act,
which is to say that the golem names clay under inscription, responsive without bearing the
grammar of its response. My point is not that the golem speaks where sculpture remained
silent, but that both belong to regimes in which matter is worked beyond itself, and that what
shifts when AI enters the scene is that matter can now be queried and prompted.

-- 97 of 336 --

97
To build a political figure from language models is less to command than to knead under
inspection, to work on a form whose outlines are already there, to lose the ability to say with
certainty where intention ends and the material’s prior organisation begins, to find the
creature’s features forming in one’s own jaw as the words will not come. “Computer Lars,”
an anagrammatic inversion of “Marcel Proust,” tested how meaning and voice transfer
through arrangement rather than singular authorship. The Synthetic Party extended this into
a political register, treating democratic speech as something composed from fragments,
archives, refusals, and absences. The language that animates LLMs is not divine naming but
statistical arrangement, and those who deploy them in political contexts are already entangled
with the creation long before any form holds together.
As soon as AI takes place within democratic rituals, media circuits, and cultural institutions, it
is slop more often than soil, slurry before clay. The rabbi who kneads without speaking,
whose mute labour makes him difficult to distinguish from the creature, is closer to sculpting
with AI than the triumphant Maharal whose holy name raises the golem to defend the
community. AI enters collective life not only as a system but also as rumour, phantasy,
experiment, story, image, and named figure, handled in public before any stable agreement
emerges about what it is. AI models may be launched as engineering objects while remaining
politically unfinished, with the technical closure spilling forward into contested public form.
If the golem names one route from commandability into answerability, it does not exhaust
the political imaginary through which technosocial sculpture passes. The condition of being
handled before any final technical settlement leads not only back to kabbalistic permutation,
but sideways into another tradition: that of the political state figured as something inherently
robotic, from Hobbes’s description of the commonwealth as “but an Artificial Man”
(Hobbes 1651) onward. What recurs across such figurations, as I discuss in Article 3, is not
that golems or robots are literally running society, but that the political keeps describing itself
as if they were. The robot enters here as a figure of delegated operation that AI politicians
reactivate by positioning it within public address.
The philosopher Simondon helps clarify the status of that figure. In the introduction to On
the Mode of Existence of Technical Objects, he insists that “the robot does not exist” (Simondon
2017: 16), not because technical systems are unreal, but because the robot is first of all a
figment produced by two contradictory attitudes toward technics: its exclusion from the
world of significations and reduction to utility, and its simultaneous apprehension as strange,
hostile, and quasi-intentional. The robot is the imagined figure through which this supposed

-- 98 of 336 --

98
hostility is given motive and interiority. That misrecognition is then managed through a logic
of servitude, articulated in the injunction to place machines “in the service of man” as a
means of neutralising the threat culture itself has projected (Simondon 2017: 17). The two
attitudes sustain each other. To exclude technics from the world of significations is already to
mark it as something whose signifying capacity must be actively refused, and that refusal is
the gesture through which culture invests the technical with what it then attributes to the
machine’s own nature. For me, the robot’s ‘non-existence’ names not a lack in technics but a
cultural afterimage produced whenever a suppressed signification returns as hostile agency.
Read from the present conjuncture, AI politicians do not abolish this circuit so much as
displace its site. Signification no longer stabilises in the mute servant or the mechanical
double, but circulates across interfaces, archives, mediations, and data worlds. The ‘robot’
thereby enters political address less as an image of command than as a figure of response but
still carrying with it the entire mythological weight accumulated through its cultural handling.
Formulating this displacement as a tendency internal to political reason, philosopher Yuk
Hui reads Hegel’s “mechanical state” as a milestone in Reason’s self-realisation, and suggests
that digital infrastructures supersede the administrative layer through which representative
parliamentarism operates. Hui remarks in passing that “We may even see within a given time
the obsolescence of politicians, as Günther Anders said about the human being” (Hui 2025:
200). His Hegelian reading, oriented toward rational concretisation, leaves this analogy largely
unexplained, as the dialectic here has little place for a synthesis that belatedly resolves into a
further phase. However, placed side by side, the obsolescence of the politician and the
obsolescence of the human open a passage from figuration to disproportion, to the point
where scale, capacity, and responsibility no longer fit the figure of the leader.
Two senses of obsolescence are in play in Hui’s phrase between the obsolescence of the
politician and the obsolescence of the human, the first an overtaking at the level of scale, and
the second a surplus of mediation. Anders’ analysis of human obsolescence belongs to his
reflections on the nuclear bomb as a robotic arrangement. Die Antiquiertheit des Menschen, first
published in 1956, uses antiquiert to designate how the human being has been rendered
historically belated by the powers humans have made and can no longer keep pace with. The
human is not obsolete the way a superseded tool is outdated but has been overtaken by what
it has set in motion and cannot recall. Decades later, the virtual politician Wiktoria Cukt
would turn the phrase “obsolescence of politicians” into a campaign slogan in the Polish
presidential elections of 2000: Politycy są zbędni, ‘politicians are obsolete’, or more literally,

-- 99 of 336 --

99
‘politicians are unnecessary.’ With Cukt’s slogan, zbędni signifies something distinct from
obsolescence or antiquated in the German, as politicians are not belated but disposable,
redundant, and surplus to requirement. Where the bomb rendered ‘the human’ a historical
category, the algorithmic organisation of response made ‘the politician’ superfluous. The
function of directing and commanding is running without the body that once carried it, with
the steering leader left standing in a symbolic role largely incapable of grasping what it made.
In On the Bomb and the Roots of Our Apocalypse Blindness, Anders pursues the catastrophe of
scale to its structural limit. He traces how the organic premise ‘All humans are mortal’ has
been displaced first by ‘All humans can be killed’ in the extermination camps, and then, with
the bomb, by ‘Humanity can be killed as a whole’ (Anders 2025: 209). That species-scale
grammar is almost cliché in contemporary AI discourse, ranging from Eliezer Yudkowsky’s
warning that building superintelligent machines means that ‘literally everyone on Earth will
die’ (Yudkowsky 2023) to the UN Secretary-General’s call for an AI body modelled on the
International Atomic Energy Agency (Guterres 2023). Anders’ analysis, however, diagnoses
on the structural level how technical arrangement outruns any subject that still answers for it.
Asking the reader to assume the bomb has been deployed (an assumption Anders later calls
conservative, since the bomb is continuously ‘used’ through the deterrent arrangements that
sustain it as permanent threat; Anders 2025: 220), Anders demonstrates that the chain of
events leading up to catastrophe has been composed of so many links, agencies, intermediate
steps, and partial actions, taken by so many authorities, that “in the end, nobody would have
done it, as everyone would have merely done some other thing” (Anders 2025: 211). To
absorb whatever residual conscience remains, “oracle machines, automatic electronic
consciences” have been constructed, “to which responsibility can now be delegated,” while
“humans stand by with as much gratitude as triumph, washing their hands in innocence”
(Anders 2025: 211). The operator has “substituted ‘responsibility’ for a mechanical
‘response’” (Anders 2025: 211, English in original), and this transfer of responsibility onto
response reformats the moral course of action into whatever the computation has deemed.
What Anders describes here is a robotic arrangement in which the question of whether one
ought to act has been displaced by the question of what response a given configuration will
produce. The displacement proceeds so smoothly because, “even where there are no such
robots”, the mere complexity of modern organisation absorbs any moral scruples,
performing “a similar function to electrical resistors” (Anders 2025: 212). Anders condenses
this through a proverb attributed to ‘the Molussians’, an invented people whose Swiftian

-- 100 of 336 --

100
satirical geography runs through his work as a fictional ethnography of catastrophe: “Dirt
divided up a thousand times is clean” (ibid.). The critical point is not that conscience has
been transferred from one agent to another but that those who occupy positions within such
arrangements “are excluded from the possibility of having a conscience in the first place”
(ibid.). The catastrophe lies in the broken proportion the bomb introduced: an arrangement
whose effects exceed the imaginative, affective, and moral capacities of those entangled in it.
The AI does not arrive in political settings with a pre-shaped sculptural form. It inherits prior
figurations of worked matter and delegated operation, converging in figures that respond
without coinciding with whatever animates them, and taking a shape of its own through the
social processes that handle it and thickens into the density of an actor through the labour of
those who argue with it, legislate around it, elect its proxies, attribute will to it, or refuse to
