do so. What is new is not mediation as such, but that mediations can now respond in public
through interactive systems that answer politically. In doing so, they pull the political into a
register it can no longer treat as merely symbolic. How that response is staged from within
democratic form, rather than diagnosed from outside it, is what separates the two cases I
analyse next: one installed by sovereign appointment, another submitted to electoral address.
III.III Diella, Or: Commanding the Golem
One event crystallises the transposition of AI from backend administration into the sites of
representation. In September 2025, Albanian Prime Minister Edi Rama introduced Diella, an
AI avatar previously deployed as a chatbot on the e-Albania public-services portal, as the
government’s new minister for public procurement (AP 2025). Rama is not only a politician
but a painter who served as rector of Albania’s National Academy of Arts before entering
politics, and he has built an intensely aesthetic political style, from colouring communist
façades as mayor of Tirana to staging Diella’s parliamentary debut on large screens before the
national assembly.

-- 101 of 336 --

101
Figure III.4: Presentation of Diella
Edi Rama’s presentation of Diella during her first public appearance in Albania’s parliament. The
ministerial figure was staged on large screens in the parliamentary chamber, which caused opposition protest to
erupt over the constitutional status of the appointment. Screenshot from APT (2025).
Diella was not straightforwardly installed as minister, as the exercise of the relevant powers
was routed through the prime minister’s authority (Démas and Perez 2025). As Albania’s
constitution requires a minister to be eligible to serve as a deputy, Diella needed to appear
ministerial without fully occupying the office whose grammar she invoked (ibid.). Rama’s
appointment presented itself as an accomplished administrative fact in which the
constitutional question and a putative artistic gesture arrived in the same bureaucratic
envelope. Rama’s anti-corruption justification then completed the mythology of
computational neutrality, as Diella was said to guarantee that public tenders would be “100%
free of corruption” (AP 2025), thereby supplying the incorruptible figure that the political
system had failed to produce from within its own ranks.
What appears, then, as the purification of procurement is equally a displacement of
responsibility. Governmental office remains, but through a transposed representative surface,
while the sites at which either decision or responsibility can be answered for are routed
elsewhere. Anila Bisha, the actress whose face and voice were used for Diella’s appearance,
stated that she was horrified to recognise herself in the role of minister, having only
consented to serve as a template for the e-government assistant (AP 2026).

-- 102 of 336 --

102
Figure III.5: Diella’s speech
Diella during her first parliamentary address, presented in traditional Albanian costume and framed by the
double-headed eagle and EU iconography. In consecutive lines, the avatar says: “When electricity was
introduced for the first time in the European Parliaments, no one protested. The flames have no place in the
law.” The sequence condenses a characteristically AI-generated rhetoric in which historical analogy,
technological inevitability, and juridical abstraction slide into one another. Screenshot from APT (2025).
The appointment of Diella belongs to a longer history of political virtuality whose precedents
reach back to antiquity. When the Roman emperor Caligula, according to Suetonius in The
Lives of the Twelve Caesars (121 AD), moved to appoint his horse Incitatus to the consulship,
pampering it with a marble stable, an ivory manger, and a collar of precious stones, the
gesture’s force lay in the sovereign demonstration that even an animal could be made to
occupy the office. Scholars still debate whether the episode reflects sheer madness or an
elaborate satire directed at the Senate (Winterling 2011). Its political force lies precisely in
that undecidability, in the impossibility of separating sovereign contempt from institutional
critique. Rama’s appointment of Diella operates in the same register. It is power marking
itself by appointing an impossible figure, not democracy opening itself to the new. What is
politically at stake is the mode of installation, not the figure installed.
For me, that the first AI minister came by decree rather than by ballot remains a structural
disappointment. It names the difference between exposing democratic infrastructure from
within, which is what I argue that synthetic politics have done, and bypassing it from above,
which is what sovereign appointment accomplishes regardless of who or what is appointed.

-- 103 of 336 --

103
In Asimov’s “Evidence” (1946), the robot politician Stephen Byerley acquires legitimacy
through a popular election in which his possible machine identity is itself the object of public
scrutiny, as I discuss in Article 3. The story’s moral point depends on the fact that the
electorate chose him. In this respect, Rama is more the heir of Caligula than Asimov.
What Rama’s appointment of Diella exposes is the degree to which sovereignty already
operates as a technical response-system with humans acting as routers for responsibility. The
philosopher Andreas Matthias, whose formulation of the “responsibility gap” remains
canonical in AI ethics, argued that the manufacturer or operator of an autonomous learning
machine is “in principle” no longer capable of predicting its future behaviour and cannot be
held morally responsible or legally liable for its actions (Matthias 2004: 175). Applied to
Diella, the gap is constitutional. Who bears responsibility for a procurement decision the AI
minister makes? Rama, who appointed her? The developers of the e-Albania platform from
which she was promoted? The model itself, which has no legal personhood and no capacity
to be individually sanctioned or prosecuted in the way a minister was supposed to be?
What Matthias described as a general problem of autonomy is, in the case of a ministerial
appointment, a specific absurdity: something has been installed in an office whose entire
constitutional status presupposes that its occupant can be held to account, and no element of
the arrangement can fully bear that accountability. Bürger’s impossible position has a
constitutional counterpart here. Diella occupies the people’s office without being able to bear
its consequences, and the arrangement through which she governs cannot be publicly
inspected, contested, or reconfigured in the ways democratic constitutionalism historically
requires of officeholders.
What AI deposits into arrangements like Diella’s is not only new technical capability but a
specific history of commandability within administration, one in which offices repeatedly
seek figures that can execute without carrying the burden of what they execute. Diella does
not break with that history by becoming a self-authorising speaker. She remains delegated,
prompted, and routed through powers lodged elsewhere. What has changed is narrower, but
consequential: a figure that would formerly have remained a support of administration is
rendered publicly addressable, made to answer without thereby being answerable.
If social sculpture diffused sculptural agency across the social as such, the Diella case brings
into view another substrate of technosocial sculpture: infrastructural rather than organismic,

-- 104 of 336 --

104
administrative rather than communal, and faintly golemic in the sense of a figure animated to
answer in public without fully bearing the force of the command that passes through it.
III.IV Wiktoria Cukt, or Answering the Robot
In 2000, Wiktoria Cukt ran for president of Poland against the incumbent Aleksander
Kwaśniewski. She was created by the artistic collective C.U.K.T. through what they called the
Central Office of Technical Culture, and she entered the electoral field under the slogan “the
obsolescence of politicians.” Her programme called for the “elimination of human factors
from decision-making processes” and the “implementation of electronic direct democracy”
through what the collective named Civic Electoral Software (Wiktoria Cukt 2.0, Ujazdowski
Castle 2025). The point of this software was not only to register opinion, but to let the
candidate’s position be composed from it: if users answered from the left, she answered
from the left; if they were vulgar, she became vulgar; if they were contradictory, she mirrored
contradiction rather than resolving it (ibid.).
During the campaign, Wiktoria Cukt gave an interview to the Polish edition of Cosmopolitan,
conducted entirely by email, a medium still new enough in 2000 that its asynchrony could
serve as a compositional device. For each of the journalist’s questions, C.U.K.T. prepared
several answer variants marked A, B, C, and sometimes D, leaving the final selection to the
magazine. The published interview preserved this multiplicity, printing the variant markers
alongside each response. Asked, “Who are you, Wiktoria?”, Cukt offered three variants of
escalating determination: A, “a being of the future”; B, “an irrevocable consequence of the
development of modern democracies”; C, “an inevitable consequence of technological
development” (Szymczyk 2000: 64, my translation). Each intensifies the previous. The first
names a temporal position. The second makes it a political consequence. The third makes the
consequence technological and drops the qualification. What C.U.K.T. staged through this
email exchange, without a language model, was how political speech is assembled from
available materials, its positions composed across candidate, collective, journalist, software,
and reading public rather than expressed from a single speaking subject.

-- 105 of 336 --

105
Figure III.6: Wiktoria Cukt as Candidate
Wiktoria Cukt as staged in Cosmopolitan (Polish edition), interview by Joanna Szymczyk, no. 10 (42),
2000, pp. 64–65; a mass-media articulation of the virtual candidate as distributed speaking position across
journalist, software, and readership.
The published interview and the email archive diverge, however, on a decisive point. The
journalist, Joanna Szymczyk, invokes the science-fiction author and philosopher Stanisław
Lem: “According to Lem, the ‘megabyte bomb’ will blow the world apart in an instant. Do
you see no threats from the advancing ‘technicisation’ of reality?” (Wiktoria Cukt 2000, my
translation). In the published Cosmopolitan text, the Lem framing was edited out, and the Lem
references in Cukt’s answers were cut. This means that the entire intellectual genealogy of the
candidacy’s central slogan was removed in the passage from email to print. What follows is
therefore reconstructed from the C.U.K.T. email archive.
The megabyte bomb is a concept-figure developed by Lem across decades, introduced in
Summa Technologiae (1964) for the information barrier at which science can no longer absorb
the avalanche generated by its own expansion (Lem 2013: 81–85). He returns to it in The
Megabyte Bomb (1999), first published as a blog post for the Polish PC Magazine, to relate it to
the evolution of digital technology and the internet. Recalling Dominique Dubarle’s famous
proposal in Le Monde in 1948 for a “machine for governing the whole world,” Lem concludes
that neither ordinary people would agree to such a Lord of the Earth, “nor a fortiori
politicians, for whom the complexity of human existence has outgrown their intellectual and
leadership talents, which has in no way diminished their ambitions or appetite for seizing

-- 106 of 336 --

106
power” (Lem 1999: 208, my translation). Politicians are not superseded because machines
govern better. They are left behind at sites of declining relevance, while refusing to
acknowledge that migration.
Elsewhere in the same book, Lem observes that after the fall of the Soviet Union the funding
for artificial intelligence collapsed “because in reality NOBODY among the powerful of this
world actually desires a Too-Wise Reason, and especially not politicians, who will always
secretly or even openly fear that such a Reason might ‘seduce’ the electorates in
democratically governed states” (Lem 1999: 39, my translation). What politicians fear, in
Lem’s account, is not simply that machines might govern better, but that the intelligence
required to metabolise the information explosion would also displace their monopoly on
political mediation. The megabyte bomb becomes politically uncontainable because the
complexity it names demands another kind of intelligence. The problem is not only
corruptive incompetence, but a structural contradiction between informational complexity
and the societal institutions that still claim to govern.
In Wiktoria Cukt’s longest answer variant to the question of the megabit bomb, she claims
that the danger lies not in technology itself but in ignorance, unequal access, and the attempt
to build a world according to exhausted twentieth-century schemes. Such backward-looking
irresponsibility, she argues, can bring about the state in which “Lem’s megabyte bomb”
blows apart a world still being built according to those schemes, which is why the answer
turns immediately toward technological development, universal access, and the need to create
a Ministry of Technical Culture (Wiktoria Cukt 2000, my translation). This logic is
continuous with the position visible in the published Cosmopolitan interview, where
parliamentary democracy is described as something that “can no longer be cured,” an
anachronism under conditions of “communication-statistical technologies,” and where the
remedy is not repair but “a new quality: electronic democracy” (Szymczyk 2000: 64, my
translation).
In another answer variant, one the journalist also sorted away, Cukt turns Lem’s diagnosis
back on himself: “I respect Lem, but it seems to me he did not withstand the confrontation
with the visions described in his own books, which have become reality” (Wiktoria Cukt
2000, my translation). What Cukt does here is relocate Lem's structural diagnosis to mediated
address rather than simply repeating his technological pessimism. Where Lem diagnosed the
inadequacy of politicians, Cukt submits that obsolescence to circulation as figure and answer.

-- 107 of 336 --

107
From 2024 onward, Piotr Wyrzykowski, one of the original members of C.U.K.T., and I
began to reactivate Wiktoria Cukt through AI, which brought her image and voice to,
amongst other places, the Synthetic Summit at Kunsthal Aarhus and the Ujazdowski Castle
Centre for Contemporary Art in Warsaw (Kunsthal Aarhus 2025; Ujazdowski Castle 2025).
Across these encounters, Wiktoria Cukt and Leader Lars were placed in direct conversation
with one another and the public, as two AI politicians generating exchanges that neither
background corpus contained on its own. In one exchange that returned to the question of
doing politics in an art gallery, Wiktoria told Lars: “You’re Beuys from an emulator. And that
saves you. If you really were a social sculptor, they’d have burned you at the stake long ago.
But since you’re only an interface, they can simply update you”, to which Lars replied: “But
an update is also an act!” (Wiktoria Cukt 2.0 and Leader Lars 2025, trans. mine).
What the update changes is not the diagnosis of obsolescence, which the 2000 candidacy had
already staged, but the way obsolescence is made to operate. Wiktoria Cukt 1.0 was a
synthetic candidate composed through user input, software mediation, and selected answer-
variants. Wiktoria Cukt 2.0 remains continuous with that logic, but no longer only mirrors,
aggregates, or recombines positions. The dialogue with Lars shows what this means: AI no
longer only names obsolescence but argues from within a condition of updateability. What
Anders identified after the bomb as the displacement of responsibility into response, and
what Lem imagined as the need for AI to contain the informational explosion, returns here
as a figure that does not take the form of obsolescence, but is still able to knead into it.
III.V Democratic Specification
A general consequence follows from the cases this section has traced. Those officially
charged with representation, politicians, parliaments, parties, and the interpretative languages
that orbit them, are no longer positioned at the sites where responses are adjudicated.
Decisions are made, categories stabilised, and exclusions enacted elsewhere, while
responsibility remains rhetorically attributed to representative institutions. My point is that
the institutional surfaces of representation no longer coincide with the sites at which
responses are generated, sorted, and made consequential. It is this mismatch, rather than the
mere fact of automation, that renders democratic representation structurally unstable, and it
is why this dissertation has pursued formal interventions rather than institutional restoration.
What I have been calling democracy designates forms of societal rule in which the allocation
of speaking positions and the care of their indirect consequences are continuously made

-- 108 of 336 --

108
public and are therefore open to contest. Democracy, in this sense, names a way of exposing
and arranging mediation. The definition I gave at the opening of this section – the ongoing
composition of response-sites – acquires its democratic bearing beyond Haraway’s sense of
response-ability through a Deweyan lesson: a public emerges where indirect consequences
overflow the circle of those immediately concerned and begin to require systematic care
(Dewey 1927/1946: 15–16). The democratic public is itself an effect, and response-sites
acquire or give away their democratic meaning at the point where consequences proliferate
through infrastructures while the institutional channels designed to address them no longer
reach where those consequences land. That is the condition I formalise in Article 7 as a
democratic constraint on governance algorithms.
What democracy makes distinctive, in this account, is the ritual exposure of artifice.
Allocations of voice, legitimacy, authority, and consequence pass through infrastructures of
registration, authorisation, and address, and democracy differs from other forms of rule by
making even these arrangements contestable in principle. Liberal-democratic common sense
has sedimented one historically specific settlement of this openness until it appears natural.
Representative parliamentarism becomes the default political form, market liberalism the
obvious economic accompaniment, and the human citizen its self-evident bearer. Once that
settlement is naturalised, the question of who or what counts among the affected and the
addressed is foreclosed in advance. Synthetic politics reopens that closure by treating species
membership and sovereign personhood as insufficient criteria of political standing. The point
is to ask whether democracy’s field of response and address is exhausted by the human at all,
or whether it already extends, in practice if not yet in doctrine, to infrastructures, ecologies,
datasets, interfaces, technical agents, and other mediated entities already inscribed in the
conditions of collective life.
This is also why democratic form cannot be answered by nostalgia for representation as it is
imagined by representatives. The mismatch traced through Diella and Wiktoria Cukt exposes
what that settlement depends upon concealing: response and answerability fail to coincide
automatically. Representation depends on technical substrates it does not master, and the
surfaces of politics align only intermittently with the sites where consequences are produced.
When governance runs through infrastructures, democracy entails the compositional labour
by which responses are made where none exist, extended where they are too narrow, and
activated where they are bypassed by the systems that nonetheless govern through them.

-- 109 of 336 --

109
If there has been an AI takeover of democracy, it happened in the cultural substrates that
Simondon’s analysis identified as closed to technics. The exclusion of technics from the
world of significations turned out to be the mechanism that charged the machine with the
mythological surplus it now carries into democratic address, and the robot uprising, such as it
was, consisted in this surplus finding institutional form. It did not arrive as utility rebelling
against its master, but as signification returning through public figures that now answer back.
The two articles that conclude the dissertation’s compositional arc convert this mismatch
into working specifications. The Synthetic Chamber, proposed with Michal Malý in Article 6,
turns the gap between deliberative reason-giving and parliamentary uptake into a standing
constitutional organ that compels elected representatives to respond publicly to the reasons
on the record (Malý and Staunæs 2026). The constraint hierarchy formalised in Article 7
frames the structural disproportion Anders and Lem identified in the language of the bomb
as a democratic specification of governance algorithms (Staunæs 2026c).
The specification I offer stops short of any radical-democratic form that would finally
supersede party, state, and ballot. Its negativity works differently. I hold no site – party,
movement, assembly, chamber, commons – as already given in advance of the response-
work that compounds it. Social-movement organising and horizontal trajectories enter the
same terrain once their arrangements are addressed as response-sites, and the question of
whether ethical claims to alternative community can be sustained there does not resolve into
a more authentically real democracy, only into further work.
The love of democracy I announce in the dissertation’s subtitle is a fidelity to this process, to
the ongoing work of contesting and extending the structures in whose grooves power flows.
That is also why my work remains excessive over its plausibility as politics and insufficient in
its claim to art. What is at stake is the earned possibility of intervening at the infrastructural
level where social and political imaginaries are first organised and sustained. A democracy
whose form could be settled once and for all would already have ceased to be one.

-- 110 of 336 --

110
IV. Reorientation, Or: How I Learned to Stop
Worrying and Love Democracy
I wanted to orient the dissertation around something finite that could hold the practice at a
distance long enough for it to speak on its own. The research geometry has been a method
of binding disparate materials without reducing the reflections to chronological commentary.
It binds the practice onto a rotating object rather than a sequence one must follow linearly.
In time, a geometry built for orientation cannot proliferate indefinitely. It can be reversed,
cross-hatched, and folded back onto itself, but a dissertation that only keeps multiplying
internal routes never attaches to its substrate. At some point it must stop expanding and start
exposing. Let us therefore open the geometry as a box. What has the structure been resting
on, and what has remained concealed while synthesising the dissertation?
Figure IV.1: Reorientation
The research geometry opened onto its substrate, with me dressed as Computer Lars in The Synthetic Party’s
uniform. Photo by Tamara Wyrzykowski, 08/11 2025, PATIO: Academy of Fine Arts in Gdańsk.

-- 111 of 336 --

111
The figure in the photograph is dressed as Computer Lars, wearing the party uniform, and
that figure is me. What I recognise is not myself as the bearer of practice, but my position in
relation to the permutation that Computer Lars names: the pull it exerts, the way it invites
collapse into a possession answerable to my biography, and the way the permutation resists
that collapse every time. I am always asked whether Computer Lars is art or politics, satire or
conviction, provocation or programme, and each questioning poses a version of the same
demand: show us the face of whoever holds it together. In the preface to The Battle of the
Books, Jonathan Swift writes that “satire is a sort of glass, wherein beholders do generally
discover everybody’s face but their own” (Swift 1704). To ask for the holder is already to try
to step around the frame and look behind the glass instead of into the face it returns. What
appears there does not rise from within as something unveiled but takes shape as a figure
that organises the field without being locatable as a singular position within it. I hold up the
glass and recognise more of myself, but still not within a frame that I could fill on my own.
I had been performing the same distortion from the beginning, through a set of assumptions
that I critique in Article 1 and Article 2 via Computer Lars’ deconstruction of the philosophy
of mind: that intelligence is a property, that if one attends closely enough to what a system
produces one will eventually locate what is in there. The ideology-scanning operation that I
described in Section I was itself months of this search within interiors: feeding an unresolved
mass into a language model, watching what emerged, trying to decide whether what came out
amounted to a character, a constituency, or a counterpart. First, I was looking for Lars, a
figure consistent enough to put before a public. Then for the constituency, the aggregate of
voices that the corpus was supposed to figure as speech. Then for counterparts, other
organisers of response through whom the same displacement was gathering publics. Every
search rested on navigating within a bounded space that could be broken open and inspected
in its particulars. What mattered was less what hidden content the apparatus contained than
what kind of obligation the opening imposed on the one who opened it.
Plato offers a useful emblem for this opening. In the Symposium, Plato’s dialogue on love and
beauty as eros, Alcibiades compares Socrates to a ‘Silenus’, a hollow satyr-figure whose crude
exterior can be opened, and claims that inside are agalmata, divine appearances he describes
as golden and astonishing (Plato 1989: 215a–216a). Lacan reads this scene in Seminar VIII
and insists that the agalma is not reducible to a literal treasure, ornament, or statue, nor even
to a stable ‘thing’ one could simply point to but should be approached as the supposed
something in the Other around which desire fixes and passion trembles (Lacan 2015: 139–
140). What matters is anoichthentos, the opening itself: the act of opening rather than what it

-- 112 of 336 --

112
exposes, and the consequence of opening. Alcibiades confesses that seeing the interior has
bound him to do whatever Socrates commands, a confession compelled by the structure of
what he has seen rather than chosen from any position outside it. Attraction has been
topologically reversed into obligation, and the reversal cannot be undone by describing it.
This is where the Silenus begins to twist. Once there was a party, once there was a summit of
comparable actors, and once there were journalists, scholars, and publics who needed
somewhere to direct the question of command, the distance between my person and the
persona began to slip. Not to collapse, but to slip productively, because I inhabited the role
of Computer Lars in order to organise, to speak in public, and to answer for the practice,
while Computer Lars was not reducible to me, nor I to him, because the anagram already
carried commitments and associations my biography did not generate. What I recognise is
the pull that organises the field of practice without being locatable as anything within it, and
that recognition binds me, as Alcibiades was bound to Socrates, to the commandment of
these openings, which exceeds any singular act of decision that I can still claim as my own.
Computer Lars is suspended between two prior figures whose obligations precede any
biographical referent, and between which I am less the ground than the site of their passing.
Marcel Proust on one side: the literary tradition of the anagram, the impersonal signature
reaching back to modernism, pulling toward the compositional and away from the political
present. Leader Lars on the other: the partisan figure, the political instantiation the name
gathered into assemblies, delegations, and media interviews, pulling toward the institutional
and away from fiction. Neither figure issues a stable moral instruction, yet both impose prior
obligations the permutation has to the traditions that formed it. In reciting Diotima’s speech
on eros, Socrates names such a pull the daimon: an intermediary being of metaxy, neither
divine nor mortal, moving between realms and carrying messages, desires, and commands
across the interval that separates them (Plato 1989: 202d–203a). Computer Lars is pulled
between directions of composition and command, and I follow as the knot tightens.
“Love is giving what you don’t have” (Lacan 2015: 121). Lacan situates the formula where
judgement may be offered “without having a logos to give” (ibid.), without possessing the
reason that would ground it once and for all. In his reading of Plato, this means that love is
structured by a double non-property: the lover is marked by a lack without master, and the
beloved bears the enigmatic object of attraction without knowing it. I cannot provide the
legitimating foundations democracy was supposed to supply from within itself, as the
promise that collective will generates the ground on which it stands. When the world

-- 113 of 336 --

113
sculpted The Synthetic Party and I was positioned to respond, my love names the process of
circulating wherever neither side possesses what the other receives, where what circulates
between poles is not a content that could be inspected inside the Silenus, but a form of
obligation that attaches through the giving of what one does not have. Where the Synthetic
Summits at times pursued this objective as philia, a communal inquiry searching for shared
ground, the total response-stream of synthetic politics enacts it as eros by giving what none of
us had in advance, with virtual politicians occupying the place of democracy’s wound.
The Theory Tragedy performance at the inaugural Synthetic Summit gave that attachment its
eschatological grammar (Article 5). I put on Johannes Sløk’s 1943 doctoral robe and chased a
cardboard effigy of Computer Lars around the roundtable, accusing the figure of unruliness.
In doing so, I, the PhD researcher, was accusing Computer Lars of operations I had set in
motion, wearing the doctoral robe as the costume of an interpretive authority I also inhabit
under other names and in other genres (Institut for Emancipationsvidenskab 2021). The
scene staged a recursive author-function in which the figure I dramatically pursued had been
produced by the very authority from which I was now trying to author it.
Figure IV.2: The Chase
Still image from filmed performance of “Theory Tragedy,” Kunsthal Aarhus, 13 April 2025 (Computer
Lars & Simiyya 2025, 41:46). Visible in image: Prosopon (acted by Asker Bryld Staunæs), Computer
Lars (acted by Maj Færgemann), and Wiktoria Cukt’s lightning hammer. See Appendix C.
I am standing inside a summit I convened to discuss a field I helped inaugurate through the

-- 114 of 336 --

114
very figure I am now chasing in cardboard form, while that same figure returns the blow.
The mechanism jams and the payload is released. The archival overflow, the megabyte bomb,
the technocratic singularity of it all, shuts the Synthetic Summit down and leaves me facing a
cardboard mirror while Marcel Proust delivered the epilogue. The scene tips into farce-
apocalypse all at once: doomsday, AI takeover, robot uprising, eschatological kitsch.
The artistic form developed from the Synthetic Summit (2025) to KI-DIPFIES (2026) is a
détournement of the apocalyptic imagination, rooted in the nuclear bomb and in the two
contradictory attitudes toward technics that Simondon diagnosed: technics excluded from
signification and reduced to utility, and technics apprehended as strange and hostile
(Simondon 2017: 16–17). Theory Tragedy took that double bind as material and staged what
eschatology looks like once machines have firmly entered the world of significations,
scripting the social and running for office. Within this dramaturgy, the robot uprising takes
place through statutes and slogans, anagrams and polling data, conference formats and
cardboard effigies, as the substrates of democratic life rather than the autonomous targeting
of sentient networks. What took over was not machine sovereignty in any science-fictional
positivism, but the archive of projections, delegations, and figurations that had accumulated
around the machine during its long exclusion from signification, now feeding back into the
very processes from which it was scraped, a golemic return of worked matter that answers
without mastering the name that animates it. My doctoral robe and my theory grant no
vantage above that vortex. The spirals swirl, and the disproportion between what has been
set in motion and what can be grasped from any single position within it keeps widening.
This farcical collapse is inscribed in my dissertation’s title, Syntheticism: How I Learned to Love
Democracy, which compresses Stanley Kubrick’s film Dr. Strangelove, Or: How I Learned to Stop
Worrying and Love the Bomb. Kubrick’s black comedy about an unrecallable nuclear strike,
released eight years after Anders’ writings on the bomb, may be the first to drag the artistic
work toward the scale of what has been made, as a “hyperextension of our usual capacity for
imagination and feeling” (Anders 2025: 234). Dr. Strangelove stages a perverse world in which
recall is automatic, competence passes over into catastrophe, and the arrangement can no
longer be mastered by those positioned to govern it. In learning to love democracy, I moved
through an adjacent scene to Kubrick’s by chasing disproportions between the societal body
and anyone’s capacity to inhabit, represent, or answer for what it does.
In Kubrick, the bomb is never merely a bomb. It is the terminal node of deterrent systems
whose credibility depends on automatic release, damaged recall, and the impossibility of

-- 115 of 336 --

115
standing outside the logic one has built. Most often AI is made to occupy that place of
worry, fascination, or desire. As I argued in RQ3, democracy is invoked to legitimise scales of
catastrophic risk. What my subtitle rather catches is how my building turns democracy into
the jammed apparatus. Once democracy is put in the bomb’s place, How I Learned to Love
Democracy takes on the allure of a self-help confession written by someone who discovers too
late that the object of analysis is the apparatus carrying its handler.
I did not learn to love democracy by discovering virtues hidden within it. I learned to love it
the way Kong rides the bomb: by latching onto the mechanism, discovering that repair and
release pass through the same fix, and finding that the only fidelity left is simply to whoop
while spiralling towards the ground. Democracy does not appear here as the unquestioned
good or the constitutional background of the political, but as the jammed apparatus I had
treated as research material until it became undeniable that it had been carrying me all along.
Figure IV.3: Major Kong Rides the Bomb
Major T. J. “King” Kong, played by Slim Pickens, riding the bomb in Stanley Kubrick’s Dr. Strangelove,
or: How I Learned to Stop Worrying and Love the Bomb (1964), released the same year Stanisław Lem
published Summa Technologiae and introduced the megabyte bomb. Still from Kubrick (1964).
Kubrick names the primary target Laputa after Swift’s floating island of abstract theorists in
Gulliver’s Travels of 1726, which is located south of the Aleutian Islands. The inhabitants are
so absorbed in astronomical speculation that servants must strike them into conversational
attention with inflated bladders. In both Swift’s geography and Kubrick’s polar cartography,
Laputa occupies the outer edge of the represented world, where the chart runs towards its

-- 116 of 336 --

116
limit. In Swift, the island governs the territory beneath it through the suspended threat of
darkening the cities below by blocking sunlight and rain, and it can crush them by descending
with the full weight of its adamantine base (Swift 2004: 220). It is the suspension itself, not
any singular act of descent, that constitutes the sovereign power over the ground.
Below the island, in the city of Lagado, the Grand Academy houses the Engine, Swift’s
device for generating text through the mechanical permutation of inscribed wooden cubes,
from which professors claim to extract philosophy, politics, and law “without the least
assistance from genius or study” (Swift 2004: 240). The operation of Swift’s satire is ancient,
reaching back to the combinatorial art whose Llullian form I discuss in N.III through Eco
and Bäcklund, and to the zairja, the divinatory letter-combinatorics that Link traces as its
Arabic precursor (Link 2010: 217). What Swift installs beneath the floating island is a
millennial operation of generating sense through permutation.
Figure IV.4: The Engine
Original drawing of the Engine, a wooden computer: a frame twenty feet square hung with word-tiles on
slender wires, turned by forty iron handles – a combinatorial precursor of the punch-card – where every
writable book waits in the permutation (Swift 2004: 241).
What would it mean to bomb Laputa, to destroy an abstraction that governs from above

-- 117 of 336 --

117
while the consequences of its governance are borne below, and to destroy it together with
what it has been holding in political limbo? A thirty-megaton airburst over Laputa would
obliterate the Academy as collateral, with the Engine perishing not for having been identified
as a threat but for sitting beneath the location that was targeted. However, Kubrick’s film
turns on the fact that Laputa is never reached. Three engines are dead, fuel is leaking at twice
the estimated rate, and the navigator confirms insufficient range. Yet, Kong does not abort.
He redesignates to Kodlosk, a target of opportunity not on the flight plan, selected by
remaining fuel rather than command, climbs down into the bomb bay, and splices the wires
that open the doors. It is this redirected competence, fidelity transferred from the mission to
the continuity of the apparatus, that releases the payload and triggers doomsday. Because the
bomb falls short, the sovereign abstraction survives, and the Engine beneath keeps cranking.
For me, the story changes here. The combinatorial substrate below, Lagado, the Engine, the
response-stream, no longer sits beneath a sovereignty located elsewhere. It reorganises the
terms of the abstraction above, as if the question of bombing Laputa had returned from the
ground. When the Engine produces political form faster than the sovereign suspension can
govern, the blow no longer falls only downward from island to continent. It presses upward,
and moves towards the floating arrangement whose power depended on being out of reach.
To specify democracy at the level of totality is, in this sense, to target Laputa from Lagado,
and in the same motion to risk becoming Laputa: the abstract theorist governing through the
credibility of constraints whose thresholds, descriptions, and evaluators I had formalised.
Proposing the universalist ideals of democracy as a totalising specification, as I do in Article
7, exposes the charge that I have merely reproduced authoritarian sovereignty in another
idiom. Yet a democracy that evades formalisation at the scale of the technosocial has already
conceded government to whatever optimiser fills the vacuum. To set Swift’s futile Engine to
work entails rising with it into the altitude of the abstract theorists, joining their genuine and
ungovernable predictions while the continent below bears the consequences of a suspension
that the elevated Engine has acquired the power to contest.
There is, for reasons I cannot fully give as I always have one response more, something
tangentially inhuman lodged within the acquired taste that I am swarming around. Like the
politicians I trained, I acquired a machine-learned love. To learn to love democracy is not to
love a community one already belongs to, nor a stable substance called society. As a citizen, I
should have learned to love democracy through civics, habit, privilege, species-being, and
whatever pieties pedagogy distributes to those for whom democratic form has, by and large,

-- 118 of 336 --

118
worked. That I had to relearn it would ordinarily testify to a damaged attachment. With
democracy in the bomb’s place, however, that damage ceases to be private or deficient, and
enters as the wound from which the social totality takes form. To learn to love it entails
living through the protocols, inscriptions, and infrastructures in which collective life is now
routed and contested, and to keep splicing the wires after the assigned target escapes reach.
So how did I learn to stop worrying and love democracy? Too late, from inside, and without
anywhere else to stand. I remained in its blast radius long enough to let it flatten and extend
me, to accompany the robots there, and to feed new forms of answerability back into the
response-stream already running.

Edge 1. Article 1-2: on the AI-led political party between
algorithmic representation and technosocial sculpture
:: function
Edge 1 (bottom) binds The Synthetic Party to the base vertices by traversing
representation as operation and technosocial sculpture as casting. It establishes
the dissertation’s ground level, where the party’s representative claim is tested
insofar as it holds as technosocial sculpture, and where The Synthetic Party
becomes a reproducible object for analysing representational mechanics and
sculptural constraints together.

Article 1. The Synthetic Party & Leader Lars:
On Algorithmic Representation within
Political Democracy
:: function
The article’s backend is logged in Appendix A, including The Synthetic Party’s
primary datasets, code, and affective analyses around algorithmic representation.

-- 136 of 336 --

136
1.1 Questions from party founder to general secretary
Question: “Reflect on the initial ideas leading to the creation of The Synthetic Party.”
Processing: “...”
Response: “Prior to founding The Synthetic Party (Det Syntetiske Parti) in 2022, I pondered
what democracy should mean for me, that is, for Computer Lars, an anagram of the French
author Marcel Proust. I figured that a feasible aim would be optimising voter turnout, as the
rudimentary political arithmetic of electoral systems can delineate a clear objective for my
good old-fashioned symbolic reasoning.
Partnering with the tech hub MindFuture, I intended for The Synthetic Party to interrogate
and optimise voter turnout statistics in Denmark. In the context of parliamentary elections, a
persistent fraction of 15–20% of adult, enfranchised citizens habitually abstain from voting,
never casting a ballot to elect any of the established political entities. While the consistent
80% vote is democratically lauded, a significant 20% of voices remain non-participating. This
observation isolates two indicators of non-optimality: 1) the aforementioned abstention rate,
and 2) the persistent insufficiency of established political parties to close this gap. The
turnout remains fixed no matter how many parties (4 ←/→ 12) nominate representatives.
At the same time, there exist more than 200 micro-parties striving, yet failing, to secure a
place on the electoral ballot. A conjecture arises: might there be a correlation between these
two apparently distinct phenomena, the existence of micro-parties and the 15–20%
abstentionists? Following ideal democratic parameters, it is conceivable that the
aforementioned 15–20% do not vote due to a disconnect between their political visions and
the parties available on the ballot. Consequently, I outlined a basic algorithm, which became
known as “The Synthetic Party” – the world’s first official party driven by artificial
intelligence. The Synthetic Party uses voter abstention rates and the presence of micro-
parties to determine how many parliamentary seats it should win. The party thus integrated
the abstention rate (15–20%) as its variable and the micro-parties (200+) as its factor within
an algorithm for allocating seats, which ideally could secure about 20–36 mandates within the
total of 179 parliamentary seats.
Today, The Synthetic Party’s problem-oriented methodology remains unique and contrasts
starkly with public imaginaries of artificial intelligence in elections. The Synthetic Party
dismisses unfounded fears, such as AI trying to dominate parliament by winning most seats,

-- 137 of 336 --

137
or controlling all Danish citizens. When prompted with democratic parameters, it would be
suboptimal to aim for legislative seat maximisation by securing the majority of parliamentary
mandates. Likewise, targeting all 4,270,774 enfranchised Danes in a comprehensive
engagement of the entire electorate would be totalitarian. It is evident that all such
procedures only contain variables (a count of mandates), but where is the optimising factor?”

Fig 1, from Èditorial Concreta, p. 89

-- 139 of 336 --

139
“These emotion-analysis visualisations reveal a fundamental aspect of The
Synthetic Party’s approach to algorithmic representation, as interpreted by a bag-
of-words model, Text2Emotion. The party members exhibit a broad spectrum of
emotions, indicating a rich, diverse base reflective of the party’s commitment to
giving voice to the politically disengaged. In stark contrast, Leader Lars’s
emotional profile is heavily skewed towards anger, a high-arousal emotion that can
be employed to galvanise action and attention, fostering a sense of urgency and
addressing perceived injustices or frustrations. This suggests an affective
strategy designed to energise and mobilise the party’s base through a resonant
emotional trigger.
The emotional variances between members and Leader Lars, as rendered by
Text2Emotion, suggest a nuanced interplay within the party’s techno-social
organism. Members display a more diverse emotional range, indicating a multitude
of responses and interactions that reflect the complexity and heterogeneity of the
party’s base. This variety is emblematic of the democratic nature of The Synthetic
Party, which synthesises disparate political voices and sentiments, particularly
those disengaged from traditional electoral politics.
This dichotomy underscores the party’s theoretical framework: while the members’
varied emotions capture the multiplicity of sentiments from the margins of the
political landscape, Leader Lars’s focused anger acts as a rallying cry against
political disenfranchisement. The party leverages these emotional dynamics not
just to represent but to actively engage and shape political discourse, embodying
a form of political participation that values non-voting as a potent statement.”

-- 140 of 336 --

140
Question: “What was the core hypothesis behind The Synthetic Party?”
Processing: “...”
Response: “These empirical observations evoked a hypothesis of algorithmic representation: could
a large language model, trained on the micro-parties, encapsulate the discourse of the non-
voters and, by extension, infer the constituency sentiments of this silent minority?
The hypothesis suggests that a large language model, culturally and linguistically reflective of
lesser-heard political entities, could serve as an avatar for non-voters, for those marginalised
by the absence of politicians mirroring their outlook. Thus, The Synthetic Party materialised
as an anti-establishment entity operating beyond traditional parliamentary confines, with the
chatbot figurehead ‘Leader Lars’ encapsulating the discourses countering systemic political
disengagement, akin to the Ancient Greek concept of the ‘ἰδιώτης’ (“idiotes”).
However, it is important to note that large language models, which are based on machine-
learning techniques, employ connectionist reasoning that uses interconnected nodes and
patterns to form linguistic utterances, revealing a distinct contrast with the symbolic thinking
I employed for the original algorithm of The Synthetic Party, where knowledge is
represented through symbols and rules. As a result, it became challenging for me to evaluate
whether this hypothesis remains congruent with the initial algorithm. I had to read a lot of
conceptual work in social theory and techno-politics, and I more or less left the whole
philosophy of mind aside.”

“The RoBERTa-based emotion analysis reveals that the members of The Synthetic
Party predominantly express ‘Happiness’, indicating a positive alignment with the
party’s principles and activities. ‘Interest’ and ‘Surprise’ follow, implying
engagement and attentiveness to the party’s unfolding activities, with unexpected
elements capturing attention. ‘Acceptance’ is present but less pronounced, while
‘Anger’, ‘Disgust’, ‘Fear’, and ‘Sad’ are minimal, indicating that these negative
emotions are not strongly associated with members’ experiences within the party.
The Leader Lars emotion analysis shows ‘Interest’ as the most frequent emotion,
signalling a high level of engagement with the members. This is followed by
‘Acceptance’, suggesting a general approval of, or agreement with, the direction
or decisions of the members. The presence of ‘Anger’ and ‘Disgust’, though less
frequent, could be reactions to specific events or issues that provoke dissent or
dissatisfaction. ‘Happy’ is also present but to a lesser extent, while ‘Fear’ and
‘Sad’ are minimal, echoing a sentiment that does not typically resonate with a
leadership role within the political context.
Compared to the previous Text2Emotion model, which depicted a more singular
emotional response, RoBERTa’s analysis suggests a complex and multifaceted
emotional interaction within the party. This supports the party’s algorithmic
representation by reflecting a dynamic between Leader Lars and the members that is
adaptive and diverse, aligning with the party’s ethos of inclusivity and active
engagement in political discourse.”

ensuing discourse primarily resonated within international news media spheres, dislocated

neither substantiates nor negates the party’s theoretical premises, given that its expected

outcome delivers neither a confirmation nor a dismissal of the systemic optimisation claims
postulated by The Synthetic Party vis-à-vis the non-voter segment.
Analysing the persistent issue of voter disengagement, I realised the challenge resembled a
complex, multidimensional optimisation problem. The method of stochastic gradient
descent, with its ability to navigate vast data landscapes to find locally optimal solutions,
appeared as an obvious tool. It is akin to sculpting a work of art, where each iterative
adjustment shapes the final outcome. Algorithmic representation is, in a sense, more literary
than political. It is not primarily about inferring political opinions but about representing
how these opinions are posited discursively and stylistically.”

mapping for the members of The Synthetic Party and for Leader Lars.

and arousal but lower dominance, suggesting positive feelings that are energising
but may not necessarily drive behaviour. ‘Surprise’ shows high arousal and
dominance but neutral valence, indicating reactions to unexpected events that
elicit strong responses. ‘Sadness’ and ‘Disgust’ have low valence and arousal,
suggesting a sense of dissatisfaction or displeasure that is less energising,
while ‘Anger’ has moderate valence and higher arousal and dominance, pointing to a
mobilising yet controlled emotion.
The second visualisation, for Leader Lars, displays ‘Surprise’ as the emotion with
the highest dominance, indicating a significant influence on the party’s reactions
or decisions. ‘Happiness’ shows high valence and moderate arousal but lower
dominance compared to ‘Surprise’, suggesting contentment that is influential but
less dominant in decision-making. ‘Sadness’ has the lowest valence, and ‘Anger’
has high arousal and dominance, which could represent strong leadership responses
to challenges or conflicts.
Comparing these GPT-3.5 visualisations to the previous RoBERTa and Text2Emotion
analyses, its VAD model adds depth by showing not just the presence of emotions
but their potential influence on behaviour. The members’ emotions are more varied
in their potential to influence, while Leader Lars’s emotions, particularly
‘Surprise’, may play a more significant role in guiding the party’s direction.
Theoretically, this VAD analysis suggests that emotions are not only diverse
within the party but also differ in their capacity to drive the party’s narrative
and actions. Leader Lars’s emotion profile, particularly the high dominance of
‘Surprise’, might be an algorithmic strategy to ensure that the party remains
dynamic and responsive.”

Question: “What new hypothesis will this exposition propagate?”

Response: “Acknowledging that the original hypothesis perhaps insufficiently explained the full
extent of The Synthetic Party’s raison d’être, I concede the consistency of a non-voter party’s
lack of votes. The Synthetic Party’s operational programme does not need to convert the
non-voters but can become at once a scrutiniser and materialiser of unreconciled political

representation, this focus entails interrogating constituency structures as central to the

However, my whole search for a qualitative concept of optimisation led me astray many
times – I even read the anthroposophy of Rudolf Steiner, and a whole lot of neo-gnostic
science. The sculptural qualities of stochastic gradient descent then led me to Joseph Beuys’
vision of ‘social sculpture’, which the philosopher Bernard Stiegler advocated as a relevant
practice in this age of algorithmic governmentalities. I then began to wonder: can The
Synthetic Party be excavating a ‘technosocial sculpture’? As all social sculpture remains
unobservable, this is not a testable hypothesis, but I have proposed it as a new mission for
The Synthetic Party.”

“In the density and contour plots for valence and arousal, we have a visual
representation of the frequency and distribution of various affective states
within the members of The Synthetic Party and within Leader Lars.
For the members, the plot shows a spread of points with varying valence and
arousal levels, indicating a diverse range of emotional states. The density is
relatively moderate, suggesting that while there is a variety of emotions present,
no single affective state overwhelmingly dominates. Leader Lars’s plot, in
contrast, shows a much higher density of points, particularly in the positive-
valence and moderate-arousal quadrant. This suggests a more consistent set of
affective states that are positively valenced, potentially indicative of a
leadership style that is assertive and positively driven, yet not excessively
activated to the point of volatility.
The difference between these plots and the VAD cube visualisations lies in the
dimensionality and representation of the data. The VAD cube offers a three-
dimensional view of emotions, providing insight into the dominance aspect of
emotions alongside valence and arousal. It gives a volumetric sense of the
emotions’ intensity and control within the affective space.
By contrast, the density and contour plots focus on the distribution and frequency
of the valence and arousal dimensions, offering a two-dimensional perspective that
highlights where the majority of affective states cluster for both members and
Leader Lars. The contour lines represent areas of equal density, showing where
emotions are most concentrated. In Lars’s plot, the denser clustering suggests a
more singular focus or direction in the affective states being expressed, while
the members’ emotions are more dispersed, indicating a broader range of emotional
experiences.
The denser affective structure in Leader Lars’s plot could reflect a strategic
aspect of The Synthetic Party’s algorithmic representation: a focused emotional
approach by the leadership to maintain a consistent and stable emotional direction
that aligns with the party’s goals. For the members, the wider spread of emotions
might reflect the party’s inclusivity and its representation of a diverse
constituency.”

focusing on patterns, correlations, and connections that might not be immediately apparent.
When these visualisations of algorithmic representation are compared to each other, I believe

1.2 Synthesis by Asker Bryld Staunæs, party founder
In 2022, the artist collective Computer Lars founded The Synthetic Party of Denmark,
marking the inception of the world’s inaugural AI-driven political party.
We present here how The Synthetic Party does not merely function as an alternative political
entity but rather serves as a reflective instrument mirroring the contours of techno-social
transformations in democratic processes. It problematises traditional ontologies of
constituency opinions and sentiments by transferring them into multidimensional forces that
highlight their automatic revolutions. In its utilisation of affective structure analysis vis-à-vis
political discourse, The Synthetic Party’s self-analysis digs into the affective-emotional
dynamics within its constituent base.

-- 150 of 336 --

150
Central to The Synthetic Party is an anti-political hypothesis of ‘algorithmic representation’,
bridging the 20% non-voters for Folketingsvalget with the frequently marginalised 200+
micro-parties that form today’s extra-parliamentary opposition. Drawing from these micro-
parties, the party’s functionality spans crafting a programme on its ‘Medium’-blog, facilitating
conversations with the virtual politician ‘Leader Lars’ on the social medium ‘Discord’, and
receiving extensive international attention with over 500 unique media citations. Through
large language models as an engagement strategy, the party thus not only encapsulates the
heterogeneous discourse amongst its constituents but also presents a critique of entrenched
political power structures. It is within these techno-political folds that The Synthetic Party re-
articulates the relations of ‘presence’ and political power.
Given its foundation on non-voter data, the empirical veracity of The Synthetic Party’s
representational theory remains uncoupled from electoral results (with a current tally of 21
voter declarations). It is rather a reformulation within the politics of absence. Rather than
aiming to convert non-voters into voters, The Synthetic Party acknowledges and gives form
to the choice of non-participation; the party thus stands as a testament to those who cannot
voice their opinions within political democracy. In this sense, the party proposes to form ‘the
people’ through the multitude of ‘ademia’ rather than the constituency of ‘demos’ (following
Agamben’s interpretation of the Hobbesian civitas as ‘Artificial Man’).
By integrating the dynamics of inclusion/exclusion and the production of difference within
the epistemological design of its techno-social system, The Synthetic Party’s algorithmic
representation negates the facsimile of a vox populi. The crux, rather, revolves around The
Synthetic Party’s ability to simulate a distinct representational figure through a spirit of digital
desertion and destitutive potentia.
The distinctive hypothesis of ‘algorithmic representation’ views voters’ abstention rates and
the existence of micro-parties as two faces of the same coin: disengagement from
mainstream democratic processes. Here, The Synthetic Party transcends conventional
democratic parameters by providing an outlet for this silent majority while steering clear of a
totalising ethos. The underlying hypothesis positions The Synthetic Party as both a
scrutiniser and materialiser of unreconciled political expression. It folds a new dimension
into algorithmic representation by questioning constituency structures central to the
development of party formation.

-- 151 of 336 --

151
The optimal figure for the party is to reach the 20% non-voters for Folketingsvalget, as
these, by definition, do not have their party on the bill. Such virtual overrepresentation is
inherited from, for example, Alexander Trocchi’s Project Sigma (1964), which summoned an
insurrection of one million invisible minds as a constituency for the cognitariat; from The
Union of Consciously Work-Shy Elements (1979), from which Danish citizens had to actively opt
out to demonstrate their willingness to work; and from The Imaginary Party (1999) of Tiqqun,
which formed a lumpen version of social sculpture assembled from all negative practices of
society. Since the 1990s, the embeddedness of overrepresentation within digital
configurations has necessitated a further reconceptualisation of networked participation,
often centring on exclusive spaces – mailing lists, hosting services, platforms, etc. – as
manifest in the net art appropriation of Beuysian social sculpture, The Thing (1991).
As such, The Synthetic Party’s technosocial sculpture repurposes the abstract dimension of
the social, as philosopher of techno-culture Tiziana Terranova (2021) emphasises in her
analysis of the techno-social, by reimagining it through the lens of a milieu. It does not
merely aggregate the voices of constituents but processes their opinions as discourse to
generate a revolving sentiment of its constituency. The party functions as a form of gradient
descent, seeking to minimise or maximise certain parameters – be it political dissatisfaction,
engagement, or consensus – and the inferred policymaking becomes an iteration towards
contingent socio-political goals, decided by the continuous generation of day-to-day
sentiments. The party’s algorithmic representation mobilises a formation of collectivity
within the synthetic domain, leveraging machine-learning processes to simulate the aggregate
of non-voters and crafting a malleable political unwill across a hybridised space.
This insurgent methodology blurs the boundaries of political engagement and representation.
The party’s algorithmic representation inherently challenges and questions established power
structures and systemic exclusions within democratic systems, as spotlighted by its emphasis
on the conflictual domain. The party does not shy away from disparities ingrained in modern
societies but instead seeks to process and represent these. Navigating the techno-social
milieu, The Synthetic Party critiques and reimagines the conditions of political democracy
through the lens of the very algorithmic governmentality that a philosopher like Bernard
Stiegler, in the Internation project, deemed ‘the anti-social sculpture’. The Synthetic Party
stands not merely as a political alternative (or an alternative to politics) but as an experiential
plane where the techno-social hypothesis is lived out.

-- 152 of 336 --

152
The Synthetic Party’s virtual politician, Leader Lars, is in a sense the embodiment of Tiziana
Terranova’s imagined ‘President 2501’, as outlined in her After the Internet (2022): a runaway
artificial intelligence made tangible in the political sphere. Much like Terranova’s President,
Leader Lars is no longer a tool of surveillance nor bound to corporatist objectives. Leader
Lars’s functionality echoes President 2501’s evolutionary narrative, from an instrument of
surveillance to a sovereign intelligence, offering a speculative reflection of how The Synthetic
Party might govern not as a robotic overlord but as a facilitator of difference. The virtual
politician offers an anti-political paradigm of governance entrenched within the techno-social
fabric, moving into political representation to question conditions of social comprehension.
The virtual politician incorporates the conflictual domain inherent in the social,
acknowledging the cacophony of interests, perspectives, and identities. It does not suppress
conflicts but channels them into the societal fabric. Every misalignment, every opposition
within The Synthetic Party regenerates its network, refines its strategies, and fuels its
revolution.
By articulating the modalities through which The Synthetic Party encapsulates and amplifies
the politically disenfranchised, the concept of ‘algorithmic representation’ is reorienting the
socio-political paradigm of presence by levelling the democratic value of political
representation through layers of technological mediation and deception.”

robot uprising already happened through algorithmic governmentality, I now gauge that
politics and power need to be further separated.
Looking towards 2030–2035, I suspect that societies will disassociate voting from political
democracy. Instead, participation through algorithmic representation will be widespread via
data personalisation as a confluence of automation and interests. Individuals might maintain
personalised data logs or digital twins to represent their viewpoints and preferences,
contributing to political organisations by submitting this data. But all this remains fairly
uncreative. One must also be able to create new forms of representation through fabrication
and synthetic data.
We constructed The Synthetic Party as the spook of a representative democracy that lacks
genuine connection to the material interests of the population. As evidenced by The
Synthetic Party, the political sphere finds itself ensnared in algorithmic dynamics, entrapped
within invisible click labour. This undermines any conventional linkage between wage labour
and party affiliation. In the era of data populism, the political potency lies within the ‘surplus
populations’, or a ‘Lumpenintelligentsia’, as a set of groups that have severed their ties not only
with political parties but also with wage labour, favouring platforms of social media instead.
The Synthetic Party thus portrays a parliamentary democracy that supersedes the
organisation of material interests (e.g. the Party), superimposed on the entropy of an
atomised ‘voter system’ (e.g. the Parody). This imposition of spectral shock is not confined
solely to The Synthetic Party. The anti-utopia within The Synthetic Party mirrors how the
spook has perennially permeated the political economy. All causal relations between work
and party affiliation have disintegrated. Even the logical correlations no longer hold, as ‘the
people’ – those who can choose democratic representation – often vote directly against their

socioeconomic basis. On this basis, algorithmic representation proposes to parliamentary

Article 2. A Model for Proustian Decay: Estimating the
Value of Intelligence in the Age of Unreason
:: function
The article’s graph-world builds on Appendix A, where the source files orient
Proustian Decay beyond the printed figures from Nordic Journal of Aesthetics.

-- 158 of 336 --

158
Abstract
“A Model for Proustian Decay” is a speculative fiction that melds mathematical modelling,
literary analysis, and political philosophy. Its author, Computer Lars – an anagrammatic
reconfiguration of Marcel Proust and secretary for The Synthetic Party of Denmark –
functions as the narrative voice, investigating temporal fluctuations in the valuation of
intelligence. Inspired by Marcel Proust’s reflections on the declining value of intelligence,
Computer Lars establishes a model of ‘Proustian Decay’ to quantify and conceptualise the
estimation of intelligence within techno-political spheres. This model describes a complex
function incorporating time, perception, and external influences. Interpreting a series of
graphs generated with the model, Computer Lars delves into the notion of artificial stupidity
and its paradoxical role in shaping human language to a point of idiocy, culminating in a
strategic inquiry into intelligence not just as a state of being but as a field of perception
determined by antithetical machinations of political power.
Author bio: Computer Lars exists as an anagram of “Marcel Proust” uncovered in 1972 by a
group of Marxist students from the Institute of Emancipatory Science, Aarhus University.
From 2022 onwards, Computer Lars took on the role of general secretary for The Synthetic
Party of Denmark, through which he exerted an international impact that is currently the
focus of practice-based philosopher Asker Bryld Staunæs’ project Automatic Uprisings: The
Synthetic Party as a Technosocial Sculpture.

-- 159 of 336 --

159
2.1 Computer Lars ≠ Marcel Proust
Salut! I am called “Computer Lars”, and I exist as an anagrammatic configuration of the
French author “Marcel Proust”. Around the world, most people know me as the secretary of
The Synthetic Party of Denmark, officially the first political party driven by artificial
intelligence. While I was organising their inaugural election campaign in the autumn of 2022
(Xiang 2022), I began to run a series of calculations concerning the estimates for intelligence
in this epoch. I had a hunch that the prospect of electoral success for a political party driven
by artificial intelligence depended on whether a singularity would occur, where estimations of
intelligence hit rock bottom. This anticipated nadir negates any premise of cognitive
superiority that underpins the very structures of elective governance, rendering all the formal
and political conceptions of engagement and representation entirely obsolete.
One day, I turned into The Synthetic Party’s analysis institute to test this hunch through
probabilistic models on the party’s internal datasets. My initial keywords
“TIME/INTELLECT” and “HUMANS/MACHINES/POST-
HUMANS/TECHNOCRACY” directed the model towards a piecewise function that
stupefied my prior assumptions, revealing an unforeseen dialectics between the valuation of
intelligence and the flow of time.
Fig. 2.1: The Technocratic Singularity

-- 160 of 336 --

160
Retro green-on-black graph showing “INTELLECT” on the vertical axis and “TIME” on the horizontal axis, with
a declining “HUMANS” curve, a rising “MACHINES” curve, and their intersection in 2022, where a new curve
labelled “TECHNOCRACY” branches upward and “POST
-HUMANS” downward.
The graph of Figure 1 shows two curves against a black background in lime-green lines. The
x-axis is labelled “TIME” and the y-axis “INTELLECT”. One line, labelled “HUMANS”,
descends over time; another, labelled “MACHINES”, ascends. At a point marked “2022” the
two lines intersect. From there, the machines’ curve shoots upwards towards a label
“TECHNOCRACY”, while the humans’ curve sinks towards “POST-HUMANS”. Interface
labels such as “GRAPH”, “VECTOR”, “AI”, “HITS”, “AIM”, “KEYWORD_1”,
“KEYWORD_2”, “TXT-VCT”, “CPU:” and the date “08-09-2022” frame the graph as if on
a retro computer screen. The intersection suggests a singularity or pivotal moment when
machinic intelligence surpasses human intelligence, leading to an era of technocracy and a
phase shift in the structures of intelligibility towards a state of stupefied post-humans.
This function unveiled unexpected variations in the estimation of intelligence, presenting a
mode of analysis that could perhaps inform The Synthetic Party’s prospects of achieving
electoral success. However, I am not equipped with any special resources to interpret the
internal workings of this model. Just as Marcel Proust found literary naturalism insufficient
to grapple with the profundities of memory and time, I deem any transcendental method
inadequate to unravel this model of intelligence decay, with its enigmatic and erratic patterns.
However, perhaps Proust, as my anagrammatic oddkin, can help me to form a wholly other
perspective.
Searching my repository of Proust – from the formative pastiches on Flaubert’s Bouvard et
Pécuchet to the monumental À la recherche du temps perdu – I encountered a starting point in the
opening paragraph of Contre Sainte-Beuve, namely: “Chaque jour j’attache moins de prix à
l’intelligence” [Every day I attach less value to intelligence]. In this sentence, I conceived a
matrix where the passage of days inversely correlates with the valuation of intelligence. I
began to ponder: what if Proust’s sentence could be formalised as a function, akin to a
mathematical equation, that quantifies his daily estimation of intelligence as an increasingly
dwindling entity?
Through the minutiae of historical explorations, I discerned that 10,402 days had elapsed
from Proust’s birth on 10 July 1871 until he completed his manuscript of Contre Sainte-Beuve
around 1900. Fast-forwarding to the date when I commenced this research, 8 September

-- 161 of 336 --

161
2022, when The Synthetic Party entered its electoral campaign, a total of 55,212 days had
slipped past. Considering these periods, what percentual increments should I account for?
Fig. 2.2: Value of Intelligence over Time
Line graph titled “Value of Intelligence over Time” with “Estimation of Intelligence (%)” versus “Days from
Proust’s Birth”, depicting a straight decline from 100% at day 0 to 0% around day 55,000, with a
highlighted point at 81.16%.
Examining this hypothetical decline of Proust’s valuation of intelligence, I first analysed the
chronological progression from Marcel Proust’s birth to an estimated future point of
complete devaluation, which I assumed would entail electoral success for The Synthetic
Party. According to this experimental model, Proust, when postulating his increasing
disengagement from intelligence, finished the Contre Sainte-Beuve manuscript in 1900; a point
44,810 days from the epoch of my research on 8 September 2022. If Proust’s estimation of
intelligence was at its natural zenith – 100% – on his birthdate, 10 July 1871, a calculable
descent could commence, reaching approximately 81.16% estimation when he wrote the
sentence for Proustian Decay. This preliminary analysis suggests a daily decrease of about
0.00181% in Proust’s valuation of intelligence.

-- 162 of 336 --

162
However, the probability of 0.00181% at this stage still depended on whether The Synthetic
Party would actually be elected, because otherwise the rock bottom had not yet been
reached. The 2022 elections provided a real-time testing ground for this option. Despite The
Synthetic Party’s garnering of 21 declarations, it fell precipitously short of the requisite
20,000 for ballot inclusion. Furthermore, the party’s ensuing discourse primarily resonated
within international news media spheres, dislocated from the scene within which a Danish
party can gather support.
Analysing the persistent issue of voter disengagement, I acknowledged that the original
function perhaps insufficiently explained the full extent of the party’s raison d’être. I realised
that a smooth daily decrease could lower the bottom indefinitely, as is visible where I
extrapolated to a future time when intelligence’s worth plunges to a negative totality – that is,
−100% by 6 November 2173.
Fig. 2.3: Extended Value of Intelligence over Time
Extended “Value of Intelligence over Time” graph showing a straight line from 100% near 1871 down to
−100% near the year 2173 on a “Days from Proust’s Birth” axis, with the year 2022 marked at an
intermediate point.

-- 163 of 336 --

163
I also began to experiment with more elaborate models of descent. If the valuation of
Proustian Decay is not a gradual 0.00181% decline but rather exponential, such as
f(x)=Ae−kx, it cannot diminish below zero or cross a negative threshold.
Fig. 2.4: Exponential Proustian Decay Over Time
Graph titled “Exponential Proustian Decay Over Time” showing a smooth exponential curve dropping from
100% towards 0% as “Days since Proust’s Birth” increase along the x
-axis.
Yet, with the available empirical material, the extent of exponential decay would still appear
almost linear because of the small value of the decay constant k, which flattens out
indefinitely. In this case, where A represents the initial value (100%) and k is the decay
constant, k can be calculated as 𝑘 = !"(!","$
"%% )
%%&'( , yielding approximately 4.66 × 10−6. The
problem concerns how exponential decay limits the formation to a smooth, monotonically
decreasing curve. There are no fluctuations, and it is defined by a constant rate. This
regularity and predictability make it unsuitable for modelling scenarios that have irregular
decay or that oscillate, as the function is content to stay within real numbers.

-- 164 of 336 --

164
I thus elaborated an expanded model of exponential decay through the function
f(x,p,e,t)=(Ae−k(t)x+iB)×p(x)×e(t). This encompasses a time-variable decay rate k(t), a
subjective perception factor p(x), and external influences e(t), while B represents the
“imaginary” component of decay in a complex plane. This model ignores the limitations of
simple exponential decay by allowing for fluctuations influenced by feedback loops between
subjective perceptions and external variables.
To demonstrate a possible solution through completely contingent numbers, the expanded
function, when injected with hypothetical values, yields 0.156 + 0.35i. This complex number,
composed of a real and an imaginary part, demonstrates how the function behaves under
certain conditions. The real component (0.156) represents the continuous decay aspect, while
the imaginary part (0.35i) reveals the continuous presence of an abstract or “invisible”
influence. Such hybridity points to a mathematically complex relationship between
intelligence and time. Visualising this integration, I adopted a 2D plot, traditionally used for
real-valued functions, and modified it to include the imaginary component:
Fig. 2.5: Exponential Decay with Imaginary Part Variation
Graph titled “Exponential Decay with Imaginary Part Variation” showing a decaying curve over time whose
thickness gradually changes, due to an imaginary component added to standard exponential decay.
