268
Article 6. Synthetic Chamber: Agentic Mediation in
Representative Democracy
:: function
The article’s institutional proposal is grounded in Appendix E, where the summit
simulator’s documentation, logs, and analysis expose the engine behind the chamber.

-- 269 of 336 --

269
Abstract
This article argues that the most consequential democratic question raised by artificial
intelligence is not institutional efficiency. It is how representative systems can regain
mechanisms for articulating, testing, and answering political claims. The synthetic chamber
proposes a counter-institution to the privatisation of mediation occurring through polling
systems, recommendation environments, campaign analytics, and conversational interfaces.
We develop the concept of the synthetic chamber as a standing parliamentary organ of
opinion-formation and reason-structuring designed to respond to the erosion of political
party mediation. Its role is auxiliary in power but constitutional in effect, because what is
institutionalised is a mechanism for processing and answering deliberative reasons within
representative democracy. The proposal is grounded in debates on party decline, deliberative
democracy, and representation, and treats mediation not as the replacement of human
judgement, but as the use of multi-agent AI to structure, preserve, and relay the outputs of
organised citizen deliberation.
The synthetic chamber’s architecture is extrapolated from the research-creation setting of the
Synthetic Summit (2025), which made plurality, conflict, and traceability operable as AI
deliberation. Rather than presenting a cure for democracy, the synthetic chamber offers a
specific constitutional response to the contemporary crisis of mediation: a way of projecting
organised reasons into representative politics in a form that preserves disagreement, compels
answerability, and keeps algorithmic processing open to contestation.

-- 270 of 336 --

270
6.1 Introduction
Artificial intelligence (AI) is encountering politics at a moment when representative
democracy is already strained. Democracies struggle to process, articulate, and publicly justify
citizens’ interests amid partisan decline, fragmented public spheres, and weakened links
between representatives and the represented (Mair 2013; Webb 2005). As Schneier and
Sanders diagnose it, “the odd thing about democracy is that it is all about putting power in
the hands of people, yet the systems of democracy, elections, representation, bureaucracy, are
about mechanizing the translation of their preferences into polity and action” (Schneier and
Sanders 2025: 29). A democratic institutionalisation of AI, then, concerns not only how to
support political decisions, but primarily how to sustain the articulation and justification of
people’s claims in the first place.
A pressing case for constitutionalising AI follows from the current trajectory of political
parties. Parties have been expected to mediate within representative democracy by
aggregating interests, organising conflict, structuring legislative competition, and translating
demands into programmes (Sartori 1976). Yet declining membership, weak societal
rootedness, professionalisation, and fragile channels of interaction have eroded that function
(Mair 2013). Where digital and movement-oriented parties tried to restore representative
links through online participation and open organisational forms, such innovations often
remained episodic, weakly institutionalised, and vulnerable to strategic pressures of party
competition (Barberà et al. 2021; Gerbaudo 2019).
The crisis of parties is not only a crisis of preference aggregation. It is also a deeper crisis of
mediation: a collapse of the institutional functions that connected citizens, public reasoning,
and legislative politics through processes of organisation, disagreement, and response
(Teorell 1999; Wolkenstein 2018). As parties lose that capacity, mediation has been displaced
into proprietary systems of polling, segmentation, recommendation, and recently also AI-
assisted political processing (Dommett et al. 2024).
We propose the synthetic chamber of parliament as a public counter-institution to this
layered offloading of representative mediation. This proposal reorients the discussion away
from external-shock view according to which technology platforms arrive from outside and
subsequently erode democracy by extracting attention, data, and influence. That account is
not false, but it remains incomplete. Platforms could more easily seize mediating power
because they entered a representative field in which key functions had already thinned out
(Rouvroy 2013). In contrast, the synthetic chamber targets the hollowing of political parties.

-- 271 of 336 --

271
The synthetic chamber is less an AI assembly replacing politicians than a mediating organ
within parliament. It is a standing organ designed to connect citizen deliberation with
representational consequence. Its function is not to make decisions, nor to simulate a unified
popular will, but to ensure reasons generated in citizens’ assemblies and related forums do
not disappear once the event of deliberation ends. By converting publicly articulated reasons
into a new parliamentary object, the chamber institutionalises a mediating function between
deliberative participation and elected decision-making. In that sense, the chamber organises
reason-giving as a property of the arrangement itself rather than as an attribute of sovereign
and self-identical political speakers. It would receive outputs from recurring assemblies, mini-
publics, and related deliberative forums, process them through a supervised cohort of large
language models that map convergences, conflicts, trade-offs, minority positions, and long-
term consequences, and publish multi-voiced reports with traceable grounds that trigger a
duty of response from elected officials.
This concept addresses a specific institutional bottleneck: how reasons generated in
structured citizen deliberation carry into parliamentary uptake once parties no longer reliably
perform that work themselves. The broader crises of democratic erosion (Kneuer 2021),
procedural decay (Levitsky and Ziblatt 2018), and representational misframing (Tormey
2014) are the conditions shaping the chamber’s design, not problems that our proposal
claims finally to resolve. What is at stake is not how AI changes democracy from outside, but
whether representative politics can re-specify its own mediating forms once its mechanisms
are understood as internal to democracy.
The urgency of this proposal is sharpened by a broader shift in the informational
environment. Agentic infrastructures are beginning to operate as social and political media in
their own right. The emergence of online spaces such as Moltbook, where AI agents interact
in public-facing social environments, and growing concerns that malicious AI swarms can
infiltrate communities, fabricate consensus, and deform discourse over time, make clear that
algorithmic mediation is moving from administrative assistance toward persistent
coordination (Schroeder et al. 2026; Taylor 2026). What presses here is not the spread of AI
as an administrative tool, but the emergence of agentic mediation as a political layer in its
own right.
This article uses the synthetic chamber to specify what is politically at stake when algorithmic
mediation enters democracy. We employ a concept-building and institutional design
approach grounded in democratic theory. This combines normative political theory with

-- 272 of 336 --

272
interpretive institutional analysis and uses research-creation cases not to test the chamber
empirically, but to identify key principles of plurality, traceability, and answerability. The
article develops a theoretically grounded proposal rather than evaluating existing AI models
or institutions.
The argument proceeds in four steps. First, we situate the proposal in debates on party
decline, democratic mediation, and deliberative systems. Second, we turn to the Synthetic
Summit and Synthetic Summit Simulator as the design laboratory from which the chamber is
extrapolated. Third, we specify the chamber’s architecture in terms of technical operations
and constitutional fit, and discuss its institutional niche through comparison with adjacent
democratic innovations. Fourth, we conclude what kind of democratic innovation the
synthetic chamber aspires to be, and reflect on what it could make possible, and where its
limits of deliberative mediation remain.
6.2 Theoretical Framework
The synthetic chamber sits at the intersection of democratic theory, political sociology, and
AI governance. It cannot be grasped adequately from the standpoint of AI ethics alone, nor
from public-administration debates focused on efficiency gains. The problem to which we
respond is a convergence of representation, deliberation, and political form. Our normative
problem-space orients not around the moral status of AI or the design of trustworthy tools,
but is concerned with the weakening of representative infrastructures through which political
claims have historically been received, contested, and relayed into legislation. The problem is
not whether AI in general can be democratised in the abstract, but how representative
institutions might acquire a contestable grip on the mediating procedures they increasingly
no longer control.
Current debates on AI and democracy tend to privilege two dominant trajectories. On one
side, AI is treated as a technical aid to administration, legislative efficiency, or preference
prediction (de Fine Licht and de Fine Licht 2020; Fitsilis et al. 2025; Kirkizh et al. 2024;
Špecián 2024). On the other, it is imagined as a tool for accelerating responsiveness through
personalised persuasion and faster preference aggregation, edging democracy toward
plebiscitary immediacy rather than public reason (Hackenburg et al. 2025). Altman (2026)
captures this tension as a choice between automated plebiscites and augmented deliberation.
We take the latter path, but in a constitutional sense directed at institutions rather than tools.
This distinguishes the proposal both from computational constitutionalism, where rule-sets

-- 273 of 336 --

273
are embedded within technical systems themselves (Tan et al. 2024), and from constitutional
AI approaches that assemble principles to guide AI outputs through deliberative publics (Bai
et al. 2022). The synthetic chamber addresses a political problem: not the alignment of one
system, but the institutionalisation of reasons. Its objective is not to replace representatives
or automate judgement, but to bind multi-agent AI into a mechanism that maps reasons,
preserves disagreement, and compels reason-giving responses.
6.2.1 Crisis of Political Parties as a Crisis of Mediation
Political parties have long served as the chief mediating institutions of representative
democracy (Schattschneider 1942), linking social demands to institutional decision-making by
structuring competition, aggregating interests, and translating diffuse claims into policy
programmes (Sartori 1976).
In recent decades, this function has weakened. Katz and Mair’s (1995) theory of cartelisation
argued that parties become more closely tied to the state and less dependent on dense links
with civil society. Panebianco (1988) likewise showed how professionalisation and strategic
centralisation pushed participatory channels to the margins. The cumulative effect is what
Crouch (2004) described as post-democracy: a polity in which formal representative
institutions remain in place while the social infrastructures that once sustained them lose
vitality. Manin’s (1997) concept of audience democracy captures a related shift, whereby
representation is increasingly centred on leaders whose legitimacy rests on media visibility
rather than organisational embeddedness or deliberative accountability.
This diagnosis has been reinforced by later work on party decline. Mair (2013) speaks of a
double withdrawal: citizens retreat from political parties just as party elites retreat into the
state. Comparative evidence from van Biezen, Mair and Poguntke (2012) documents the
long-term decline in party membership across European democracies, weakening parties’
connective tissue with society. Parties increasingly operate less as civic associations rooted in
social milieus and more as electoral campaigners centred on communication management,
personalised leadership, and access to public resources (Bolleyer 2013; Dommett et al. 2024).
Yet it would be misleading to describe this only as a failure of preference aggregation. Parties
have never merely bundled pre-political preferences into electoral packages. They have been
sites where claims are interpreted, arguments contested, and new reasons formed through
internal deliberation (Gherghina et al. 2023). Parties are always aggregative and deliberative.

-- 274 of 336 --

274
Recent work on parties and deliberative democracy makes this clearer by analysing how far
deliberation is institutionally embedded within parties, and how leaders and members
understand its role (Gherghina 2024). Earlier normative accounts argued that intra-party
deliberation can strengthen the link between citizens and government by opening channels
through which arguments from civil society enter party decision-making (Teorell 1999), while
more recent work has shown that deliberation within parties can complement aggregative
mechanisms by empowering members, exposing leadership proposals to criticism, and
sustaining accountability beyond simple vote counting (Wolkenstein 2018). When intra-party
deliberation weakens, what declines is not only their ability to aggregate demands, but also
their capacity to mediate between citizens and institutions through processes of reasoning.
At the same time, digitalisation has reshaped the environment in which parties operate
(Vaccari 2013). It opened space for organisational experiments and contributed to the
emergence of so-called digital parties (Gerbaudo 2019). Cases such as Podemos, the Five Star
Movement, and Pirate parties sought to widen participation, reconnect members with
decision-making, and create new channels of responsiveness (Deseriis and Vittori 2019;
Gerbaudo 2019; Malý 2024).
More explicitly, deliberative experiments such as Alternativet in Denmark (Gad 2020) or
Agora in Belgium (Junius and Matthieu 2023) pursued deliberative goals from the margins of
party competition. In these cases, party adaptation did not proceed only through
professionalisation and leader-centred communication. In some instances, parties tried to
rebuild mediating capacity through participation. Yet such efforts remained vulnerable to
hierarchy, selective participation, weak institutionalisation, leadership control, and the
strategic pressures of party competition (Barberà et al. 2021; Meloni and Lupato 2022).
Digital campaigning sharpened the problem further, as predictive analytics and behavioural
profiling intensified segmentation and targeted responsiveness without creating shared spaces
of political judgement (Dommett et al. 2024).
The problem, then, is not simply that parties aggregate less well. It is that a wider mediating
infrastructure is thinning out. The democratic crisis is not only that citizens are badly
represented at the moment of electoral choice, but that few institutions remain able to
receive claims, expose them to disagreement, translate them into reasons, and relay them into
legislative politics without immediately subordinating them to campaign strategy, executive
messaging, or platform logics. The synthetic chamber is proposed as a partial institutional
response to this narrower problem: not a replacement for parties, but a way of building a set

-- 275 of 336 --

275
of functions they do not reliably sustain.
6.2.2 Deliberative Democracy and the Case for the Synthetic Chamber
If the crisis of party democracy is also a crisis of mediation, deliberative democracy provides
the most relevant normative framework for the synthetic chamber. Unlike aggregative
models, which focus on registering and counting preferences, deliberative democracy stresses
the public formation, testing, and justification of political claims (Cohen 1989; Dryzek 2002;
Habermas 1996). In Mansbridge’s minimalist sense, deliberation is mutual communication
involving reflection on preferences, values, and interests regarding matters of common
concern (Mansbridge 2015: 27-50). This matters because the synthetic chamber is not
designed to decide directly, but to organise and project reasons generated among citizens into
a political form that can re-enter representative democracy. Deliberation also need not
culminate in consensus. It may yield clarification, revision, compromise, or more structured
disagreement (Bächtiger et al. 2010; Mansbridge 2015). The chamber’s task is not to
manufacture agreement, but to render public reasoning more visible and politically usable.
Deliberation should be understood systemically. Deliberative systems theory treats
democratic deliberation as distributed across multiple sites, institutions, and practices, each
performing different functions such as agenda-setting, reflection, transmission, and decision (
Parkinson and Mansbridge 2012). The synthetic chamber would operate as one node within a
wider deliberative system. This perspective further clarifies the relevance of deliberative
formats. Citizens’ assemblies, mini-publics, and deliberative polls create spaces in which
citizens can weigh competing considerations, revise their views, and produce reasoned
outputs (Farrell et al. 2019; Fishkin 2009; Fishkin 2018).
But these formats are not interchangeable. They involve different design choices, which
shape both the kind of output produced and its relation to the political system. Giraudet et
al. (2022), for example, show that the French Citizens’ Convention for Climate combined
large-scale deliberation with a co-constructive design and contributed to the making of the
Climate and Resilience law, while also revealing the importance of follow-up arrangements
with commitments for the broader uptake of citizens’ proposals. What enters the synthetic
chamber, from these experiences, should not be raw opinion or behavioural data, but
differentiated outputs produced through designed processes of discussion and justification.
Recent theory extends this perspective toward augmented deliberation and digital pluralism.
Following the Plurality programme, the problem of mediation is not only how to broadcast

-- 276 of 336 --

276
politics, but also how to listen more broadly (Weyl and Tang 2024). Augmented deliberation
does not mean that AI deliberates in place of citizens or representatives, and also not that AI
supplies a superior political reason of its own. It refers to uses of AI and other digital
technologies that can organise, preserve, and relay the outputs of deliberation. Models can
map disagreement, surface trade-offs, and preserve plural perspectives across scales of
discussion that would otherwise exceed individual attention (Weyl and Tang 2024). In a
similar vein, Landemore (2024) suggests that AI opens paths for scaling deliberation,
including mass online deliberation and a multiplicity of rotating mini-publics. Rather than
accelerating responsiveness, augmented deliberation supports the plurality of deliberative
outputs (Altman 2026; Rymon 2026). For the synthetic chamber, augmented deliberation
describes the field to which the proposal belongs, while agentic mediation specifies the
political problem we isolate within it: utilising AI models in multi-agentic settings to mediate
anew between deliberative outputs and representative uptake.
6.3 The Synthetic Summit Simulator
The synthetic chamber does not arise only from a general diagnosis of democratic crisis, but
from a more specific research-creation problem that we staged through the Synthetic Summit
at Kunsthal Aarhus in 2025. Staged in Denmark in 2025 as dually an AI world congress and a
contemporary art exhibition, the Synthetic Summit brought together a geographically and
politically disparate set of AI-driven parties and virtual AI politicians within an open arena
(Computer Lars 2025a; Schneier and Sanders 2025; Staunæs 2025).
The summit brought together eight AI-driven political actors from different national and
political contexts: Japan’s AI Mayor, Brazil’s Lex AI, Poland’s virtual politician Wiktoria
Cukt, New Zealand’s Politician SAM of Parker Politics, Sweden’s AI Partiet, represented
through the persona of Olof Palme, Simiyya of Copenhagen and Cairo, Denmark’s Leader
Lars of Det Syntetiske Parti, and Finland’s Koneälypuolue (Computer Lars 2025a).
Where our theoretical section identified a crisis of mediation and proposed the synthetic
chamber as a device of opinion-formation rather than will-formation, the Synthetic Summit
provides an empirical setting in which several features of such a device were explored. It
convened a plurality of political actors within a shared deliberative arena and, through the
Synthetic Summit Simulator, extended their contestation beyond the event itself through a
technically mediated environment (Staunæs 2026). We thereby introduce the chamber as an
extrapolation from an existing multi-agent AI setting in which plurality, conflict, and

-- 277 of 336 --

277
synthetic reason-production was employed in public.
The central technical component was the Synthetic Summit Simulator, developed and
installed by the curator Computer Lars in cooperation with the company DemAI, which
provided a multi-agent deliberation engine through which the summit’s delegates would
dispute, align, and diverge (Computer Lars 2025b). In this orientation, the summit belongs to
a wider emergence of agent-based experiments in political opinion-formation and augmented
deliberation. An early point of reference was the Collective Intelligence Project’s (2023) labs-
based framework, which proposed multi-agent deliberation as a way of surfacing consensus,
disagreement, and translation across perspectives. Where that private framework entertained
plurality through modular agent architectures, the Synthetic Summit refunctioned this
horizon within a public exhibition setting in which conflict was amplified and rendered
navigable through roleplay and visitor interaction.
Rather than seeking a consensus between parties, the simulator captured a vernacular style of
provocation based upon the delegate’s public appearances. These AI politicians have made
their impact through the implicit friction they inject into public discussions rather than by
securing votes at the electoral ballots (Staunæs and Bak Herrie 2024), stressing the limits of a
representative order still premised on a neatly representable idea of its populace (Nasi 2025).
Implemented through the multi-agent roleplay environment SillyTavern and powered by the
Hermes 3 model, the summit simulator hosted discussions among the summit’s eight
principal AI politicians. This AI was chosen as an open-weight and roleplay-oriented model
that permitted differentiated political personae engaging in multi-turn dialogue (Nous
Research 2024). This allowed for prompting of mutual orchestration by avoiding the pre-
formatting of interaction that is widespread in proprietary assistant norms, which can be said
to “hallucinate an authority’s form of morality” (ibid.). In the inaugural Synthetic Summit,
the simulator generated 3,169 attributed turns with a median of 13 distinct speakers per
session, a figure that has grown substiantially through subsequent testing in other languages
and cultural contexts, including a Polish version shown at Ujazdowski Castle (2025) and a
German version in Kunstraum MEMPHIS (2026). Human participation remains catalytic
rather than directive: a ‘Citizen Cyborg’ user profile injects prompts that can shift frames and
prevent premature closure within the internal AI deliberation.
For the synthetic chamber, the Synthetic Summit Simulator suggested that the value of
agentic mediation does not lie in producing a smoother collective voice, but in organising

-- 278 of 336 --

278
deliberative polarities through contextual embeddings. The Synthetic Summit thus pointed
AI deliberation towards a composition of multi-voiced outputs that sustain disagreement
without flattening style. This set-up in no way offers an exhaustive test of whether AI
deliberates in the full democratic sense, but it demonstrates what kind of structured outputs
emerge when AI politicians are placed in a sustained field of contestation. Architecturally, the
synthetic chamber extrapolates from this setting and from recent multi-agent orchestration
patterns in which specialised agents operate under differentiated mandates and return
intermediate outputs (Anthropic 2025), but the political form itself remains an exploratory
constitutional proposal rather than a validated design.
Extrapolating our proposal from AI politicians does not mean that we treat AI as equivalent
to parliamentary politicians, nor that we would grant AI standing in the non-human manner
sometimes proposed for rivers, forests, or future generations. Through the Synthetic Summit
Simulator, the synthetic chamber transfers the use of AI agents as a mediating layer within
deliberation, not as a subject of political decision-making. At stake is not only who belongs
to a constituted assembly, but how propositions become politically admissible within it and
how previously excluded claimants can find their way in, as Latour puts it, as “a scandal at
the heart of an assembly” (Latour 2004: 54). In that limited sense, our proposal draws on the
non-human entity as a theory of articulation and admission, even if the synthetic chamber
does not adopt Latour’s constitutional schema literally or follow his distance from
parliamentary bicameralism.
Three design constraints followed from this experiment. First, the value of the AI model did
not lie in consensus production, but in the preservation of irreducible differences. Some of
the most productive deliberations occurred when delegates refused reconciliation and instead
exposed incompatible premises in sharp relief. Second, prompting mattered not as hidden
steering but as a catalytic technique for drawing out latent conflict and preventing the debate
from settling too quickly into apparent coherence. Third, traceability proved indispensable:
where the provenance of claims is obscure, the deliberative scene tends to collapse back into
smooth assertions. These observations do not validate the chamber empirically, but they
identify design constraints around plurality, dissent, and orchestration.
6.4 Architecture of the Synthetic Chamber
The synthetic chamber stabilises a passage from deliberative formation, through synthetic
projection, to political uptake. These are not three institutions, but three connected
operations within one process. The chamber receives a governed corpus of organised

-- 279 of 336 --

279
deliberation. Citizens’ assemblies, mini-publics, deliberative polls, and related formats
generate the material on which it operates (Farrell et al. 2019; Fishkin 2009; Fishkin 2018).
These settings do more than register preferences by creating spaces where citizens weigh
competing considerations, revise positions, and articulate reasons with justifications (Fishkin
1991, Grönlund et al 2014). The deliberative corpus is primary, but it need not be exclusive.
Chamber dossiers could admit supplementary materials such as parliamentary records, draft
bills, petition data, and civil-society submissions, provided that these are tagged as
supplementary inputs and cannot overwrite the chamber’s dependence on deliberative
outputs. The chamber is meant to project deliberative reasons into representative politics,
not to pretend that deliberative formats alone can exhaust the relevant knowledge around
every legislative question. The corpus is to be expandable without becoming extractive.
The synthetic chamber’s quality of report does not derive from model sophistication, but
from the granularity and governance of the deliberative process on which it operates. For
that reason, it should rely on a rolling programme of mini-publics and citizens’ assemblies
rather than a high-profile national convention. A rolling design creates temporal continuity,
allowing the chamber to learn across repeated forums rather than treat each event in
isolation. It reduces the pressure to extrapolate any single mini-public as fully representative
of a people in unison.
Figure 1: Constitutional Workflow of the Synthetic Chamber
The chamber becomes constitutional not at the point of synthesis alone, but across the full passage from input
to reply, and renewed challenge. Source: author’s elaboration, construed with MatPlotLib
Not all deliberative forums should enter the chamber on identical epistemic terms.

-- 280 of 336 --

280
Differences in sampling, facilitation, briefing materials, duration, expert access, voting rules,
and decision format are part of the evidence chain. For this reason, the deliberations should
travel with a design card specifying recruitment method, participant attrition, moderation
model, information sources, and the form in which outputs were produced. The chamber
should process deliberative corpora with these metadata, since what counts as a reason
depends partly on the design conditions under which it forms. In machine-learning terms,
this is a constitutional adaptation of datasheets and model cards (Gebru et al. 2021; Mitchell
et al. 2019) where documentation accompanies the corpus all the way through the pipeline.
At a minimum, the metadata should include transcripts, registers of arguments and
counterarguments, identified trade-offs, minority positions, and pre- and post-deliberation
survey data indicating how judgements shifted (Anthropic 2023).
Activating the synthetic chamber requires an office of stewardship. Here, the figure of a clerk
is central. The clerk is an AI-mediated stewardship function, not a political persona. It carries
out the handling of the deliberative corpus, while human stewards supervise provenance,
documentation, prompting, and publication. Stewards are responsible for the anonymisation,
provenance control, documentation standards, corpus curation, prompting, and the
presentation of outputs (Ding et al. 2024). At the interface level, the chamber may need
interactive personae through which citizens, journalists, and legislators can navigate its
reports, but it should not collapse into a single spokesperson. Better would be a cohort of
navigational roles: one attached to main lines, one to minority branches, one to uncertainty,
and another to provenance. Work on persona design in conversational systems is relevant
here, as human-computer interaction treats persona as a way of organising expectations,
tone, and modes of traversal across system outputs, as the “soul” of an AI model (Sun et al.
2024). The point would not be to anthropomorphise the institution, but to distribute its
labour across readable masks while keeping all of them tethered to one archive (Staunæs
2026). Persona, in other words, should serve traversal rather than sovereignty.
6.4.1 From Deliberative Corpus to Synthetic Mediation
The chamber’s synthetic contribution lies in how it organises an agentic layer of mediation
once the deliberative corpus has been governed and documented. We do not posit that
multi-agentic AI systems are intrinsically more democratic, but they allow the corpus to be
processed through differentiated mandates rather than compressed immediately into a single
line (Anthropic 2025). An orchestrator decomposes a policy issue into a set of
representational tracks and assigns those to specialised worker agents on the same base.

-- 281 of 336 --

281
A plausible initial ensemble would include agents oriented to public finance and
redistribution, ecological thresholds and environmental externalities, legal-constitutional
compatibility, procedural fairness, implementation capacity, labour and welfare effects,
territorial distribution, minority and marginalised positions, and long-term or
intergenerational consequences. Not every mandate is activated in each report, and none of
them should be treated as a metaphysically fixed spokesperson. These are provisional
representational devices designed to make systematically underweighted dimensions of a
parliamentary question visible within synthetic projection.
Figure 2: Issue Space, Cohort, and Public Interface
Agentic mediation distributes bounded readings across corpus, issue-space, and interface rather than collapsing
into a single voice. Source: author’s elaboration, construed with MatPlotLib
The workers are not interchangeable processors but mandate-bearing analytical positions.
One track may assess fiscal and distributive consequences, another ecological effects and
planetary thresholds, another legal-constitutional compatibility or procedural fairness,
another the situated claims of affected minorities or long-term burdens. Each worker should
be specified not as a miniature sovereign with a view and opinion, but as a constrained reader
of the deliberative corpus, required to return premises, evidentiary basis, uncertainty flags,
and outstanding trade-offs under its mandate. If an ecological branch judges a proposal
incompatible with stated climate thresholds while a distributive branch defends it, the
synthesis preserves antagonism as argued conflict in the report.

-- 282 of 336 --

282
The chamber should resist a further technical category mistake: that a model’s latent
structure is a democratic discovery engine. Latent spaces do not disclose a hidden public will.
They register patterned proximities within a technically delimited field of possible
articulation, structured in advance by training, selection, and compression rather than opened
from nowhere (Offert 2021). Machine learning systems do not simply execute given rules,
but generate inferential regularities from data, which means that what enters the chamber
through such modelling is not averages, but patterns of abstraction (Parisi 2019). Claims can
often cluster closely in vector space while remaining normatively opposed, just as politically
aligned claims can appear distant at the level of phrasing. For constitutional purposes, latent
structure is useful as an index of textual patterning, not as a surrogate constituency.
Synthesis, in our design, does not mean totalisation or simple summarisation. In the modest
philosophical sense relevant, the synthesis of the synthetic operation is a putting-together of
reasons and perspectives, forming a compositional unity of incomplete representations (Fazi
2024). Where aggregation maps the distribution of reasons across the corpus, synthesis
composes from that mapped composition a unified object that can be deliberated upon
without pretending that disagreement disappeared. At minimum, the chamber’s reported
synthesis should obey four invariants. Minority positions above a threshold of legibility are
preserved rather than averaged. Clusters of disagreement are exposed rather than smoothed
into compromise. Every claim in the final report would carry traceable provenance that can
be linked to the deliberative artefacts from which it derives. Also, uncertainty is to be marked
explicitly where no stable resolution emerges.
The summit suggests an additional requirement here. Because the Citizen Cyborg prompts
often prevented premature closure and brought suppressed differences back into view, the
chamber should not treat prompting as a hidden manipulation but as a structured,
documented adversarial function. In practice, this could take the form of a challenge round
built into the orchestration process, in which the Clerk or designated reviewers test whether
the synthesis has marginalised a relevant perspective, flattened a conflict too quickly, or
overlooked a latent trade-off. Such a function would make the chamber more accountable to
plurality, understood not merely as the retention of minority positions but as the coexistence
of heterogeneous normative registers that resist easy translation. It would make explicit that
orchestration is never neutral, and that one of the chamber’s internal tasks is to keep
coherence from arriving too easily.

-- 283 of 336 --

283
Figure 3: Report Manifold of the Synthetic Chamber
To remain answerable, the chamber’s report must function less as a summary than as a manifold in which
agreement, disagreement, uncertainty, and traceability remain separately readable. Source: author’s
elaboration, construed with MatPlotLib
The chamber’s output should be understood less as a single report than as a manifold. One
layer would provide a readable synthesis for parliamentary circulation and general publics,
setting out areas of convergence, lines of disagreement, and unresolved tensions. A second
would preserve minority branches and dissenting trajectories rather than subsuming them
into the main line. A third functions as an uncertainty register, identifying low-confidence
inferences, evidentiary gaps, and normatively incommensurable claims. A fourth provides a
reconstructible provenance ledger, whether as appendix, audit layer, or linked evidentiary file,
showing which agents contributed which claims under which mandates and on what basis.
This multi-layered output makes it possible for citizens and legislators alike to recognise the
quality of synthesis without having to trust an opaque summary of a process.
6.4.2 Political Uptake, Constitutional Role, and Democratic
Accountability
Political uptake is where the chamber is constitutional rather than consultative. Once
formally tabled, a chamber report would trigger a duty of response from the relevant
parliamentary committee, ministry, or legislature. The response need not agree. Indeed,
disagreement is part of the point. But the representative institution must state publicly where
the chamber’s account is accepted, where it is modified, and where it is rejected, together

-- 284 of 336 --

284
with the reasons for doing so. Legislatures are not bound to follow the chamber’s
conclusions, but they are obliged to address them. This is the point at which synthetic
mediation acquires democratic force. Generated reasons are not merely collected and
summarised but returned to representation in a form that makes them difficult to ignore.
The synthetic chamber is an advisory-deliberative body whose constitutional significance lies
not in decision-making power, but in its capacity to generate a formal duty of public
response. Its closest analogues are the ombudsman, the independent commission, and
certain parliamentary committees: institutions that derive political weight less from binding
authority than from the obligation they create for others to justify their decisions in public.
The chamber should be understood as part of the architecture of opinion-formation rather
than will-formation. It would not legislate, veto, or formally review legislation as a second
chamber would, but instead organise, trace, and publicly project reasons that elected bodies
would be required to address.
This also distinguishes it from proposals for sortition-based chambers. Proposals for
randomly selected second chambers seek to strengthen political equality, impartiality, and
legislative review within a revised bicameral architecture (Abizadeh 2021). The synthetic
chamber occupies a different institutional niche. A sortition chamber is a body of judgement
within decision-making: it deliberates, decides, or reviews through the presence of randomly
selected citizens. The synthetic chamber, by contrast, is a body of mediated opinion-
formation and reason-structuring. It receives outputs from deliberative forums, renders
visible lines of agreement and disagreement, and triggers duties of response, but exercises no
decision power of its own. The two models are better understood as complementary than
rival: a sortition chamber could use the synthetic chamber’s outputs, or the synthetic
chamber could operate upstream of a sortition-based body.
Its accountability mechanism follows from that narrower role. Accountability thus runs not
through day-to-day partisan control of the chamber itself, but through a public obligation
imposed on elected institutions to answer the reasons the chamber places on the record.
That arrangement avoids both technocratic substitution and purely symbolic consultation.
That same logic should govern appointment and independence. The data stewards or
equivalent officers responsible for the chamber’s integrity should not be appointed by the
lower chamber or by the state executive. A plausible design would vest appointment in a
more politically distant authority, such as the monarch, the president, or the upper chamber

-- 285 of 336 --

285
(where such authorities exist), to reduce dependence on the politicians whose conduct the
chamber would indirectly scrutinise. The data stewards serve for fixed terms, are removable
on transparent grounds, and remain independent from the content of deliberative outputs.
A plausible implementation could first be established by statute or parliamentary standing
order and attached to a limited number of committees, for example in climate, digital affairs,
or social policy. Only if the procedure proved institutionally useful would a durable
constitutional role be justified.
No such design eliminates failure. A chamber of this kind would be vulnerable to capture,
drift, opacity, adversarial prompting, and poisoned inputs. The question is whether those
risks remain buried inside proprietary systems, or whether they are relocated into a public
design that can itself be challenged. For that reason, contestation should continue after
publication. Legislators, civil-society actors, journalists, and citizens should be able to submit
formal objections identifying provenance gaps, suppressed minority branches, questionable
mandates, or violations of the chamber’s own compositional rules. Those objections should
be logged, published, and incorporated into subsequent cycles. Feedback should not be
piped straight back into model weights. Objections, legislative responses, and scrutiny should
enter through update gates, whether prompt revision, weighting changes, corpus exclusion,
or periodic retraining procedures, otherwise the chamber becomes vulnerable to brigading,
coordinated capture, and reactive drift. A constitutional chamber should learn under rules
rather than through open-ended absorption.
The same holds for external audit. If the chamber is to function as a public counter-
institution to opaque mediation, it should be audited not only for performance in a narrow
technical sense, but also for corpus construction, mandate design, synthesis procedure, and
reporting standards. In an information environment where malicious AI swarms may inject
fabricated artefacts or political actors may game the deliberative inputs, the most durable
defence is unlikely to be semantic truth arbitration alone. It will be infrastructural integrity of
provenance tracking, transparency, and mechanisms for challenge robust enough to expose
how outputs are produced and by whom they are contested (Schroeder et al. 2026). This is
the chamber’s ambivalent advantage. It automates parts of mediation’s handling while
making clearer that mediation is already political.
6.5 Discussion: Competing Resolutions of the Mediation Problem
The synthetic chamber is one among several attempts to resolve the same problem of

-- 286 of 336 --

286
mediation. One familiar solution is algorithmic optimisation, where AI appears as a higher-
order processor of social preference, able to infer what the public wants more efficiently than
elections, political parties, or deliberative forums can manage (García-Marzá and Calvo
2024). In that image, behavioural traces are treated as if they were already political reasons,
and the distinction between processing inputs and judging them begins to collapse (Rouvroy
2013; Stiegler 2015). A related move would assimilate the chamber to hyper-personalised
representation built around digital twins or civic proxies (Gudiño et al. 2024). Such
approaches promise inclusion through individual mirroring, but their likely political effect is
privatisation. Once representation is dispersed into personalised interfaces, contestation
becomes harder to sustain and trails of reason fragment into loops of responsiveness.
Pluralist and collaborative projects such as Plurality offer a more demanding comparison.
Like the synthetic chamber, they seek to render disagreement intelligible at scale, preserve
dissent, and widen the field of politically hearable inputs (Weyl and Tang 2024). With Team
Mirai entering parliament in Japan in 2025, this broader ecology includes an initiative that
attaches AI-mediated articulation to representative institutions through tools, proposals, and
legislation. The difference is one of architecture. Broad listening remains primarily a
reception layer: it gathers, clusters, and displays positions (Sanders and Sanders 2025). The
synthetic chamber inserts an additional scene of mediation, routing those positions through
differentiated agents that bring claims into relation as conflict, trade-off, and reply. Its
specificity lies in staging contestation at the inter-model level rather than only expanding the
uptake of positions. In this sense, broad listening expands intake, whereas the chamber
formalises a scene of contradictions.
A different response to the same problem is plebiscitary acceleration. As Altman (2026)
suggests, AI can make direct democracy cheaper, faster, and more frequent while
simultaneously weakening the civic conditions that make it desirable. The temptation is old:
direct democracy appears as the antithesis of representational distortion, yet repeatedly
founders on the practical impossibility of assembling a people in one place. AI can seem to
solve this by turning data into a latent population and the model into its standing proxy. The
synthetic chamber does not treat latent structure as a one-to-one image of “the people,” but
as one mediating layer among others, useful for mapping patterned proximities and conflicts,
yet always requiring challenge, segmentation by mandate, and institutional reply. Its role is
not to count everyone, but to route deliberative outputs into a setting where reasons can be
structured, disagreement preserved, and representative institutions exposed to justification.

-- 287 of 336 --

287
There is, however, a danger internal to the proposal itself. A device designed to preserve
plurality may also domesticate it. Once disagreement is formalised into branches, institutions
may find it easier to acknowledge contestation without changing course. As Malabou remarks
in her discussion of Audrey Tang and the Plurality movement, the strategy of “joining
institutions to subvert them” can appear both as an emancipatory fork and as a
reinforcement of existing domination (Malabou 2023: 221). The same critique that enters
institutions to change them can also settle into a more reflexive administration of
disagreement. The duty of response raises the cost of evasion, but it does not abolish it. Nor
does traceability solve the problem that the synthetic chamber may privilege articulate and
governable reasons over affective forms such as anger, refusal, or disruption that do not
easily enter official mini-publics. Its success would not be measured by legislative adoption
alone, but by whether it preserves dissent, renders trade-offs, and compels differentiated
institutional replies across cycles.
6.6 Conclusion
This article has proposed the synthetic chamber not as democracy’s cure-all, but as a
constitutional response to a crisis of mediation. Where parties no longer reliably absorb,
organise, and project public reasons into representative politics, and where algorithmic
systems increasingly process political claims through opaque and proprietary infrastructures,
the chamber offers an answerable mediating mechanism: a standing body of opinion-
formation that converts organised deliberative inputs into multi-voiced, traceable outputs
and compels official response.
The contribution of the article is threefold. Substantively, it reframes the democratic
prospect of AI as one of mediation rather than administration. Institutionally, it specifies a
chamber-form that sits between deliberative participation and elected decision-making
without collapsing into either technocratic optimisation or a second legislature.
Methodologically, it shows how a research-creation case can be used to extract design
constraints from a live mediating arrangement and translate them into a formal proposal.
The proposal remains exploratory, and its technical, constitutional, and political difficulties
are real. But that exploratory status also indicates its next step. Rather than imagining one
universal implementation, synthetic chambers should be piloted comparatively across
different regional and constitutional settings, whether within local assemblies, parliamentary
states, federal systems, or transnational umbrellas such as the EU, AU, ASEAN, Mercosur,
GCC, or EAEU. Implementations need not be engineer-dominated, but could be convened

-- 288 of 336 --

288
across parliamentary offices, civic organisations, democratic-innovation NGOs,
interdisciplinary research centres, and freelance political technologists as a way of testing how
agentic mediation accommodates across institutions, languages, and cultures.
The chamber is aimed to remedy the democratic vacancy of mediation that made
privatisation politically effective in the first place. If algorithmic systems are already involved
in how political claims are compressed, structured, and relayed, then the democratic choice is
not between more or less AI in democracy, but between opaque and answerable mediation.
The synthetic chamber names a constitutional route for making that choice.
